8. Service Deep Dives

Canonical Design Intent

Every module below is described not just by what it does today, but by what it should own and never own — the structure that lets the module be reviewed, replaced, or scaled without rewriting the rest of SmartLoad. Each module has a Design Contract block stating its purpose, owned and forbidden responsibilities, inputs, outputs, contract surface, fallback behavior, scalability and observability expectations, plus current state, target state, and gap to close.

How to read each module page. The existing sections (Purpose, Inputs/Outputs, Internal logic, Implementation readiness, Open questions) describe the module as it exists or is planned. The added Design Contract block describes what the module should be — the canonical target. Where current and target diverge, the divergence is recorded as a Gap to Close.

8.1 Load Balancer (NGINX) Canonical Wired

Purpose

The single entry point for all client HTTP traffic. Translates routing intelligence into concrete request-forwarding actions while preserving deterministic safety fallbacks.

Responsibilities

  • Accept HTTP/1.1 requests on port 80 (internal); exposed as 8080 on the host via docker-compose port mapping.
  • Apply current effective routing config (upstream weights and exclusions).
  • Run periodic health checks against backends and exclude unresponsive ones.
  • Write a structured JSON access log per request (timestamp, selected backend, latency, status) to stdout and to a shared volume — the volume is read by the lb-otel-shipper sidecar, which produces the actual OTLP emission off the request path.

Inputs

  • Client HTTP requests.
  • Routing config (statically generated initially; dynamically updated by sidecar in Phase 2).

Outputs

  • Forwarded requests to selected backend.
  • Per-request JSON access log to stdout (for docker compose logs) and to a shared volume /nginx-logs/access.log (for the shipper). Fields: timestamp, service, client_ip, request, status, backend, latency, upstream_latency.
  • Indirect (via lb-otel-shipper): canonical flat metric names — request_count, request_latency_ms, error_rate — emitted as one OTLP/HTTP-JSON gauge data point per request to the OTel Collector.

Dependencies

  • Shared volume nginx-logs mounted by NGINX (rw) and by the shipper (ro).
  • lb-otel-shipper sidecar — reads the access log and emits OTLP off the request path. Failure of the shipper does not affect request serving (NGINX keeps writing logs; metrics simply don't reach the DB until the shipper recovers).
  • Sidecar (Python or Go) for live config updates from Redis (planned T2.1) — separate from the shipper; this is the routing controller.

Internal logic

Phase 1: classical NGINX upstream block with all healthy backends in round-robin. Health checks remove failures within 2 cycles.

Phase 2 (T2.1): sidecar process subscribes to:

  • smartload.anomaly → maintain set of UNHEALTHY backend IDs and remove from upstream.
  • smartload.routing → if mode=active, set upstream weight=score proportional to RL scores.
  • smartload.policy → if safe_mode=true, reset all weights to equal and stop applying RL.
After applying changes, the sidecar regenerates nginx.conf and runs nginx -s reload (graceful reload).

Routing decision hierarchy (canonical)

  1. Anomaly constraints first: drop UNHEALTHY backends from the upstream list.
  2. Active policy: if safe_mode=true, switch to equal weights and stop reading RL.
  3. RL recommendation: when allowed and available, set weights from RoutingRecommendation.server_rankings.
  4. Classical fallback: round-robin / least-connections is always available if RL is missing, warming up, or stale.

State handled

  • NGINX upstream config (regenerated by sidecar).
  • Stateless w.r.t. individual requests — no session affinity.

Fallback behavior

If Redis is unreachable or the sidecar dies: NGINX continues serving traffic with the last successfully applied config. If anomaly signals are stale, NGINX still has health checks as a second-line defense.

Constraints

P99 routing-latency overhead < 5 ms at ≤ 1000 req/s with ≥ 1 healthy backend.

Implementation readiness

AspectStatus
NGINX deployed in docker-composeDONE (Phase 0)
OTel instrumentation (T1.2) — log-based via lb-otel-shipper sidecarDONE (S2)
Sidecar reads Redis (T2.1, critical path node 4)DONE (T2.1)

Open questions / blockers

  • Sidecar language: Python — confirmed and implemented. Docker SDK + Flask, same pattern as autoscaler.
  • NGINX reload mechanism: nginx -s reload via docker exec — confirmed and implemented. Config rewrite uses the include-file strategy (only upstream.conf changes).
Design Contract — Load Balancer
Role in the middleware
The single ingress and the only data-plane component. Translates pre-computed routing intelligence into per-request forwarding decisions.
Why it exists
SmartLoad is middleware: there must be exactly one front door. The LB is also the natural enforcement point for the routing hierarchy because it is the only process that sees every request.
Owned responsibilities
  • Accept HTTP/1.1 on port 80 (container) — exposed as 8080 on the host.
  • Apply currently loaded upstream config (weights + exclusions).
  • Run passive (NGINX) and active health checks.
  • Write per-request JSON access log line to stdout + shared volume. The shipper sidecar (off the request path) converts each line to OTLP fire-and-forget.
Non-responsibilities
  • Must NOT call Redis or TimescaleDB on the request path.
  • Must NOT compute anomaly, forecast, or RL scores in process.
  • Must NOT carry per-client state (no session affinity).
  • Must NOT decide policy; it consumes the effective config produced by the Sidecar.
Inputs
Client HTTP requests; current effective nginx.conf (rewritten by the Sidecar); active-check probes against backends.
Outputs
Forwarded requests; HTTP responses; structured JSON access log (to stdout + shared volume nginx-logs). The shipper sidecar derives canonical flat metrics from the log: request_count, request_latency_ms, error_rate (one OTLP gauge data point per request, per metric).
Internal logic expectations
Stateless w.r.t. requests; carries only the loaded upstream block. No Lua scripting on the hot path. No embedded ML.
Data dependencies
None at runtime beyond the local config file. The access log write is buffered fire-and-forget — collector outage, shipper outage, or shared-volume back-pressure must not block requests. NGINX itself does not connect to the OTel Collector; that link is the shipper's responsibility.
Required interfaces
Inbound HTTP (container port 80, host port 8080); local file nginx.conf (written by Sidecar); local Unix socket for nginx -s reload; shared volume nginx-logs mounted at /var/log/nginx for the shipper to tail.
Failure / fallback behavior
If Sidecar is dead or Redis unreachable: continue serving with last-known-good config. If active check fails for a backend: NGINX max_fails+fail_timeout excludes it independently of anomaly signals.
Scalability expectations
P99 routing overhead < 5 ms at ≤ 1000 rps with ≥ 1 healthy backend. Horizontally scalable behind a TCP balancer (out of prototype scope but not blocked).
Observability expectations
Per-request structured access log with selected backend, latency, status. The shipper emits request_latency_ms as a gauge per request (preserving the per-request distribution — anomaly detection's STDDEV/MAX queries depend on this fidelity). /health endpoint reports NGINX worker liveness + Sidecar last-success time.
Security / safety expectations
No TLS termination in prototype (assumed trusted internal network). The Sidecar must validate config before reload — a malformed write must not bring NGINX down.
Current state
NGINX wired in services/load-balancer/nginx/; round-robin upstream over test-backend; JSON access log written to both /dev/stdout (for docker compose logs) and /nginx-logs/access.log on the shared nginx-logs volume. The lb-otel-shipper sidecar is live (T1.2) and emits per-request OTLP. No routing Sidecar (T2.1) yet.
Target state
NGINX (vanilla) + two sidecars: (1) lb-otel-shipper (wired in T1.2) for telemetry emission; (2) routing Sidecar (T2.1) that enforces hierarchy anomaly exclusion → policy short-circuit → RL weights → classical default and reloads NGINX via nginx -s reload.
Gap to close
  • T1.2: Land lb-otel-shipper sidecar. Done — T1.2
  • T2.1: Build the routing Sidecar — subscribe to anomaly/routing/policy, regenerate nginx.conf, signal reload. Critical path S4
  • Routing Sidecar language (Python default) Needs confirmation. Shipper language confirmed: Python.
Review checklist
  • Does any code on the LB process call Redis or TimescaleDB? (Must be no.)
  • Is the routing Sidecar the only writer of nginx.conf?
  • Is the Sidecar's config render reviewed for injection risks (escaping backend IDs, etc.)?
  • Does NGINX continue serving if either sidecar (shipper or routing) dies?
  • Are reload events bounded (no thrashing under rapid signal changes)?
  • Is per-request telemetry produced fire-and-forget (NGINX never blocks on the shipper or the collector)?
  • Does the shipper emit canonical flat metric names (request_count, request_latency_ms, error_rate) — the names hardcoded into queries.py ANOMALY_QUERY?

8.1.1 LB OTel Shipper Canonical Wired T1.2 wired

Purpose

A small Python sidecar to NGINX whose only job is converting NGINX's per-request JSON access log into OTLP/HTTP-JSON metric data points and POSTing them to the OTel Collector. Decouples NGINX (the data plane) from telemetry plumbing — NGINX writes a log line and never connects to the collector itself.

Why it exists (vs. an in-NGINX OTel module)

SmartLoad's AI engines query AVG(value), MAX(value), STDDEV(value) over rolling windows from the metrics table. To produce meaningful STDDEV/MAX the table must contain per-request samples. The candidate alternative — F5's nginx-otel module + collector spanmetrics connector — aggregates spans into windowed means before they ever leave the collector, flattening the per-request distribution. The shipper preserves per-request fidelity by emitting one OTLP gauge data point per HTTP request.

Responsibilities

  • Tail /nginx-logs/access.log (seek from end on start; do not re-emit historical lines).
  • Parse each JSON log line; tolerate malformed lines (log + skip, never crash).
  • For each parsed line, emit three OTLP gauge data points with shared resource attributes:
    • request_count = 1
    • request_latency_ms = latency field × 1000 (NGINX $request_time is seconds)
    • error_rate = 1 if status ≥ 500 else 0 (engines aggregate via AVG)
  • Set resource attributes service.name=load-balancer and instance=<hostname>.
  • POST OTLP/HTTP-JSON to http://otel-collector:4318/v1/metrics with a strict timeout. On any error (timeout, conn refused, 5xx), log + drop. Never raise into the tailing loop.

Inputs

  • Read-only mount of the nginx-logs shared volume at /nginx-logs/. (Not /var/log/nginx/ — the nginx:alpine base image symlinks access.log there to /dev/stdout, which is unseekable.)

Outputs

  • OTLP/HTTP-JSON metric batches to the OTel Collector (POST /v1/metrics).
  • Process logs to stdout (own observability — line counts, dropped-batch counts).

Dependencies

  • NGINX writing the access log to the shared volume.
  • OTel Collector reachable at otel-collector:4318. Down → metrics dropped silently for that interval; the request path is unaffected.

Internal logic

Single-process, single-thread tail loop with a small thread pool for outbound HTTP. The OTLP payload schema matches Telemetry Service (§8.3)'s accepted shape exactly: resourceMetrics[].scopeMetrics[].metrics[].gauge.dataPoints[] with asInt/asDouble values and nanosecond timeUnixNano. No histograms (telemetry drops histograms — rows_dropped_shape).

State handled

  • Last-read file position (in-process; restart loses position by design — start_at: end).
  • No persistence to disk or DB.

Fallback behavior

Three independent failure modes, all isolated from the request path:

  • Shipper down → NGINX keeps serving + writing logs; metrics simply don't reach the DB until the shipper restarts. Log file accumulates on the volume; bounded by NGINX's own log rotation.
  • Collector down → shipper logs the error, drops the batch, continues tailing. No retries (fire-and-forget).
  • Telemetry Service down (downstream of collector) → collector's otlphttp/telemetry exporter handles its own retry/backoff; shipper unaffected.

Constraints

  • Memory footprint < 50 MB.
  • Sustained throughput ≥ 1000 req/s without falling behind the log file (verified by integration test).
  • Zero impact on NGINX request latency under shipper failure.

Implementation readiness

AspectStatus
Approach chosen (B — log-based shipper, not nginx-otel module)DECIDED
Shipper code (services/lb-otel-shipper/)DONE
Shared volume + compose wiring (nginx-logs)DONE
Integration test asserting STDDEV > 0 on real LB trafficDONE
Design Contract — LB OTel Shipper
Role in the middleware
Telemetry source for the load balancer. Sits in the control plane (off the request path) and converts NGINX access logs into the canonical OTLP form expected by the rest of the telemetry pipeline.
Why it exists
Per-request fidelity for AI engine queries (STDDEV/MAX over rolling windows). The OTel collector ecosystem lacks a clean log-record→per-record-numeric-metric path, and the F5 nginx-otel module aggregates into windowed histograms upstream of the database. A small purpose-built shipper avoids both compromises.
Owned responsibilities
  • Tail /nginx-logs/access.log from end-of-file at startup.
  • Parse JSON, emit per-request OTLP gauge data points for the canonical flat metric names.
  • POST OTLP/HTTP-JSON to the OTel Collector; drop on error.
Non-responsibilities
  • Must NOT write to TimescaleDB directly (only the Telemetry Service may write metrics — single-writer rule).
  • Must NOT make the collector or telemetry service mandatory for NGINX availability.
  • Must NOT retry indefinitely on outbound failure (drops preserve fire-and-forget).
  • Must NOT alter or rotate the access log file (read-only mount).
Inputs
Read-only access log file on shared volume nginx-logs.
Outputs
OTLP/HTTP-JSON metric batches to http://otel-collector:4318/v1/metrics using the canonical flat metric names (request_count, request_latency_ms, error_rate) with resource attributes service.name=load-balancer and instance=<hostname>.
Internal logic expectations
Single-process tail loop. No state persisted across restarts. No threading on the parse path; small thread pool only for outbound POSTs with strict timeout. No histograms (avoid telemetry's rows_dropped_shape path).
Data dependencies
NGINX writing JSON access logs to the shared volume. Down/full → shipper has nothing to read; recovers when NGINX resumes writing.
Required interfaces
Read-only mount of nginx-logs at /nginx-logs/; outbound HTTP to otel-collector:4318.
Failure / fallback behavior
All failures (collector down, network blip, malformed log line) result in dropped metrics — never request-path impact, never crash.
Scalability expectations
≥ 1000 req/s sustained with < 50 MB RSS. If NGINX is scaled horizontally, one shipper per NGINX replica (each with its own instance attribute).
Observability expectations
Stdout logs of lines processed and batches dropped. No /health endpoint at T1.2 — process-restart on Docker handles liveness; observed indirectly via row arrival in metrics.
Security / safety expectations
Log file is read-only mount. Outbound HTTP only — no inbound listeners. No secrets in transit (the OTLP path is internal-network only in prototype).
Current state
T1.2 wired. services/lb-otel-shipper/app.py tails /nginx-logs/access.log, batches per-request gauges every ~1 s, fire-and-forget POST to http://otel-collector:4318/v1/metrics. Live in docker-compose.yml as the lb-otel-shipper service on the shared nginx-logs volume (LB writes rw at /nginx-logs, shipper reads ro). 13 unit + integration tests cover parser shape, envelope schema, malformed-line handling, and end-to-end per-request fidelity on the live stack — all green in CI.
Target state
Same — current state matches the canonical contract. Future hardening: per-LB-replica horizontal scale-out (one shipper per NGINX), inotify-based tail to drop the 50 ms poll, optional OTLP/protobuf to halve the bytes-on-wire for high-throughput deployments.
Gap to close
  • Shipper code, compose wiring, integration test. Done — T1.2
  • Inotify tail loop (drop the 50 ms poll). Deferred — current poll is below the noise floor at prototype rps.
  • OTLP/protobuf output. Deferred — JSON keeps the receiver Flask-only.
Review checklist
  • Does the shipper emit only the canonical flat metric names (request_count, request_latency_ms, error_rate)?
  • Are gauges used (no histograms — telemetry drops histograms)?
  • Is the resource attribute service.name=load-balancer set so engine WHERE service='load-balancer' filters match?
  • On collector outage, does the shipper drop and continue rather than retry/block?
  • Does shipper crash leave NGINX serving 100% of requests with normal latency?
  • Does the integration test assert per-request fidelity (STDDEV > 0, not just row count)?

8.2 Classical Routing Engine Canonical

Purpose

SmartLoad's deterministic safety net. Always-available routing logic that runs whenever AI signals are unavailable, stale, or disabled. Also serves as the comparison baseline for benchmarks.

Algorithms

  • Round-robin — default; fair cyclical distribution across all healthy backends.
  • Least-connections — routes to the backend currently handling fewest active requests.
  • Weighted round-robin — optional; respects backend weight in upstream config.

Why it matters

Reflects a core SmartLoad philosophy: intelligent components augment proven strategies, never replace them outright. Once shipped, the engine needs no maintenance.

Where it lives

Embedded inside the NGINX upstream configuration — no standalone microservice. The classical strategy is the result of how upstream weights are set when no RL signal applies.

Implementation readiness

DONE Round-robin works in the Phase 0 stack against the test-backend pool.

Design Contract — Classical Routing
Role in the middleware
The deterministic floor of the routing hierarchy. Always available, dependency-free, well-understood. Also the comparison baseline for benchmarks.
Why it exists
Principle 4 (graceful degradation) requires a dependency-free routing path. Classical also lets the project measure AI lift quantitatively in benchmarks.
Owned responsibilities
Round-robin / least-connections / weighted-RR semantics — implemented natively by NGINX upstream config, not as a separate service.
Non-responsibilities
Must NOT be moved into a Python service. Must NOT depend on Redis or TimescaleDB. Must remain trivially deployable with no AI services running.
Required interfaces
NGINX upstream block. Configurable via weight=N per server line.
Failure / fallback behavior
It is the fallback. If everything else fails, classical still serves traffic correctly.
Observability expectations
Every request log records the selected backend. Aggregate latency / RPS visible in Grafana. No special instrumentation required.
Current state
Round-robin against the test-backend pool. Wired and verified in Phase 0.
Target state
No structural change. Once the Sidecar exists, it composes classical with anomaly + RL by setting weight on each upstream entry.
Gap to close
None structurally. Verified in B1 baseline benchmark.
Review checklist
  • Does the LB still serve requests when all AI services are down?
  • Is classical the documented benchmark baseline (B1)?
  • Is the path through classical observable (request log + per-backend counters)?
  • Does the design avoid promoting classical into a separate service (it should remain embedded)?

8.3 Telemetry Service Canonical Wired T1.1 wired

Purpose

The sensory system of SmartLoad. Receives OTLP metrics, writes them to TimescaleDB, exposes them as queryable JSON for AI services and operators.

Inputs / Outputs

  • In: OTLP metrics (port 4318 HTTP / 4317 gRPC) from OTel Collector.
  • Out: rows in metrics(time, service, instance, metric_name, value); HTTP GET /api/v1/metrics?service=&window=.

Internal logic

The OTel Collector forwards each metric batch to POST /v1/metrics via its otlphttp/telemetry exporter (encoding: json). The service walks the OTLP envelope, extracts gauge and sum data points into canonical (time, service, instance, metric_name, value) tuples, and persists them with psycopg2.extras.execute_batch(METRICS_INSERT, rows, page_size=500). Histogram / summary / exponentialHistogram are out of the SmartLoad metric set and are counted as rows_dropped_shape rather than persisted.

Backpressure containment: the ingress endpoint always returns 200 to the collector. DB-write failures bump rows_dropped_db and log; never silent and never propagated to the data plane.

Read API: GET /api/v1/metrics?service=&window= accepts Prometheus-style window strings (30s / 5m / 1h / 2d) and queries metrics with %s::interval, %s, %s bind params (SOT §11). Returns {service, window, rows: [...]}; row count capped at READ_API_ROW_LIMIT (default 10 000).

Observability: GET /api/v1/stats returns rows_written / batches_written / rows_dropped_db / rows_dropped_shape counters guarded by a threading.Lock.

Schema (canonical)

CREATE TABLE metrics (
  time         TIMESTAMPTZ      NOT NULL,
  service      TEXT             NOT NULL,
  instance     TEXT             NOT NULL,
  metric_name  TEXT             NOT NULL,
  value        DOUBLE PRECISION NOT NULL
);
SELECT create_hypertable('metrics', 'time', if_not_exists => TRUE);

A richer schema (SDP/db/V1__smartload_schema.sql) exists as a future redesign; see §6.4 Metrics DB.

Acceptance criteria (T1.1) Met

  • OTLP/HTTP-JSON received via the Collector pipeline. The collector continues to terminate 4317/4318 and forwards to telemetry:8081/v1/metrics in JSON encoding.
  • Rows visible in metrics — verified by tests/integration/test_telemetry_ingest.py asserting post-insert content via direct psycopg2 SELECT against the live stack.
  • curl http://localhost:8081/api/v1/metrics?service=load-balancer&window=5m returns JSON — covered by test_read_api_returns_recent_rows plus a 5-case parametric over invalid window strings.

Open questions / blockers

  • Needs confirmation — Whether the simple metrics table will be migrated to the richer V1 schema (telemetry_metrics with normalized FK relationships) in Phase 2.
Design Contract — Telemetry Service
Role in the middleware
The sensory boundary. Translates OTLP fan-in into rows in TimescaleDB and exposes a JSON read API for operators and tests.
Why it exists
Without a single ingestion service, every other component would invent its own metric write path. Centralizing here enforces single-writer ownership of telemetry (principle 8).
Owned responsibilities
  • Be the only writer of metrics.
  • Receive OTLP (HTTP 4318 / gRPC 4317) from the OTel Collector.
  • Persist rows via METRICS_INSERT (canonical SQL).
  • Expose GET /api/v1/metrics?service=&window= for operators / dashboards.
  • Expose /health that actually verifies Redis + TimescaleDB connectivity.
Non-responsibilities
Must NOT compute analytics or derived signals (anomaly, forecast). Must NOT publish on decision channels. Must NOT cache writes in process memory in a way that risks data loss on restart.
Inputs
OTLP metric streams from NGINX, backends, and other services.
Outputs
Rows in metrics(time, service, instance, metric_name, value); JSON over HTTP for the read API.
Internal logic expectations
Receive → validate envelope → batch insert → ACK. Batched writes preferred for throughput; synchronous writes acceptable for the prototype.
Data dependencies
TimescaleDB (write); Redis (only for liveness / future policy subscription).
Required interfaces
OTLP HTTP 4318, gRPC 4317; HTTP /api/v1/metrics, /health; SQL via queries.py (METRICS_INSERT).
Failure / fallback behavior
If TimescaleDB is unavailable, telemetry must not be lost silently — log + metric on dropped writes. Backpressure must not propagate to the data plane (the LB telemetry path — NGINX → log file → lb-otel-shipper → collector → telemetry — is fire-and-forget at every hop by design).
Scalability expectations
Linear scaling with replica count behind a TCP balancer; writes are inherently parallelizable across instances.
Observability expectations
Per-cycle metric: rows written, rows dropped, mean batch size. /health tests both Redis ping and TimescaleDB SELECT 1.
Security / safety expectations
Trusted-network assumption; no auth on prototype. Future: mTLS or shared secret on OTLP ingestion.
Current state
T1.1 wired (PR #108). POST /v1/metrics ingests OTLP/HTTP-JSON forwarded by the collector and batch-inserts via canonical METRICS_INSERT. GET /api/v1/metrics serves the read API. GET /api/v1/stats exposes write / drop counters. 25 unit + integration tests cover parser shape, read-API validation, ingress acceptance, and DB persistence — all green in CI on the live stack.
Target state
Same — current state matches the canonical contract. Future hardening: gRPC + protobuf OTLP on the receiver itself (today the collector terminates them and forwards JSON).
Gap to close
  • Implement OTLP receiver (T1.1). Done — PR #108
  • Wire METRICS_INSERT. Done — PR #108
  • Implement read API. Done — PR #108
  • Decide V1 schema migration Needs confirmation.
  • Direct OTLP/protobuf + gRPC support on the receiver — deferred; the collector continues to terminate gRPC on 4317.
Review checklist
  • Is Telemetry Service the only writer of metrics?
  • Does /health truly test dependencies (not just return 200)?
  • Are writes idempotent under retry?
  • Does the read API expose only canonical fields (no internal columns)?
  • Does write backpressure stay contained — never reaches the data plane?
  • Are dropped writes observable (metric + log)?

8.4 Metrics Database (TimescaleDB) Canonical Wired

Purpose

Long-term memory of the system: stores all telemetry, anomaly events, scaling events, and backend health. Underpins forecasting model training, anomaly model evaluation, RL replay, and Grafana dashboards.

Canonical schema (Phase 0 wired)

-- infrastructure/timescaledb/init.sql
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS timescaledb;

CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS metrics (
  time         TIMESTAMPTZ      NOT NULL,
  service      TEXT             NOT NULL,
  instance     TEXT             NOT NULL,
  metric_name  TEXT             NOT NULL,
  value        DOUBLE PRECISION NOT NULL
);
SELECT create_hypertable('metrics', 'time', if_not_exists => TRUE);
CREATE INDEX idx_metrics_service_instance ON metrics (service, instance, time DESC);
CREATE INDEX idx_metrics_metric_name      ON metrics (metric_name, time DESC);

CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS backend_health (
  time        TIMESTAMPTZ      NOT NULL,
  backend_id  TEXT             NOT NULL,
  status      TEXT             NOT NULL,   -- healthy|degraded|unhealthy
  score       DOUBLE PRECISION NOT NULL    -- 0.0=normal, 1.0=very anomalous
);
SELECT create_hypertable('backend_health', 'time', if_not_exists => TRUE);

CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS scaling_events (
  time           TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL,
  action         TEXT        NOT NULL,   -- scale_out|scale_in
  instance_count INT         NOT NULL,
  reason         TEXT
);
SELECT create_hypertable('scaling_events', 'time', if_not_exists => TRUE);

-- policy_changes: append-only audit of POST /api/v1/policy changes (T1.4).
-- One row per changed field. old_value / new_value are JSON-encoded so the
-- column can hold any field type (string, number, bool, list).
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS policy_changes (
    time            TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL,
    policy_version  INT         NOT NULL,   -- post-change version (monotonic)
    field           TEXT        NOT NULL,   -- e.g. "max_backends"
    old_value       TEXT,                   -- JSON-encoded previous value; NULL if newly set
    new_value       TEXT        NOT NULL,
    actor           TEXT        NOT NULL    -- X-Actor header, "anonymous" if absent
);
SELECT create_hypertable('policy_changes', 'time', if_not_exists => TRUE);

Schema diagram (current state)

Future redesign — V1__smartload_schema.sql Draft, pending review

A richer 6-table schema authored on 2026-05-04 exists in SDP/db/V1__smartload_schema.sql with matching draft contracts in SDP/db/SmartLoad_Feature_Contracts_v1.docx (Mar 27) and SmartLoad_Service_Contracts_v1.docx (Mar 28). It includes:

  • Regular tables: backend_nodes (UUID PK), policies (named, versioned).
  • Hypertables: telemetry_metrics (latency_ms, request_count, error_rate, cpu_usage, memory_usage, source ENUM), routing_decisions (engine_used, routing_score, request_id), anomaly_events (anomaly_type ENUM, severity ENUM, resolved flag), scaling_events (action ENUM, status ENUM).
  • 8 ENUM types, retention policies (7d / 30d / 90d), continuous aggregates (telemetry_hourly, telemetry_1min), and 7 days of synthetic seed data.

Status (per user 2026-05-04): "use the old sources that are wired; the new ones will be reviewed first, implemented, and integrated in the next iteration." This V1 design is therefore draft / not adopted. The simple 3-table schema remains canonical until V1 is reviewed and migrated.

Canonical SQL access via services/shared/queries.py

-- ANOMALY_QUERY (anomaly-detector reads every 5 s) — parameterised since PR #105
SELECT instance, metric_name,
       AVG(value)    AS avg_value,
       MAX(value)    AS max_value,
       STDDEV(value) AS std_value,
       COUNT(*)      AS sample_count
FROM metrics
WHERE time > NOW() - %s::interval
  AND service = %s
  AND metric_name = ANY(%s)
GROUP BY instance, metric_name
ORDER BY instance, metric_name;

-- Caller passes (window, service, metric_names) as bind params:
cur.execute(ANOMALY_QUERY,
            ("60 seconds", ANOMALY_DEFAULT_SERVICE, ANOMALY_METRIC_NAMES))

Other constants in the same file: FORECAST_QUERY, RL_STATE_QUERY, BACKEND_HEALTH_QUERY, METRICS_INSERT, BACKEND_HEALTH_INSERT, SCALING_EVENT_INSERT. Defaults: ANOMALY_DEFAULT_SERVICE = "load-balancer", ANOMALY_METRIC_NAMES = ["request_latency_ms", "error_rate"].

Implementation readiness

DONE Phase 0: extension installed; 3 hypertables created via init.sql; verified by TestTimescaleDBSchema (5 tests in the 33-test integration suite).

Design Contract — Metrics Database
Role in the middleware
The system's long-term memory. The shared ground truth that decision engines query and that operators visualize.
Why it exists
Time-series storage with SQL expressiveness — gives engines a familiar query surface and gives Grafana dashboards direct access without a translation layer.
Owned data classes
metrics (raw telemetry), backend_health (derived health), scaling_events. Each has exactly one writer.
Non-responsibilities
Must NOT hold transactional state. Must NOT be the bus for ephemeral signals (use Redis for that). Must NOT be queried from the data plane.
Required interfaces
PostgreSQL wire protocol on port 5432; SQL constants in services/shared/queries.py are the only canonical entry points for SmartLoad services.
Failure / fallback behavior
If the DB goes down: writes back-pressure (fail loudly, never silently); reads from engines fail their cycle and engines must skip rather than crash.
Scalability expectations
Hypertables provide native time-based partitioning. Retention is enforced per data class: 7 d for raw metrics (high-volume, derived signals live in CAGGs / backend_health), 30 d for backend_health (longer-tail anomaly history), 90 d for scaling_events (audit trail), 90 d for policy_changes (audit trail).
Observability expectations
Standard Postgres exporter to Prometheus. Grafana dashboards on hypertable size, write rate, query latency.
Security / safety expectations
Trusted-network assumption; no per-service auth in prototype. Production: per-service DB roles with least-privilege.
Current state
3-table schema with retention (7d / 30d / 90d) and the metrics_1min continuous aggregate. 33 Phase-0 wiring tests + 39 S2 baseline tests (incl. 6 DB-bound) passing in CI as of PR #105 + #106.
Target state
3-table canonical + the existing retention & CAGG. V1 6-table schema is the long-term target after Phase 2 review.
Gap to close
  • Add retention policies (add_retention_policy). Done — PR #105
  • Add continuous aggregates for dashboards. metrics_1min shipped — PR #105 A second-tier hourly CAGG remains nice-to-have for long-range Grafana panels.
  • Decide V1 schema migration path Needs confirmation.
  • Production hardening: per-service Postgres roles.
Review checklist
  • Does each table have exactly one writer service?
  • Are all SQL queries from SmartLoad services in queries.py?
  • Are indexes informed by canonical query patterns?
  • Is unbounded growth prevented by retention policies?
  • Are schema changes versioned alongside init.sql + queries.py + tests?

8.5 Anomaly Detection Engine Canonical Phase-1 wired (flagged off) #138 + N1.1

Purpose

Self-protection layer. Detects unhealthy or degraded backends in real time and signals the LB to remove them from the upstream pool.

Two-tier delivery

TierSprintApproachOwner
Phase 1A (skeleton)S2 — N1.1Threshold: every 5 s, query last 60 s of latency + error rate; if latency > multiplier × rolling_mean OR error_rate > threshold → DEGRADED/UNHEALTHY.Nada
Phase 2 (real model)S4 — N2.1Isolation Forest (scikit-learn) trained on NAB + Yahoo SMD. Features: latency, error_rate, latency_rolling_mean, latency_rolling_std. Saved to services/anomaly-detector/models/isolation_forest.pkl. Falls back to threshold if model file missing. Implemented on SMD only (not NAB+SMD): NAB's univariate format doesn't map onto the 4-feature BackendFeatures schema, so tools/anomaly-training/train_smd.py trains and validates against SMD's real test_label/ alone; the SMD dim→feature mapping, machine selection, and rationale are documented in engines/isolation_forest/README.md.Nada

Inputs / Outputs

  • In (engine): SQL ANOMALY_QUERY against TimescaleDB; smartload.policy (for current anomaly_latency_multiplier, safe_mode, anomaly_response).
  • In (HTTP): POST /api/v1/isolate — operator-driven synthetic event (slice #3, #123). Body {backend_id, status: healthy|degraded|unhealthy, actor, reason}. Bypasses the engine + run loop; publishes synthetic envelope + writes backend_health row; reason carries the manual:<actor>: prefix.
  • Out: AnomalyEvent{ backend_id, status, score, timestamp, model_version?, features? } to smartload.anomaly every POLL_INTERVAL_SECONDS; rows in backend_health via BACKEND_HEALTH_INSERT.

Acceptance criteria (N1.1)

  • redis-cli SUBSCRIBE smartload.anomaly receives JSON every ~5 s.
  • Triggering RESPONSE_DELAY_MS=2000 on a backend produces DEGRADED then UNHEALTHY events.

KPIs

  • Detection latency: ≤ 2 monitoring intervals (~10 s).
  • Phase 2 model: F1 > 0.80 on test split.

Open questions

  • If Isolation Forest F1 < 0.80, fall back to LSTM autoencoder or tune contamination parameter (Risk Register).
Design Contract — Anomaly Detection Engine
Role in the middleware
SmartLoad's self-protection layer. The first level of the routing hierarchy. Decides which backends are off-limits.
Why it exists
RL cannot be trusted with the full backend pool. Without an anomaly authority, RL would be one bad inference away from routing 100% of traffic to a sick instance.
Owned responsibilities
  • Run a 5 s detection cycle.
  • Be the only writer of backend_health.
  • Publish AnomalyEvent on smartload.anomaly.
  • Maintain a configurable threshold + an Isolation Forest model (Phase 2).
Non-responsibilities
Must NOT decide weights (that's RL + Sidecar). Must NOT write nginx.conf. Must NOT proxy traffic. Must NOT derive its own routing decisions.
Inputs
SQL ANOMALY_QUERY parameterized with (window, service, metric_names) — the service filter is a parameter, not hardcoded, so backend-emitted telemetry can also flow through once available; smartload.policy for anomaly_latency_multiplier, anomaly_response, and anomaly_recovery_window_seconds.
Outputs
Wrapped envelope on smartload.anomaly with payload {backend_id, status, score, model_version}; rows in backend_health via BACKEND_HEALTH_INSERT.
Internal logic expectations
Stateless across cycles for the threshold tier. For the model tier: load isolation_forest.pkl once at startup; if missing, fall back to threshold rule and log degraded mode.
Data dependencies
TimescaleDB read; Redis publish; Redis subscribe (policy).
Required interfaces
SQL via queries.py; Redis via contracts.py (AnomalyEvent); /health.
Failure / fallback behavior
Model file missing → fall back to threshold rule (skeleton mode). DB unreachable → skip cycle, log, do not publish (subscribers will treat absence as "use last known"). Multi-cycle outage → policy / operator can switch safe_mode=true.
Scalability expectations
Single replica is sufficient for prototype scale. Replication strategy if needed: sticky-by-backend partitioning (each replica owns a slice of backends).
Observability expectations
Per-cycle metrics: cycle duration, count of DEGRADED, count of UNHEALTHY, model F1 (offline measurement). /health verifies DB + Redis.
Security / safety expectations
Detection latency target ≤ 2 monitoring intervals (~10 s). KPI: F1 > 0.80 on test split.
Current state
Run loop wired in app.py + runloop.py via the engine_base ABC and select_engine() factory (#138). Subscribes to smartload.policy and reconstructs the engine on each update so policy-derived params (e.g. anomaly_latency_multiplier) take effect within one cycle. Threshold baseline ships and is wired to ANOMALY_QUERY; on every POLL_INTERVAL_SECONDS, the loop pivots rows into BackendFeatures, scores each backend, and publishes an AnomalyEvent envelope. Falls back to threshold automatically if the requested engine raises on load. Enabled by default since v1.0.7g (ANOMALY_RUNLOOP_ENABLED=true); revert with =false in .env for debugging. Unit tests live at tests/unit/anomaly-detector/test_runloop.py.
Target state
Isolation Forest plugin shipped (#101 closed v1.0.7ab) at engines/isolation_forest/engine.py with trained .pkl at services/anomaly-detector/models/, F1=0.8012 on SMD holdout. Remaining: production-scale calibration so the model is actually usable as the default engine (currently under-reacting per the bench — #165), auto-recovery cool-down before clearing UNHEALTHY status, and own-metrics scraped by Prometheus (anomaly_detector_inferences_total, _inference_errors_total, _inference_latency_seconds, _engine_loaded).
Gap to close
  • Isolation Forest engine.py + trained .pkl drop-in (#101 — model handoff from Nada). Shipped v1.0.7ab. Follow-up: production_scaler re-calibration to lift the 25% bench agreement above the 80% threshold needed to flip the compose default (#165).
  • Auto-recovery logic with cool-down before clearing UNHEALTHY.
  • Own-metrics emission (counters + latency histogram) + /metrics Prometheus endpoint.
  • Flip ANOMALY_RUNLOOP_ENABLED default to true after dev-stack smoke.
  • F1 measurement harness for the test split (Phase 2 evaluation gate).
Review checklist
  • Is Anomaly Detector the only writer of backend_health?
  • Does the engine read only via canonical SQL constants?
  • Does the runtime image exclude training datasets?
  • Is there a fallback for missing model file?
  • Does the engine subscribe to smartload.policy for threshold updates?
  • Is detection latency observable?

8.6 Forecasting Engine Canonical Phase-1 wired (flagged off) #138 + N1.2

Purpose

Anticipates near-future load so the autoscaler can act before demand peaks — moving SmartLoad from reactive to proactive scaling.

Two-tier delivery

TierSprintApproach
Phase 1A (skeleton)S2 — N1.2Every 60 s: query last 1 h of request_rate; compute rolling mean → next-5-min forecast.
Phase 2 (real model)S4 — N2.2ARIMA or Prophet trained on Borg traces (load_borg_timeseries()). Hold-out evaluation reports MAE / MAPE. Falls back to moving average if model file missing.

Outputs

ForecastResult{ horizon_minutes, predicted_rps, confidence_lower, confidence_upper, timestamp } on smartload.forecast every ~60 s.

KPI

MAPE < 20% on 5-minute horizon over held-out data (target N2.2).

Limitations

Forecasting cannot capture flash crowds. Autoscaler always retains a reactive fallback path based on current telemetry.

Design Contract — Forecasting Engine
Role in the middleware
Predictive scaling driver. Moves SmartLoad from reactive to proactive capacity decisions.
Why it exists
Reactive autoscaling is too late under burst load — by the time current capacity is exceeded, latency has already spiked. Forecast lets capacity arrive ahead of demand.
Owned responsibilities
  • Run a 60 s forecast cycle.
  • Publish ForecastResult on smartload.forecast.
  • Own one model artifact (arima.pkl or prophet.pkl).
Non-responsibilities
Must NOT influence per-request routing (forecast errors must not corrupt routing). Must NOT trigger scaling itself (Autoscaler is the actuator).
Inputs
FORECAST_QUERY (1 h window of request_rate); smartload.policy for horizon settings.
Outputs
ForecastResult{ horizon_minutes, predicted_rps, confidence_lower, confidence_upper, timestamp }.
Internal logic expectations
Load model once at startup. Compute one inference per cycle. If model missing → moving-average fallback. If query fails → publish nothing this cycle.
Data dependencies
TimescaleDB read; Redis publish.
Failure / fallback behavior
Model load failure → moving average. Multi-cycle outage → Autoscaler falls back to reactive scaling on current telemetry.
Scalability expectations
Single replica. Inference is constant-time after model load.
Observability expectations
Per-cycle: predicted vs actual (lag join 5 min later). MAPE rolling. Cycle duration. /health.
Security / safety expectations
No client-controlled inputs. KPI: MAPE < 20% on 5 min horizon.
Current state
Run loop wired in app.py + runloop.py via the engine_base ABC and select_engine() factory (#138 round 2). Subscribes to smartload.policy and reconstructs the engine on each update (the safe_mode field gates publishing). Moving-average baseline ships and is wired to FORECAST_QUERY; on every POLL_INTERVAL_SECONDS, the loop converts rows into a HistoryWindow, runs engine.forecast(), and publishes a ForecastResult envelope. Falls back to moving-average automatically if the requested engine raises on load. Enabled by default since v1.0.7g (FORECAST_RUNLOOP_ENABLED=true); revert with =false in .env for debugging. ARIMA engine shipped v1.0.7i: engines/arima/engine.py + models/arima_model.pkl (ARIMA(3,0,1), 36.9 MB, test MAPE 25.0% on the Alibaba trace — +22.77% over the moving-average baseline). SOT KPI of <20% MAPE not yet met, so moving_average remains the default; operators activate ARIMA via FORECAST_ENGINE=arima in .env. Verified live: engine.loaded=arima, ready=true, predicted=30.84 rps, CI=[16.11, 45.57] (statsmodels conf_int(alpha=0.05)). FORECAST_QUERY.request_rate is normalised to per-second by dividing the 1-minute bucket sum by 60 — keeps the column's units aligned with the per-second predicted_rps the autoscaler consumes for capacity math, and is pinned by tests/integration/test_s2_baseline.py::TestCanonicalSQLParameterization::test_forecast_query_normalises_to_per_second_rate. Unit tests live at tests/unit/forecasting/test_runloop.py.
Target state
ARIMA plugin (#102) implemented at engines/arima/engine.py with trained .pkl at services/forecasting/models/. 5 min horizon for fast scale-out, 1 h horizon for capacity planning. Held-out evaluation reports MAE / MAPE < 20%. Own-metrics scraped by Prometheus.
Gap to close
  • ARIMA engine.py + trained .pkl drop-in (#102 — revised model handoff from Nada per PR #144 review).
  • Wire confidence intervals through to Autoscaler heuristic.
  • Forecast-vs-actual dashboard panel in Grafana.
  • Own-metrics emission (counters + latency histogram) + /metrics Prometheus endpoint.
  • Flip FORECAST_RUNLOOP_ENABLED default to true after dev-stack smoke.
Review checklist
  • Does any consumer use the forecast for per-request decisions? (Must be no.)
  • Is moving-average fallback present for missing model?
  • Is MAPE measured on held-out data, not training data?
  • Is the 1 h capacity-planning horizon plumbed to the autoscaler distinctly from the 5 min scale-out horizon?

8.7 Reinforcement Learning Engine Canonical Phase-1 wired (flagged off) #138 + N1.3

Amendment (v1.0.7 — 2026-05-28): The trained policy is now framed as a contextual bandit (state → chosen backend) optimised with MaskablePPO, not an MDP-trained RL agent — the offline simulator replays trace windows independently of the agent's action. Canonical operating_mode set is now {shadow, hybrid} (learning retained as a backwards-compat alias for hybrid). PPOPolicy.reload(**kwargs) is now a real in-place update hook (no torch reload on policy publishes). Serving uses argmax-dominant weighting (chosen backend gets 0.7, remainder split evenly). New HEALTH_UNKNOWN state for silent backends (excluded from routing). See §22 v1.0.7 row for the full delta and the RL review changelog at G:/smartload-changelogs/ for the engineering rationale.

Purpose

The headline AI component. Learns adaptive routing policies that outperform static algorithms by optimizing long-term latency and load distribution.

Phase staging

PhaseTaskBehavior
1A (S2)N1.3 shadow scaffoldRandom policy; publishes RoutingRecommendation{ mode="shadow" }. Validates the full pipeline before any model exists.
2 (S4)N2.3 — Gym envSmartLoadEnv in services/rl-engine/training/env.py (training-only; excluded from runtime Docker image): state=[latency, queue_depth, health_flag] per backend; action=discrete backend selection; reward = −P95_latency − λ · load_imbalance (reward uses next_state latency for correct credit assignment); episode = workload trace. obs_builder.py at the service root is the shared train/serve conversion layer.
2 (S4)N2.4 — PPO training CRITICAL PATHStable-Baselines3 PPO; ~7 days; reward curves until convergence; saved to services/rl-engine/models/policy.zip. Canary check (50k steps) gates the full budget. Acceptance: PPO beats round_robin AND least_connections on p95 latency and SLO violation rate.
2 (S4)N2.5 — active modeLoads policy.zip; operating_mode ∈ {shadow, hybrid} (legacy learning alias maps to hybrid) — shadow runs full inference non-actuating; hybrid emits mode=active. T2.1 sidecar consumes scores. v1.0.7 reframe: serving uses argmax-dominant weighting (chosen backend 0.7, remainder split evenly) instead of softmax-of-logits.

Inputs / Outputs

  • In: SQL RL_STATE_QUERY from TimescaleDB; AnomalyEvent health flags; policy for mode/exploration.
  • Out: RoutingRecommendation{ mode, server_rankings:[{backend_id, score}], timestamp } on smartload.routing every ~5 s.

Safety controls

  • Default deployment uses fixed pretrained policy (no online learning).
  • Operator safe_mode=true immediately disables RL routing.
  • Anomaly-flagged backends are excluded from RL action space.
  • RL output is consumed only when mode=active; otherwise it is logged but not enforced.

Risk register entry

If PPO training diverges or exceeds the 7-day budget, fall back to DQN; pre-validate on a toy env in N2.3.

Design Contract — Reinforcement Learning Engine
Role in the middleware
Adaptive routing optimizer. The headline AI component, but architecturally subordinate to anomaly + policy.
Why it exists
Static algorithms cannot anticipate uneven backend capacity, drift, or workload skew. RL learns a policy that adjusts weights in real time.
Owned responsibilities
  • Run a 5 s inference cycle.
  • Publish RoutingRecommendation on smartload.routing.
  • Operate in shadow mode by default; switch to active only when policy permits.
  • Own its model artifact (policy.zip) and Gym env (SmartLoadEnv in services/rl-engine/training/env.py — training-only, excluded from runtime image).
Non-responsibilities
Must NOT see UNHEALTHY backends in its action space (anomaly excludes them upstream). Must NOT proxy traffic itself. Must NOT bypass safe_mode. Must NOT do online learning in the prototype.
Inputs
RL_STATE_QUERY (latency, queue_depth, CPU per backend); smartload.anomaly for active health flags; smartload.policy for mode + exploration rate.
Outputs
RoutingRecommendation{ mode, server_rankings:[{backend_id, score}], timestamp }.
Internal logic expectations
Load policy.zip at startup. Per cycle: gather state → exclude anomalous backends → forward pass → publish. State and action space exclude anomalous backends; policy never gets the chance to recommend them.
Data dependencies
TimescaleDB read; Redis publish + subscribe (anomaly, policy).
Failure / fallback behavior
Model missing → publish empty rankings with mode=shadow (Sidecar falls back to classical). DB query fails → skip cycle. safe_mode=true → publish nothing or mode=shadow; the Sidecar will ignore RL anyway.
Scalability expectations
Single replica is sufficient. Inference is bounded by model size, not by request rate.
Observability expectations
Per-cycle: chosen action (mode, top-N rankings), reward proxy (next-cycle latency vs RL prediction), exploration rate. Reward curve in Grafana during training/eval.
Security / safety expectations
Action space pre-filtered through anomaly. safe_mode kill-switch. No online learning in production prototype. Trained model is read-only at runtime.
Current state
Run loop wired in app.py + runloop.py via the policy_base ABC and select_policy() factory (#138 round 3 — closes the engine-wrapper cutover). Subscribes to smartload.policy and rebuilds the policy on each update so policy-derived params (rl_exploration_rate, rl_confidence_threshold) take effect within one cycle (kwargs are ignored gracefully by baselines that don't accept them). Queries RL_STATE_QUERY on POLL_INTERVAL_SECONDS, builds list[BackendState] via runloop.build_state_from_rows with locally-classified health (matching the threshold engine's thresholds), calls policy.act(), and publishes a RoutingRecommendation envelope. The published mode field is composed by effective_mode(): safe_mode=true forces shadow; RL_MODE != "active" forces shadow; only when both RL_MODE=active and the policy itself returns active is the LB allowed to apply weights. Falls back to random_shadow automatically if the requested policy fails to load (raising policy_ready=false on /health). Enabled by default since v1.0.7g (RL_RUNLOOP_ENABLED=true); revert with =false in .env for debugging. RL_MODE=shadow (default) remains the routing-safety pin even with the run loop on. Unit tests live at tests/unit/rl-engine/test_runloop.py (26 tests covering the mode-composition truth table, health classification, state pivot, and policy bootstrap fallback).
Target state
PPO trained offline (~7 days) on SmartLoadEnv (#27, #29). The trained policy.zip drops into services/rl-engine/models/, policies/ppo/policy.py implements the RoutingPolicy contract, and operators flip RL_POLICY=ppo + RL_MODE=active. T2.1 sidecar consumes the weighted recommendations.
Gap to close
  • N2.3: SmartLoadEnv Gym wrapper.
  • N2.4: PPO offline training run + reward-curve evidence (#27, ~7-day budget).
  • PPO policy.py + trained policy.zip drop-in (#27 model handoff).
  • N2.5: flip RL_MODE=active + verify LB sidecar consumes the weights (T2.1).
  • Anomaly-exclusion of UNHEALTHY backends from the RL action space — runloop currently builds state for every backend; PPO should filter on state.health before ranking.
  • Own-metrics emission (counters + latency histogram) + /metrics Prometheus endpoint.
  • Flip RL_RUNLOOP_ENABLED default to true after dev-stack smoke.
  • DQN fallback trigger if PPO diverges (risk-register entry).
Review checklist
  • Does the RL action space exclude UNHEALTHY backends from anomaly?
  • Does safe_mode immediately silence RL influence on routing?
  • Is the runtime container free of training data and training code?
  • Is online learning disabled in the runtime?
  • Is the reward function documented and reproducible (state → action → reward calculation)?
  • Are shadow-mode recommendations logged for offline comparison vs classical?

8.8 Autoscaler / Resource Manager Canonical Implemented T1.3 + slices #2, #3

Purpose

The actuation arm of the control loop. Adjusts the number of running test-backend containers based on forecast-driven and reactive signals. Also exposes the scaling audit stream and the operator-override scale endpoint.

Inputs / Outputs

  • In (engine): smartload.forecast messages; smartload.policy (MIN/MAX_BACKENDS, cooldown).
  • In (HTTP):
    • GET /api/v1/audit/scaling?limit=N — recent scaling_events rows, newest first; ?limit capped at 1000. Slice #2 (#122).
    • POST /api/v1/scale — operator-driven manual scale (slice #3, #123). Body {target_count, actor, reason}. Validates against live policy bounds; bypasses cooldown; writes one scaling_events row + publishes one ScalingEvent envelope with forecast_event_id=null; bumps the cooldown clock.
  • Out: Docker SDK calls (docker.from_env().containers.run/stop); scaling_events rows in TimescaleDB; ScalingEvent envelopes on smartload.scale.

Logic (T1.3)

if predicted_rps > current_backends × PER_INSTANCE_CAPACITY_RPS:
    scale_out(1)                                          # respect MAX_BACKENDS
elif predicted_rps < (current_backends − 1) × PER_INSTANCE_CAPACITY_RPS:
    scale_in(1)                                           # respect MIN_BACKENDS
respect autoscaler_cooldown_seconds (default 60)

Lower-bound: shed one backend only when doing so still leaves capacity above demand. Avoids a hysteresis tuning parameter — the cooldown timer is the sole anti-oscillation lever.

Constraints (canonical from config/policy.yaml)

  • min_backends: 1 · max_backends: 5
  • per_instance_capacity_rps: 100
  • autoscaler_cooldown_seconds: 60

KPI

New capacity available within 2 minutes of demand increase (Benchmark B2).

Design Contract — Autoscaler / Resource Manager
Role in the middleware
The actuation arm of the control loop. The only process that creates or destroys backend instances.
Why it exists
Forecasts are useless without a hand to pull the lever. Autoscaler bridges prediction to capacity.
Owned responsibilities
  • Subscribe to smartload.forecast.
  • Call Docker SDK to start / stop test-backend containers.
  • Be the only writer of scaling_events.
  • Publish smartload.scale on action (audit channel).
  • Respect min_backends, max_backends, cooldown from policy.
Non-responsibilities
Must NOT route requests. Must NOT predict load (forecast comes from Forecasting Engine). Must NOT make policy decisions (only enforce them).
Inputs
smartload.forecast; smartload.policy; current backend count from Docker SDK.
Outputs
Docker container start/stop calls; rows in scaling_events; messages on smartload.scale.
Internal logic expectations
Compare predicted RPS to current_backends × per_instance_capacity_rps. Step size of 1. Cooldown enforced between actions. Reactive fallback if forecast is stale.
Data dependencies
Docker daemon (read + write); Redis subscribe + publish; TimescaleDB write.
Failure / fallback behavior
Forecast stale (last message > 2 cycles old) → fall back to reactive scaling on current telemetry. Docker daemon unreachable → log + alert; do not retry-storm.
Scalability expectations
Single replica must hold the lock on scaling decisions to avoid double-scale. Lock can be Redis-based for the prototype.
Observability expectations
Per-action: forecast value, decision (scale_out / scale_in / no-op), reason, current backend count. Grafana dashboard panel.
Security / safety expectations
Min/max bounds always enforced. Cooldown prevents oscillation. Step size = 1 prevents thrashing.
Current state
T1.3 + T1.4 live-reload wired. Subscribes to smartload.forecast AND smartload.policy on a single pubsub; on each forecast envelope, compares predicted_rps to current_count × per_instance_capacity_rps and applies a step-1 scale via the Docker SDK (toggles compose-managed smartload-test-backend-1..5 between running/stopped). Honors min_backends, max_backends, autoscaler_cooldown_seconds loaded at startup from config/policy.yaml; on every smartload.policy envelope, swaps _policy under _state_lock via decisions.policy_from_payload and rejects backwards policy_version moves. Writes one row per action to scaling_events via canonical SCALING_EVENT_INSERT. Publishes ScalingEvent envelopes on smartload.scale with forecast_event_id provenance. Reactive fallback fires when the last forecast is older than 2 × horizon — scales on observed RPS pulled via OBSERVED_RPS_QUERY. Cluster surface is split behind a ClusterClient ABC so a KubernetesClusterClient can drop in later without touching decisions.py or app.py.
Target state
Subscribes to smartload.forecast + smartload.policy; calls docker.from_env().containers.run/stop via the DockerClusterClient implementation; LB picks up the live backend set via T2.1 Sidecar regeneration of nginx.conf.
Gap to close
  • T1.3: forecast subscription + Docker SDK actuation + cooldown. Shipped.
  • Reactive fallback path on stale forecast. Shipped (OBSERVED_RPS_QUERY + 2 × horizon threshold).
  • Cluster-aware abstraction (Docker SDK for prototype; K8s API as future target). Shipped (ClusterClient ABC + DockerClusterClient).
  • Live policy reloads on smartload.policy (T1.4). Shipped (_handle_policy_message + policy_from_payload).
  • T2.1 LB sidecar so scale-out beyond compose's initial set is routable (currently bounded by NGINX's static 1..5 upstream block).
Review checklist
  • Is Autoscaler the only writer of scaling_events?
  • Are min/max bounds enforced on every action?
  • Is cooldown enforced regardless of how loud the forecast signal is?
  • Is reactive fallback wired for stale forecast?
  • Does the Docker abstraction allow swapping to K8s API without rewriting business logic?
  • Are scaling decisions auditable from scaling_events?

8.9 Policy & Configuration Manager Canonical Wired stub T1.4 next

Purpose

Centralized governance: defines the boundaries within which automated decisions may operate. Read by every other service.

Endpoints

  • GET /api/v1/policy → returns YAML as JSON.
  • POST /api/v1/policy → updates fields, persists YAML, publishes new policy on smartload.policy.

Canonical fields (config/policy.yaml)

The canonical policy is grouped by concern (modes, scaling, anomaly thresholds, RL controls) rather than alphabetically. Operators edit this file directly when out-of-band; in-band changes go through POST /api/v1/policy.

# --- modes ---
operating_mode: hybrid                # classical | hybrid | learning
safe_mode: false                      # global kill-switch — disables RL immediately

# --- scaling bounds ---
min_backends: 1
max_backends: 5
per_instance_capacity_rps: 100        # used to compare forecast vs capacity
autoscaler_cooldown_seconds: 60       # min seconds between scale actions

# --- SLO ---
slo_p95_latency_ms: 200               # target P95; informs anomaly + alert thresholds

# --- anomaly thresholds ---
anomaly_latency_multiplier: 3.0       # latency > multiplier × rolling_mean ⇒ DEGRADED
anomaly_response: auto-isolate        # auto-isolate | advisory
anomaly_recovery_window_seconds: 30   # consecutive healthy seconds before clearing UNHEALTHY

# --- RL controls ---
rl_exploration_rate: 0.0              # 0 = pure exploitation; only non-zero in 'learning' mode
rl_confidence_threshold: 0.6          # below this, sidecar ignores RL and uses classical

Done — PR #105 config/policy.yaml, the PolicyUpdate dataclass, and the canonical SOT §11 payload shape are now in sync. The four previously-missing fields (anomaly_response, anomaly_recovery_window_seconds, rl_exploration_rate, rl_confidence_threshold) are present in all three places, and PolicyUpdate.policy_version (required) plus changed_fields (optional) match SOT §11. T1.4 only needs to wire the REST surface and the publisher loop.

Acceptance (T1.4)

  • curl -X POST localhost:8086/api/v1/policy -d '{"safe_mode": true}' → 200, publishes update.
  • All subscribers receive on smartload.policy within ~1 s.
Design Contract — Policy & Configuration Manager
Role in the middleware
Central governance. The operator's voice in the system. The single owner of policy state.
Why it exists
Without one authoritative policy source, every service would invent its own config surface. Centralizing here makes safe_mode meaningful as a global kill switch.
Owned responsibilities
  • Be the only writer of config/policy.yaml.
  • Serve GET /api/v1/policy and POST /api/v1/policy.
  • Be the only publisher on smartload.policy.
  • Validate policy changes (e.g., min_backends < max_backends, slo_p95_latency_ms > 0).
Non-responsibilities
Must NOT enforce policy itself (engines and Sidecar enforce). Must NOT subscribe to its own publish channel for state. Must NOT silently rewrite policy without an audit row.
Inputs
REST POST from operator; YAML on disk at startup.
Outputs
Updated YAML; Redis publish on every change; audit row (future: policy_changes table).
Internal logic expectations
Validate → persist YAML → publish → return 200 with new state. Idempotent: repeated POST of identical state is a no-op (no spurious publishes).
Data dependencies
Filesystem (read+write YAML); Redis publish.
Failure / fallback behavior
Validation failure → 400 with field-level error. Disk write failure → 500, no publish (consistency). Subscribers must pull on startup via REST GET to avoid relying on a missed publish.
Scalability expectations
Single replica is sufficient. Read-heavy is fine; writes are operator-initiated and rare.
Observability expectations
Audit log per change (operator, timestamp, field diff). Metric for policy version.
Security / safety expectations
Future: authn on POST. Now: trusted-network. Validation must reject incoherent states (e.g., min_backends > max_backends).
Current state
T1.4 wired. GET /api/v1/policy returns the on-disk snapshot. POST /api/v1/policy validates the payload (strict canonical-field set + per-field rules + cross-field min_backends ≤ max_backends via validation.py — unknown keys rejected with 400 + the offending field name; server-managed envelope echoes policy_version/timestamp/changed_fields stripped silently, v1.0.7p / #152), merges with existing, computes the diff, atomically writes the YAML (with bind-mount fallback), writes one row per changed field to policy_changes via canonical POLICY_CHANGE_INSERT, bumps policy_version, and publishes a canonical PolicyUpdate envelope on smartload.policy. Idempotent: a POST that matches existing state returns 200 no-op with no audit row and no publish. Startup validates the on-disk policy and logs (not crashes) on failure. Autoscaler-side live reload is wired (see §8.8). Tests: 21 unit (tests/unit/policy-manager/test_validation.py) + 20 integration (tests/integration/test_policy_validation.py).
Target state
Real REST endpoints, validation, atomic YAML write, Redis publish, idempotent semantics, audit row per change.
Gap to close
  • Validation rules on POST + service startup (min_backends < max_backends, non-negative numerics, enum values for operating_mode / safe_mode). Shipped (validation.py, 38 unit tests).
  • policy_changes hypertable + audit writes per successful POST. Shipped (one row per changed field, POLICY_CHANGE_INSERT).
  • Idempotency: skip publish when POST is a no-op against the existing state. Shipped (200 no-op response, no audit row, no publish).
  • Autoscaler-side live-reload subscriber on smartload.policy. Shipped in §8.8.
  • Future: authn for POST.
  • Future: Prometheus counter for policy_version (SOT §8.9 observability expectation).
Review checklist
  • Is Policy Manager the only writer of config/policy.yaml?
  • Is Policy Manager the only publisher on smartload.policy?
  • Is every POST validated before persistence?
  • Is the YAML write atomic (no partial-write corruption)?
  • Do all subscribers pull policy on startup AND subscribe for updates?
  • Does safe_mode propagation reach all subscribers within 1 s?

8.10 Control Bus (Redis Pub/Sub) Canonical Wired

Purpose

Asynchronous decoupling of decision producers from controller consumers. Multiple services subscribe to the same channel; publishers don't track subscribers.

Configuration (infrastructure/redis/redis.conf)

save ""              # in-memory only — no disk persistence
appendonly no
maxmemory 256mb
maxmemory-policy allkeys-lru
bind 0.0.0.0
port 6379
protected-mode no

Channels & message contracts

All messages are JSON-encoded dataclasses defined in services/shared/contracts.py (canonical):

@dataclass
class AnomalyEvent:
    backend_id: str
    status: str          # healthy | degraded | unhealthy
    score: float
    timestamp: str       # ISO 8601

@dataclass
class ForecastResult:
    horizon_minutes: int
    predicted_rps: float
    confidence_lower: float
    confidence_upper: float
    timestamp: str

@dataclass
class RoutingRecommendation:
    mode: str            # shadow | active
    server_rankings: list[dict]   # [{backend_id, score}]
    timestamp: str
ChannelPublisherSubscribers
smartload.anomalyanomaly-detectorload-balancer sidecar
smartload.forecastforecastingautoscaler
smartload.routingrl-engineload-balancer sidecar
smartload.scaleautoscalerlb-sidecar (v1.0.7z #164) + operator-ui (audit) + webhook-dispatcher (planned #130)
smartload.policypolicy-managerall AI services + sidecar

Historical The SmartLoad_API_Design.pdf proposes a versioned naming (smartload.<topic>.v1) with a richer envelope (schema_version, message_id, type, emitted_at, source, payload). This is a future API design draft — not yet adopted in contracts.py.

Design Contract — Control Bus (Redis Pub/Sub)
Role in the middleware
The communication backbone of the control plane. Asynchronous fan-out from decision producers to controller consumers.
Why it exists
Coupling decision engines directly to the Sidecar (or to each other) would make the system fragile. Pub/sub gives one-publisher / many-subscriber decoupling for free.
Owned responsibilities
Provide best-effort fan-out for the canonical channels (anomaly, forecast, routing, scale, policy). Configured non-persistent (save "", appendonly no) to make message-loss-tolerance the default.
Non-responsibilities
Must NOT be a queue (no delivery guarantees). Must NOT be a database (no persistence). Must NOT be used for request/reply (use REST). Must NOT carry per-request state.
Required interfaces
Redis protocol on port 6379. Channels named smartload.<topic>. Messages JSON-encoded from contracts.py dataclasses.
Failure / fallback behavior
Subscriber crashes → publisher unaffected. Redis itself dies → publishers continue (fire-and-forget); subscribers lose visibility. Recovery: every publisher re-emits each cycle, so subscribers converge automatically.
Scalability expectations
Pub/sub fan-out is sub-millisecond. Memory-bounded with LRU eviction.
Observability expectations
Redis exporter for Prometheus (memory, connections, pub/sub channel count). Log every publish at DEBUG with message size.
Security / safety expectations
Trusted-network. protected-mode no in prototype is acceptable; production requires AUTH or network isolation.
Current state
Wired in Phase 0. Configured non-persistent, 256 MB cap, LRU eviction. All canonical channels in active use by skeleton publishers. Observability shipped v1.0.7ac (#116): oliver006/redis_exporter runs as a compose service, scraped by Prometheus at redis_exporter job; Grafana surfaces connected clients, memory, command rate, pub/sub channel count, keyspace hits/misses, and uptime via the new SmartLoad — Redis (control bus health) dashboard at infrastructure/grafana/dashboards/smartload-redis.json.
Target state
Same. The structural design is right; only the message contents mature as engines mature. Versioned envelope (.v1) is the long-term target — adopted only when a contract genuinely breaks.
Publish-rate budget

Subscribers must keep up; Redis pub/sub has no buffer for late subscribers and a slow consumer drops envelopes silently. Per-channel budgets:

  • smartload.routing — RL engine cycle, default POLL_INTERVAL_SECONDS=5. Expected: ~0.2 pub/sec per active engine. Cap: 2 pub/sec sustained (10× headroom).
  • smartload.anomaly — anomaly-detector cycle, default POLL_INTERVAL_SECONDS=10. Expected: ~0.1 pub/sec per active engine. Cap: 1 pub/sec sustained.
  • smartload.forecast — forecasting cycle, default POLL_INTERVAL_SECONDS=30. Expected: ~0.03 pub/sec. Cap: 0.5 pub/sec sustained.
  • smartload.scale — autoscaler decision (cooldown-gated). Expected: < 0.02 pub/sec. Cap: 0.2 pub/sec sustained.
  • smartload.policy — operator-driven. Expected: < 0.01 pub/sec. Cap: 1 pub/sec sustained (operator burst).

Aggregate alert threshold: sustained rate(redis_commands_processed_total[1m]) > 50 means the bus is doing > 10× its expected steady-state work — likely a stuck client or a misconfigured publisher. The dashboard's command-rate panel is the place to eyeball this; a formal Prometheus alert rule is a follow-up once we have empirical baseline measurements from an adaptive-bench run to anchor the threshold.

Caveats. The numbers above are design targets derived from the configured poll intervals × number of services × an assumed 5-backend pool. They are not yet empirically validated. The first adaptive-bench run after #116 lands gives the baseline measurement; the alert rule lands with it (a follow-up issue captures that work if it doesn't ship in the same release).

Gap to close
  • Add Redis exporter to Prometheus. Shipped v1.0.7ac (#116).
  • Document max acceptable publish rate per channel. Done (see "Publish-rate budget" row above; v1.0.7ac). Empirical baseline + Prometheus alert rule remain as a follow-up after the next adaptive-bench run.
  • Decide on versioned envelope adoption trigger Needs confirmation.
Review checklist
  • Are all messages typed dataclasses from contracts.py?
  • Is every channel one-publisher?
  • Do all subscribers tolerate missed messages?
  • Is Redis treated as ephemeral (no service requires Redis history)?
  • Are no request/reply patterns implemented over Redis?

8.11 Operator-Side Tools — Grafana + Operator UI Canonical Home + Policy + Audit + Actions shipped (slices #1, #2, #3)

Decision: Grafana for charts, lightweight Operator UI for transparency + override

The §5 context diagram has two arrows between the Operator and the SmartLoad middleware: policy / config updates (write) and alerts & dashboard (read). The read-side surface is rendered by two operator-side tools, both sitting outside the SmartLoad middleware bubble:

  • Grafana — time-series charts (Overview / Anomaly / Forecast / RL / Scaling). The thing operators look at to understand the data.
  • Operator UI — Flask BFF (port 8090) + React/Vite SPA, not Flask+Jinja+HTMX. The original "lightweight HTMX" plan was superseded in slice #1 (commit 6f89a13). The thing operators look at to understand the decisions and to act on them safely: live engine signals, policy editing with diff preview, audit log viewer, manual override actions with confirmation modals. Embeds Grafana iframes for charts (does not rebuild charting).

Neither tool is a SmartLoad layer. Neither owns a Redis channel, a TimescaleDB hypertable, or a decision path. Remove them tomorrow and every backend feature still works — operators just drop back to curl + docker logs + raw SQL.

Why two tools, not one

Grafana is excellent at charts and time-series correlation, weak at: confirming manual actions, project-specific decision explanation, in-place policy editing with validation feedback, audit log viewing in domain language. The Operator UI fills exactly those gaps. Building charts in the Operator UI would be duplicate work; running operator-grade governance flows through a Grafana annotation hack would be brittle.

Confirmation model (operator-grade safety)

Per locked design: the UI is not an approval gate for autonomous decisions. The autoscaler keeps reacting to forecasts, the anomaly detector keeps publishing, the LB keeps rerouting around unhealthy backends — none of these wait for an operator click. The UI is an approval gate only for operator-initiated manual overrides: manual scale, manual isolate, force route weights, and policy edits. Each of those gets a confirmation modal before the POST fires. This preserves system autonomy while ensuring no destructive operator click is one accidental keystroke away.

Grafana — five planned dashboards (T2.2 — S4)

DashboardPanelsStatus
OverviewRequest rate, latency p50/p95/max, error rate, total requests, active backend count, telemetry rows ingestedShipped (S2 demo)
AnomalyAnomaly events per backend (timeline), DEGRADED/UNHEALTHY durationsT2.2
ForecastPredicted vs actual request rate (overlay)T2.2
RLRL routing weights per backend vs round-robin baselineT2.2
ScalingScale events overlay on request rateT2.2

Operator UI — page status (5 of 6 shipped)

PagePurposeStatus · slice
HomeAggregated /health across all services, polled every 10 s. Each service rendered as a coloured pill (ok / degraded / bad)Shipped — slice #1 / OUI.1 (#119)
PolicyRead current policy · edit JSON · side-by-side diff preview · commit with audit trail. Auto-refreshes every 10 sShipped — slice #1 / OUI.2 (#120)
AuditUnified viewer over policy_changes + scaling_events with kind / actor / action / limit filters and auto-refresh. Two upstream services normalised through one BFF originShipped — slice #2 / OUI.4 (#122)
ActionsScale-to-N (autoscaler) + isolate backend (anomaly-detector) + disabled force-weights placeholder. Confirmation modal per action with state-change preview. Results feed of latest 10 actionsShipped — slice #3 / OUI.5 (#123)
Live EnginesPer-engine card + SSE event feed colour-coded by channel; BFF holds an in-process per-channel deque (#121, OUI.3)Shipped — OUI.3 (#121, v1.0.7n closes e2e)
LogsPer-service tail viewer (optional / drop if time tightens)Future — #124 (OUI.6) low
Embedded metricsGrafana panels embedded in operator UI (no Grafana context switch)Future — #131 (OUI.8)
Auth (OUI.7, #125) — deferred to Phase 2 SaaS adaptation per §25 Phase scope callout. Actor identity is currently a free-text input on the Actions page.

Architectural notes

  • Manual action endpoints live on the existing services, not on the UI: POST /api/v1/scale on the autoscaler, POST /api/v1/isolate on the anomaly-detector. The UI is a client; the actions remain accessible to curl with or without it.
  • Live engine stream is fed by a tiny subscriber in the telemetry/observability layer (new service services/events-archiver/ or thread in services/telemetry/) that pushes summarised envelopes to a Redis LIST capped at 1000 entries. UI reads with LRANGE. No new hypertable required for the Sprint 4 cut.
  • Durable event archive as a events_log TimescaleDB hypertable is a Sprint 5 stretch — strengthens the thesis "explainability" claim with queryable history beyond 1000 entries. Not required for the demo.
Design Contract — Operator-Side Tools (Grafana + Operator UI)
Role in the middleware
The operator's window into the system. Renders the "alerts & dashboard" arrow from §5. Provides safe in-place edit + override surface for policy and manual actions.
Why it exists
A middleware that cannot be inspected and steered at runtime is unmaintainable. Grafana delivers visibility for free given correct telemetry; the Operator UI delivers decision transparency, governance, and human-in-the-loop override safety — the missing pieces beyond charts.
Owned responsibilities
  • Grafana: render time-series dashboards from TimescaleDB + Prometheus.
  • Operator UI: aggregate /health; surface decision-engine envelope stream; wrap POST /api/v1/policy with diff preview + confirmation; render policy_changes + scaling_events in domain language; expose manual action forms with confirmation modals before any POST.
Non-responsibilities
  • Must NOT be on the request path (neither tool sees client traffic).
  • Must NOT own any Redis channel, TimescaleDB hypertable, or decision path.
  • Must NOT be in the loop for autonomous decisions — autoscaler reacts to forecasts without operator approval; anomaly detector publishes without operator approval; LB reroutes without operator approval. The UI is transparency + override, never gate.
  • Must NOT rebuild charting — embed Grafana iframes for any chart panel.
  • Must NOT have its own backing store. Reads from existing tables and Redis.
Failure / fallback behavior
If Grafana is down, the system continues to operate; operators fall back to direct SQL. If the Operator UI is down, every operator action remains reachable via curl (the UI is a client of the same REST surface). Neither tool is on a critical path.
Current state
Grafana provisioned; Overview dashboard shipped. Four remaining Grafana dashboards (Anomaly / Forecast / RL / Scaling) pending T2.2. Operator UI five pages live on port 8090 (BFF + React SPA): Home (slice #1), Policy (slice #1), Audit (slice #2), Actions (slice #3), Live Engines (#121 session 1, 2026-05-24). Built and served from a single image (services/operator-ui/) with bff/ (Flask) on port 8090 and web/ built as a Vite SPA mounted under /assets/*. The BFF aggregates /api/ui/health + proxies /api/ui/policy, /api/ui/audit/policy, /api/ui/audit/scaling, /api/ui/scale, /api/ui/isolate to the right upstream services. For Live Engines, a daemon Redis-subscriber thread on the BFF consumes smartload.{anomaly,forecast,routing,scale} into an in-process per-channel collections.deque(maxlen=100), fans out to per-SSE-client bounded queues, and exposes GET /api/ui/engines/snapshot (parallel fan-out to each AI service's /api/v1/engine/state + ring snapshot) and GET /api/ui/engines/stream (SSE: replay + live, 15 s heartbeat comments; Gunicorn switched to gthread so long-lived streams don't block other requests). SPA fallback fixed (static_url_path=\"/assets\") so direct URLs and hard refreshes on every page render correctly.
Target state
Five Grafana dashboards version-controlled as JSON; Operator UI six pages on port 8090 (current five + Embedded metrics) embeds Grafana for charts. Both tools restored on container restart via compose.
Gap to close
  • Four remaining Grafana dashboards (Anomaly, Forecast, RL, Scaling) — T2.2.
  • Live Engines acceptance close-out — SDK methods (engines.state() + engines.stream()), e2e suite at tests/e2e/live-engines/, scenario at examples/scenarios/live-engines/, feature manifest at docs/features/live-engines.md; #121 OUI.3 — shipped 2026-06-03 in v1.0.7n; e2e suite (17 tests, 56 s) was the final close-out box.
  • Embedded metrics dashboards — #131 OUI.8 (standalone follow-up).
  • Logs page — #124 OUI.6 (low priority, optional).
  • "Force route weights" form on the Actions page — depends on T2.1 sidecar (#82); placeholder disabled today with a tooltip.
  • Operator UI authn (paired with #56) — #125 OUI.7. Deferred to the Phase 2 SaaS adaptation track per §25 Phase scope callout.
  • Optional Sprint 5 stretch: durable events_log hypertable for richer explainability history.
Review checklist
  • Is every operator action available via curl even without the UI?
  • Does every manual override action have a confirmation modal before the POST?
  • Do autonomous decisions ever wait for operator approval? (They must not.)
  • Are Grafana dashboards version-controlled as JSON?
  • Does the Operator UI embed Grafana iframes, not rebuild charting?
  • Does the Operator UI own any Redis channel or TimescaleDB hypertable? (It must not.)
