Per the canonical wiring rule, this is not adopted until reviewed and integrated.

Design Contract — Dataset / Data Prep Pipeline
Role in the middleware
Off-runtime artifact producer. Feeds offline training of anomaly + forecast + RL models.
Why it exists
Each AI engine needs labeled / clean data to train against. Without a shared canonical loader interface, every engine would reinvent its own ingestion.
Owned responsibilities
  • Acquisition scripts (scripts/download-datasets.sh).
  • Per-dataset preprocessing (datasets/<name>/preprocess.py).
  • Canonical loaders (datasets/loaders.py): load_nab, load_borg_timeseries, load_alibaba_traces, load_yahoo_smd.
  • Evaluation utilities (evaluate_anomaly.py, evaluate_forecasting.py).
Non-responsibilities
Must NOT run inside any runtime container. Must NOT write to TimescaleDB. Must NOT publish on the control bus.
Target output / deliverable
  • Versioned, gitignored datasets at data/datasets/.
  • Reproducible loaders that return canonical pandas DataFrames.
  • Per-dataset README with provenance, license, schema.
  • EDA notebooks producing distribution + anomaly-rate plots.
Failure / fallback behavior
Acquisition failures → script exits non-zero with clear error. Loader signature is the contract — engines depend only on the DataFrame shape, not on file paths.
Current state
R0.x active: NAB + Borg acquisition wired. Loaders signed but not yet returning data.
Target state
All 4 canonical datasets loadable; evaluation harnesses produce comparable metrics across runs; offline training scripts consume only loaders, never raw files.

  • Decide adoption of formal Feature Contracts Needs confirmation.

Design Contract — Testing & Benchmarking Harness
Role in the middleware
The honesty filter. Every architectural commitment in this document is only real if a test verifies it.
Why it exists
A documented design without tests rots. Integration tests against real Redis + TimescaleDB are the project's main defense against silent regressions.
Owned responsibilities
  • Unit tests for pure logic (no Redis, no DB).
  • Integration tests against real Redis + TimescaleDB (no mocks for these).
  • Benchmark harness B1 (steady), B2 (burst), B3 (degraded backend).
  • Locust-based load generator for benchmarks.
Non-responsibilities
Must NOT mock Redis or TimescaleDB in integration tests (defeats the whole purpose). Must NOT depend on internet access (datasets are pre-staged).
Target output / deliverable

  • CI integration so benchmarks run on tagged releases.
Review checklist
  • Does every new feature have at least one integration test?
  • Are integration tests using real Redis + TimescaleDB?
  • Are benchmarks reproducible (fixed seeds, fixed traffic profiles)?
  • Does CI fail if any integration test fails?
  • Are KPI thresholds (P95 latency, MAPE, F1) enforced as test assertions where measurable?

8.14 Deployment Layer Canonical docker-compose

Local / dev (current)

Single docker-compose.yml with 14 services on a private bridge network smartload-net. Healthchecks on TimescaleDB and Redis ensure dependent services start only after both are up.

# From running-the-project.md (Phase 2 — current)
cp config/.env.example .env
docker compose up --build -d
pytest tests/integration/ -v

CI/CD

GitHub Actions: lint, unit tests, build Docker images on every push. Branch policy: feat/<task-id>-short-description; one branch per task; PR to main when done.

Future deployment options (out of scope for prototype)

  • Kubernetes via Helm charts.
  • AWS EC2 + RDS for TimescaleDB.
  • Azure / GCP equivalents.

Reproducibility

All env vars documented in config/.env.example. All schemas regenerable via infrastructure/timescaledb/init.sql. Redis is intentionally non-persistent so a restart never leaves stale state.

Design Contract — Deployment Layer
Role in the middleware
The substrate. Defines how every service is built, run, and connected.
Why it exists
A documented architecture is only useful if there's a one-command path to a green stack. docker-compose up --build -d is that path for the prototype.
Owned responsibilities
  • Single docker-compose.yml orchestrating 16 containers (modulo test-backend scaling).
  • One Dockerfile per service, each reproducible from requirements.txt + Python 3.x base image.
  • Private bridge network smartload-net; healthchecks on TimescaleDB + Redis as startup dependencies.
  • CI/CD via GitHub Actions: lint, unit tests, build images on push.
  • Configuration surface: config/.env sourced from config/.env.example.
Non-responsibilities
Must NOT bake secrets into images. Must NOT pin to a single cloud provider. Must NOT couple the prototype to an orchestrator that can't run on a laptop.
Required interfaces
Docker Engine ≥ 20.x; Docker Compose v2; Python ≥ 3.10 (for tooling).
Failure / fallback behavior
Container health check fails → docker-compose marks unhealthy; dependent services wait. CI build fails → branch cannot merge.
Scalability expectations
Prototype runs single replicas. Design must be portable to Kubernetes without code changes (the design choices that enable this: Redis pub/sub, stateless services, bind to 0.0.0.0, env-driven config).
Observability expectations
Every service writes to stdout (JSON). docker compose logs -f <svc> is the prototype's primary debugging surface.
Security / safety expectations
config/.env is gitignored. CI must fail if a secret-pattern is committed. Production-bound deploys add Docker secrets / Kubernetes secrets.
Current state
docker-compose with 14 services (incl. lb-otel-shipper sidecar from T1.2). CI pipeline running on push. running-the-project.md documents the one-command flow.
Target state
Same for prototype. Future targets: Helm charts for K8s, Terraform modules for AWS / Azure / GCP. None on the critical path.
Gap to close
  • Tag-driven release builds in CI.
  • Image signing (Cosign) for production readiness.
  • Helm chart authoring for the K8s target.
Review checklist
  • Does docker compose up --build -d from a clean clone produce a green stack?
  • Is every secret sourced from config/.env, never committed?
  • Does CI fail on lint, unit, or integration test failure?
  • Is every service health-check present and meaningful?
  • Can the stack survive a single-container restart?
  • Are images tagged consistently and pushed by CI?

9. Data Ownership & Information Flow

Canonical Design Intent

Every kind of data in SmartLoad has exactly one authoritative writer and a documented set of consumers (principle 8). This section is the single place to look up who owns what. Multiple writers to the same data class is forbidden. When a service needs data it does not own, it must read it through the owner's contract — not by reaching into the same store directly.

Data classes

Data classExamplesLifetimeAuthoritative writerAuthoritative store
Raw telemetry request_count, request_latency_ms, error_rate, cpu_usage, memory_usage Persisted (TimescaleDB hypertable; retention by policy) Telemetry Service TimescaleDB metrics
Backend health (derived) status ∈ {healthy, degraded, unhealthy}, anomaly score Persisted (TimescaleDB hypertable) Anomaly Detector TimescaleDB backend_health + Redis smartload.anomaly
Forecast signals predicted_rps, confidence interval, horizon Ephemeral (Redis publish only — current); could persist for replay Forecasting Engine Redis smartload.forecast
Routing recommendations server_rankings, mode (shadow|active) Ephemeral RL Engine Redis smartload.routing
Scaling events scale_out / scale_in, instance_count, reason Persisted Autoscaler TimescaleDB scaling_events + Redis smartload.scale
Policy state operating_mode, safe_mode, min/max_backends, slo_p95_latency_ms Persistent (YAML file) + push on change Policy Manager File: config/policy.yaml + Redis smartload.policy
Effective routing config NGINX upstream block (weights + exclusions) Live config in NGINX, regenerated on signal LB Sidecar T2.1 target NGINX nginx.conf (in-memory after reload)
ML model artifacts policy.zip (PPO), isolation_forest.pkl, prophet.pkl Versioned files Offline training pipeline (notebooks / scripts) Filesystem under services/<svc>/models/
Datasets & replays NAB, Borg traces, Yahoo SMD, simulation episodes Versioned files Dataset / Data Prep pipeline Filesystem under data/datasets/ (gitignored)

Producers and consumers (information flow)

ProducerChannel / storeConsumersWhy this consumer reads this
NGINX + backendsOTLP → TimescaleDB metricsAnomaly, Forecasting, RL, Operator (Grafana)The shared ground truth of system state.
Anomaly DetectorRedis smartload.anomalyLB Sidecar (gating routing)Sidecar excludes UNHEALTHY backends from upstream.
Anomaly DetectorTimescaleDB backend_healthOperator (Grafana), post-hoc analysisAudit trail and dashboarding; not on the request path.
Forecasting EngineRedis smartload.forecastAutoscalerDrives proactive scale_out / scale_in.
RL EngineRedis smartload.routingLB Sidecar (when mode=active)Sets weighted upstream entries.
AutoscalerRedis smartload.scale + Docker SDK + scaling_eventsOperator (audit), Policy Manager (audit log)Operator visibility; downstream services do not need to react synchronously.
Policy ManagerRedis smartload.policyAll AI services + LB SidecarOne config truth for all decision components.
Operator (CLI / curl)REST POST /api/v1/policy → Policy Manager—Human-in-the-loop override.

Source-of-record rules

  1. Telemetry is owned by Telemetry Service — no other service writes to metrics.
  2. Health is owned by Anomaly Detector — neither RL nor LB derives its own health view; both read through the anomaly channel.
  3. Routing weights are owned by the LB Sidecar — RL produces recommendations, not authoritative weights. The sidecar is the merge point.
  4. Scaling actions are owned by Autoscaler — only it calls Docker SDK; only it writes scaling_events.
  5. Policy is owned by Policy Manager — it is the only writer to config/policy.yaml and the only publisher to smartload.policy.

Dedup & ordering rules (canonical)

  • Dedup by event_id — every published message carries a UUID v4 (see §11). Subscribers that perform actions (e.g. Autoscaler, LB Sidecar reload) must dedup by event_id within a 5 min window so a re-publish never causes a double action.
  • Order by timestamp — pub/sub does not guarantee order across publishers, but each channel has exactly one publisher (rule above), so timestamp ordering is well-defined per channel.
  • Snapshot semantics for policy — every smartload.policy publish is a full snapshot, not a diff. A subscriber that joins late just consumes the next publish and is up to date. policy_version is a monotonic integer; subscribers that observe a gap (v3 then v5) know they missed v4 but their state is still consistent because they have the latest snapshot.

Anti-patterns to refuse

  • An AI service writing directly to metrics (forbidden — only Telemetry Service writes).
  • RL Engine deriving health flags by reading raw latency rather than subscribing to smartload.anomaly (duplicates ownership of "health"; risks divergence).
  • LB Sidecar reading TimescaleDB on the request path (violates principle 2; data plane must remain pure).
  • Policy Manager subscribing to its own smartload.policy channel for state (it owns the state; it should not depend on its own publish loop for correctness).
  • Two services writing to the same Redis channel (channels have one publisher; multiple subscribers are fine).

Review Checklist — Data Ownership

  • Does the new code introduce a second writer to any data class above? If yes, redesign.
  • Is every read either through the owner's contract (Redis channel, REST endpoint) or through the canonical store (read-only)?
  • Does any service derive a fact that another service is the source of record for? (E.g., health, routing weights, policy.) If yes, replace with subscription to the owner.
  • Are ephemeral signals (forecast, routing) treated as best-effort by their consumers? (Subscribers must tolerate gaps.)
  • Are persistent writes idempotent under retry?

10. Storage & Schema Authority

Canonical Design Intent

Storage is an architecture decision, not an incidental wiring detail. SmartLoad uses three storage classes — a time-series database (TimescaleDB), an in-memory pub/sub (Redis), and a versioned file system for models and policy. Each is chosen for a specific access pattern; using the wrong store for the wrong pattern is a design error, not an implementation detail.

Storage classes

ClassEngineUsed forWhy this engineAnti-use
Time-series TimescaleDB (PostgreSQL extension) Telemetry, derived health, scaling events. SQL expressiveness for analytics + hypertable partitioning for fast time-range queries; familiar to ML services. Don't use it for transactional state or for ephemeral signals.
In-memory pub/sub Redis Decision signals between services (anomaly, forecast, routing, policy, scale). Microsecond-latency fan-out; dependency-light; multi-language client support. Don't treat it as a persistent store. Don't use it as a key-value cache for facts that must survive restart.
Versioned files Filesystem (git for policy.yaml; gitignored for models/datasets) ML model artifacts, policy YAML, datasets, simulation traces. Reproducibility, easy diff, easy to swap into a new container build. Don't use it for high-throughput writes or shared-by-many-writers state.

Canonical schema (TimescaleDB)

Phase 0 wired schema (3 hypertables) is the canonical baseline. A richer V1 schema exists as a Draft and is not adopted yet — see comparison below.

-- Canonical (Phase 0, wired)
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS timescaledb;

CREATE TABLE metrics (
  time         TIMESTAMPTZ      NOT NULL,
  service      TEXT             NOT NULL,
  instance     TEXT             NOT NULL,
  metric_name  TEXT             NOT NULL,
  value        DOUBLE PRECISION NOT NULL
);
SELECT create_hypertable('metrics', 'time', if_not_exists => TRUE);
CREATE INDEX idx_metrics_service_instance ON metrics (service, instance, time DESC);
CREATE INDEX idx_metrics_metric_name      ON metrics (metric_name, time DESC);

CREATE TABLE backend_health (
  time        TIMESTAMPTZ      NOT NULL,
  backend_id  TEXT             NOT NULL,
  status      TEXT             NOT NULL,   -- healthy|degraded|unhealthy
  score       DOUBLE PRECISION NOT NULL    -- 0.0=normal, 1.0=very anomalous
);
SELECT create_hypertable('backend_health', 'time', if_not_exists => TRUE);

CREATE TABLE scaling_events (
  time           TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL,
  action         TEXT        NOT NULL,    -- scale_out|scale_in
  instance_count INT         NOT NULL,
  reason         TEXT
);
SELECT create_hypertable('scaling_events', 'time', if_not_exists => TRUE);

-- Retention (added 2026-05-05, PR #105)
SELECT add_retention_policy('metrics',         INTERVAL '7 days',  if_not_exists => TRUE);
SELECT add_retention_policy('backend_health',  INTERVAL '30 days', if_not_exists => TRUE);
SELECT add_retention_policy('scaling_events',  INTERVAL '90 days', if_not_exists => TRUE);

-- Continuous aggregate (long-format, materialized_only=true)
CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW metrics_1min
WITH (timescaledb.continuous, timescaledb.materialized_only = true) AS
SELECT
  time_bucket('1 minute', time) AS bucket,
  service, instance, metric_name,
  AVG(value)    AS avg_value,
  MAX(value)    AS max_value,
  MIN(value)    AS min_value,
  SUM(value)    AS sum_value,
  STDDEV(value) AS stddev_value,
  COUNT(*)      AS sample_count
FROM metrics
GROUP BY bucket, service, instance, metric_name
WITH NO DATA;

SELECT add_continuous_aggregate_policy('metrics_1min',
  start_offset      => INTERVAL '1 hour',
  end_offset        => INTERVAL '2 minutes',
  schedule_interval => INTERVAL '1 minute',
  if_not_exists     => TRUE);

SELECT add_retention_policy('metrics_1min', INTERVAL '30 days', if_not_exists => TRUE);

P95 is intentionally not computed in the CAGG. PERCENTILE_CONT is an ordered-set aggregate which TimescaleDB CAGGs reject; engines and dashboards compute P95 on raw metrics over a bounded window (cheap at SmartLoad scale). The materialized_only=true flag means queries against the view see materialised data only; the [-2 min, now] window is only visible via raw metrics — anomaly-detector and RL state queries continue to read raw, which is the design intent.

Why this schema (rationale)

  • Wide-narrow telemetry. One row per metric reading (key/value form) keeps the schema stable as new metrics are added — no ALTER TABLE on every new feature. The trade is denormalization: queries with many metrics need to join or pivot.
  • String status column instead of an ENUM. Acceptable for the prototype; an ENUM would tighten correctness but couples schema migrations to model changes. The V1 design proposes ENUMs (see comparison).
  • No FK constraints between hypertables. TimescaleDB hypertables don't compose well with cross-hypertable FKs; the schema relies on shared column conventions (backend_id, instance) instead.
  • Indexes match the canonical query patterns in services/shared/queries.py (anomaly window query, forecast window query, RL state query). Index choices are informed by query, not by guesswork.

Preferred Canonical Design — schema

Schema design options for SmartLoad telemetry
Preferred (current canonical)
3-table wide-narrow schema

One row per (time, service, instance, metric_name, value). Extensible to new metrics without ALTER TABLE. Wired and validated by 33 integration tests.

Option B
V1 normalized 6-table

SDP/db/V1__smartload_schema.sql: backend_nodes (UUID PK), policies, telemetry_metrics with explicit columns, routing_decisions, anomaly_events, scaling_events; 8 ENUMs, retention policies, continuous aggregates.

Option C (rejected)
Pure TSDB (Prometheus-only)

No SQL store. All metrics via Prometheus queries. Cleaner for ops dashboards but loses SQL expressiveness for ML training and post-hoc analysis.

Why Option A (wide-narrow) is preferred today: Lower schema-evolution friction during the active research phase. Every new ML feature would otherwise force an ALTER TABLE. Why Option B is the strong target: Once metric set is stable, ENUMs + explicit columns + continuous aggregates give meaningful correctness and performance gains. Target — Phase 2 review Tradeoff: Option B is more schema-rigid; migration requires a backfill plan. Option B's routing_decisions table also adds a new write source on the request path or near it — needs careful design to stay off the data plane. Open: Needs confirmation on whether to migrate after Phase 2 or keep as parallel design.

Redis as a control bus (storage rules)

  • Redis is configured save "" + appendonly no: no disk persistence. The control bus assumes message loss is possible.
  • Subscribers must tolerate missed messages (publishers always re-publish on the next cycle).
  • Channel naming is smartload.<topic> with no .v1 suffix in the canonical wiring (per services/shared/contracts.py). The versioned form (smartload.<topic>.v1) appears in the API Design draft and is deferred until contract evolution requires it.
  • Maximum memory is bounded (maxmemory 256mb + allkeys-lru); sustained backlog will evict the oldest entries — pub/sub is fan-out, not a queue.

Filesystem ownership rules

  • config/policy.yaml — owned by Policy Manager. Other services may read it on startup but never write.
  • services/<svc>/models/*.pkl / policy.zip — owned by the offline training pipeline. The runtime container only reads.
  • data/datasets/ — owned by the dataset prep pipeline. Gitignored. Reproducible from versioned download scripts.
  • infrastructure/timescaledb/init.sql — owned by infra team. Re-run on every fresh deploy.

Storage anti-patterns

  • Storing decision signals (anomaly, forecast) only in Redis when they need replay or audit — escalate to TimescaleDB if needed.
  • Storing raw telemetry in Redis — Redis is fan-out, not history.
  • Adding a NEW table without updating infrastructure/timescaledb/init.sql + services/shared/queries.py.
  • Caching authoritative state (e.g., policy) in process memory without a refresh path on Redis publish.
  • Exposing internal schema directly to external clients via REST. Always project through a documented API shape.

Review Checklist — Storage & Schema

  • Is the chosen storage class right for the access pattern (time-series vs ephemeral vs versioned files)?
  • Does the table have only one writer (a single service)?
  • Are query indexes informed by the canonical queries in services/shared/queries.py?
  • Are retention and growth bounded (hypertable retention or LRU eviction)?
  • Are schema changes versioned (init.sql + matching contract update)?
  • Does the design tolerate Redis restart (no reliance on Redis as a persistence layer)?

11. Interface & Contract Authority

Canonical Design Intent

Every service interaction in SmartLoad is one of three contract types: a Redis pub/sub message, a REST endpoint, or a SQL query constant. There is no fourth. Contracts are typed and versionable — there is no ad-hoc JSON, no inline SQL outside queries.py, no cross-service imports of internal code. This section is the authority on what every contract should look like.

Supersession Notice — read this before SmartLoad_API_Design.pdf

The original SDP-era SDP/SmartLoad_API_Design.pdf is historical. It is a design draft predating implementation. This section (§11) and the per-module §8.x deep dives are the authoritative contract source. Where the PDF and this SOT disagree, this SOT wins. Major intentional deviations from the PDF — each documented in its module section:

  • REST API shape: single-document /api/v1/policy (GET/POST) instead of the PDF's multi-policy /api/v1/policies/{id}/activate. SmartLoad has one active policy at a time; the multi-policy model was scope creep. See §8.9.
  • No /internal/v1/* namespace. Services coordinate via Redis pub/sub (5 canonical channels below); the PDF's internal REST namespace was rejected in favour of the pub/sub control bus to keep coupling loose. See §8.10.
  • Channel naming: no .v1 suffix. PDF proposes smartload.<topic>.v1; current canonical is unsuffixed (smartload.anomaly, etc.). Deferred — see §15.
  • Envelope shape: leaner. PDF envelope has schema_version, message_id, type, emitted_at, source.service; the wired envelope (services/shared/contracts.py::Envelope) has event_id, source, version, timestamp, payload. Functionally equivalent, less verbose. See §15.
  • No backend registry REST. PDF has /api/v1/backends + /quarantine + /restore; the running system uses Docker SDK + NGINX upstream as the implicit registry. Manual override flows are covered by the T1.4 / OUI.5 endpoints (POST /api/v1/scale, POST /api/v1/isolate). See §8.8.
  • No /api/v1/experiments surface. Benchmarking is offline and CI-driven (issues #36–#39, #117), not REST-controlled.
  • Operator UI: PDF §11.5 listed a "Rich operator UI with full runtime control" as a future extension. As of 2026-05-13 this is in scope as a lightweight UI — transparency + override layer, not full runtime control. See §8.11 + issues #119–#125.
  • Error format: current responses use {error, field}; PDF mandates RFC 7807 Problem Details ({type, title, status, detail, instance}). Adoptable; left for the canonical doc landing under issue #53.
  • Adopted from PDF as-is: TimescaleDB + raw/aggregate tables, Redis pub/sub for decision signals, channel-per-topic separation, the decision-merge hierarchy (anomaly → policy → RL → classical → forecast drives scaling), one active policy with safe-mode override, OTLP/HTTP-JSON telemetry ingest.

The reconciliation work that produces the canonical external API doc (docs/api/api-v1.md) is tracked under #53. Once that doc lands, the PDF is officially retired — keep it in the repo only for historical / SDP-grader context.

Contract surfaces

SurfaceMechanismCanonical locationSync / asyncVersioning rule
Redis messagePub/Sub JSON envelopeservices/shared/contracts.py (dataclasses)AsyncAdd fields = compatible. Remove or rename = new channel suffix (.v2).
RESTHTTP/JSONPer-service app.py route handlers; documented under /api/v1SyncAdd field = compatible. Breaking = new /api/v2 base.
SQLParameterized SQL stringsservices/shared/queries.pySyncSchema change requires init.sql update + query update + tests.

Canonical Redis message contracts

All messages are JSON-encoded dataclasses defined in services/shared/contracts.py. The current canonical surface (no .v1 suffix):

ChannelProducerSubscribersRequired fieldsOptional fieldsCycle
smartload.anomaly Anomaly Detector LB Sidecar backend_id, status, score, timestamp features (debug) ~5 s
smartload.forecast Forecasting Engine Autoscaler horizon_minutes, predicted_rps, timestamp confidence_lower, confidence_upper, model_id ~60 s
smartload.routing RL Engine LB Sidecar mode (shadow|active), server_rankings:[{backend_id, score}], timestamp policy_version ~5 s
smartload.scale Autoscaler LB sidecar (v1.0.7z #164 — re-queries Docker pool + rewrites upstream.conf) + operator-ui (Live Engines audit) + webhook-dispatcher (planned #130) action, instance_count, reason, mechanism, timestamp forecast_event_id (provenance — joins back to triggering ForecastResult) on action
smartload.policy Policy Manager All AI services + LB Sidecar full policy snapshot, policy_version, timestamp — on change

Canonical message envelope

Every Redis message carries the same four envelope fields, regardless of channel. Payload-specific fields (anomaly score, predicted RPS, etc.) are added on top.

{
  "event_id":  "uuid-v4",                  // unique per publish; enables dedup
  "source":    "anomaly-detector",         // publisher service name (kebab-case)
  "version":   1,                          // envelope schema version (int)
  "timestamp": "2026-05-04T10:23:00.123Z", // RFC 3339 UTC
  "payload":   { ... }                     // typed dataclass body
}
FieldTypeWhy required
event_idUUID v4 stringSubscribers MAY deduplicate by ID. Without it, retries and re-publishes are indistinguishable from new events.
sourcestring (service name)Auditability. A publish on the wrong channel by the wrong service is detectable.
versionintegerEnvelope evolution. Bumped only when envelope semantics change, independent of the channel suffix.
timestampISO 8601 UTCStaleness detection. Subscribers reject messages older than a channel-specific TTL.

Envelope rules

  • Wrap, don't flatten. Payload fields live inside payload, not at the top level. This lets the envelope evolve independently of payloads.
  • No request/reply — pub/sub is one-way fan-out. If a request/reply pattern is needed, use REST.
  • No "command" channels. Channels publish state or events, not commands. The LB Sidecar decides how to act on a state observation; it is not commanded by the engines.
  • Idempotency by event_id — every consumer must handle re-publishes correctly. Engines re-emit on every cycle; consumers dedup by event_id if order matters, or simply use the latest timestamp if order doesn't.
  • Reject silently outdated payloads. If now() - timestamp > channel_ttl, drop the message. Default TTLs: anomaly 30 s, forecast 180 s, routing 30 s, policy never expires.

Channel-specific payload fields

The required envelope above is universal. Each channel adds its own payload fields (these supersede the looser earlier table):

ChannelPayload fields (required)Payload fields (optional)
smartload.anomaly backend_id, status, score features (debug map), model_version
smartload.forecast horizon_minutes, predicted_rps confidence_lower, confidence_upper, model_id
smartload.routing mode (shadow|active), server_rankings:[{backend_id, score}] policy_version (the policy snapshot RL was reasoning under)
smartload.scale action, instance_count, reason forecast_event_id (provenance — which forecast triggered this)
smartload.policy full policy snapshot (all canonical fields), policy_version (monotonic int) changed_fields: list of field names that differ from previous publish
Migration note — applied 2026-05-05 in PR #105. services/shared/contracts.py now ships the canonical Envelope dataclass plus make_envelope, publish_envelope, parse_envelope, and subscribe_envelope helpers (the latter two with an on_drop(reason, raw) callback so dropped messages are observable per SOT §8.3). The five missing channel-payload fields landed alongside: policy_version (required on PolicyUpdate, optional on RoutingRecommendation), forecast_event_id on ScalingEvent, features / model_version on AnomalyEvent, and model_id on ForecastResult. changed_fields on PolicyUpdate is the only addition beyond the SOT requirements.

Channel TTLs (anomaly 30 s, routing 30 s, forecast 180 s, policy & scale never expire) are exposed as CHANNEL_TTL_SECONDS; subscribers using subscribe_envelope drop stale and naive-datetime messages without crashing the polling loop. The legacy flat json_encode / json_decode helpers remain so the 33-test Phase 0 suite stays green during the transition.

Canonical REST endpoints

MethodPathOwner (service · port)Purpose · slice
GET/api/v1/policyPolicy Manager · 8086Read current policy as JSON. Shipped — slice #1
POST/api/v1/policyPolicy Manager · 8086Update policy fields; idempotent (no-op on unchanged body); persists YAML; writes policy_changes rows; publishes PolicyUpdate on smartload.policy. Shipped — slice #1
GET/api/v1/audit/policyPolicy Manager · 8086Recent rows from policy_changes, newest first. ?limit=N capped at 1000. Shipped — slice #1
GET/api/v1/audit/scalingAutoscaler · 8085Recent rows from scaling_events, newest first. ?limit=N capped at 1000. Shipped — slice #2 (#122)
POST/api/v1/scaleAutoscaler · 8085Manual scale to target_count bypassing cooldown. Validates against policy.min/max_backends; writes one scaling_events row with manual:<actor>: prefix; publishes one ScalingEvent with forecast_event_id=null. Shipped — slice #3 (#123)
POST/api/v1/isolateAnomaly Detector · 8082Manually publish a synthetic AnomalyEvent for a backend; writes backend_health row. Shipped — slice #3 (#123)
GET/api/v1/engine/stateAnomaly Detector · 8082Engine bootstrap (requested vs loaded + ready + error), operating-policy snapshot, runloop tick / publish counters with last-tick & last-publish ISO timestamps, last cycle's list[AnomalyScore] payload. Always 200; runloop-disabled is a state, not an error. Consumed by the BFF Live Engines snapshot endpoint. Shipped — #121 session 1
GET/api/v1/engine/stateForecasting · 8083Same shape as the anomaly-detector endpoint; last_output is a single ForecastResult dict per cycle (recorded every tick, not just on publish, so the UI sees what the engine would emit under safe_mode). Shipped — #121 session 1
GET/api/v1/engine/stateRL Engine · 8084Same shape; engine.kind="policy" and adds top-level rl_mode_env so the UI can show the operator-pinned mode independent of what the policy returned. Shipped — #121 session 1
GET/api/v1/metricsTelemetry · 8081Recent rows as JSON. Params: service (string), window (Prometheus-style: 30s / 5m / 1h / 2d). Returns {service, window, rows[]}; capped at READ_API_ROW_LIMIT (default 10 000). Shipped — T1.1
POST/v1/metricsTelemetry · 8081OTLP/HTTP-JSON ingress from the OTel Collector. Always returns 200; DB write failures observable via /api/v1/stats.rows_dropped_db. Out-of-set metric types (histogram / summary) counted as rows_dropped_shape. Shipped — T1.1
GET/api/v1/statsTelemetry · 8081Write / drop counters: rows_written, batches_written, rows_dropped_db, rows_dropped_shape. Shipped — T1.1
GET/healthEvery serviceLiveness check. Returns 200 OK if all dependencies (Redis, TimescaleDB, model files where applicable) are reachable; 503 Service Unavailable if any dependency is down. Body includes per-dependency booleans for diagnostics. HTTP 207 Multi-Status is reserved for WebDAV batch responses and must not be used here — Kubernetes liveness probes treat 2xx as healthy, so 207 would silently classify a degraded service as healthy. The load-balancer's /health is a static NGINX response ({"status":"ok","service":"load-balancer"}); other paths still proxy to the backend pool.
GET/api/v1/statusOperator UI BFF · 8090Consolidated read across every service + active policy + most recent rows from both audit streams. Always 200; per-service failures surface as {"status":"down"} in the services map, overall rolls them into one pill (ok / degraded / down). Parallel fan-out with 2 s per-service timeout. Shipped — slice #5 / OUI.9 (#149, v1.0.7q)
GET/api/v1/anomalies targetAnomaly Detector · 8082Recent anomaly events for operator dashboards.
GET/api/v1/forecast targetForecasting Engine · 8083Latest forecast snapshot.
GET/api/v1/routing/state targetLB Sidecar · T2.1Current effective routing config (debugging).

Every /api/v1/* route in this table is checked by scripts/lint-openapi.py against docs/openapi/smartload-v1.yaml. New endpoints land in the OpenAPI file in the same commit they ship in source.

Error response shape (target)

All REST errors should follow a single Problem-Details-style shape (RFC 7807 minus the URI):

{
  "error": "ValidationError",
  "message": "min_backends must be < max_backends",
  "field": "min_backends",
  "timestamp": "2026-05-04T10:23:00Z"
}
Gap Today, error responses are inconsistent across services. Standardize during Phase 2 hardening.

Canonical SQL access

All cross-service SQL is centralized in services/shared/queries.py. New queries that any service needs must be added to this file with a docstring describing producer/consumer/use case. The canonical query constants are:

  • METRICS_INSERT — Telemetry Service writes (only producer).
  • METRICS_BATCH_INSERT — multi-row insert helper for high write rates (preferred above ~50 rps).
  • ANOMALY_QUERY — Anomaly Detector reads (windowed per-instance latency / error rate).
  • FORECAST_QUERY — Forecasting Engine reads (request-rate time series, minute-bucketed).
  • RL_STATE_QUERY — RL Engine reads (per-backend latency / request count / error rate over last 30 s).
  • BACKEND_HEALTH_QUERY — read latest health per backend.
  • BACKEND_HEALTH_INSERT — Anomaly Detector writes.
  • SCALING_EVENT_INSERT — Autoscaler writes.

Canonical SQL parameterization rule

Time windows and any other dynamic SQL value must be passed as parameterized values, never via Python string formatting. The correct shape for an interval is:

# Correct — parameterized, safe, cache-friendly
ANOMALY_QUERY = """
    SELECT instance, metric_name, AVG(value), MAX(value), STDDEV(value), COUNT(*)
    FROM metrics
    WHERE time > NOW() - %s::interval
      AND service = %s
      AND metric_name = ANY(%s)
    GROUP BY instance, metric_name
    ORDER BY instance, metric_name;
"""

cursor.execute(ANOMALY_QUERY, ('60 seconds', 'load-balancer', ['request_latency_ms', 'error_rate']))

The earlier .format(window=...) pattern is forbidden because (a) it bypasses parameterization, (b) PostgreSQL cannot cache the prepared plan when the query text varies, and (c) it leaks an unsafe-shaped construct into a security-sensitive area even when the values aren't user-controlled today.

Canonical service filter rule (anomaly query)

The anomaly query takes service as a parameter rather than hardcoding 'load-balancer'. This lets the same engine reason about backend-emitted telemetry once backends start emitting OTLP directly. The default operator setting is still 'load-balancer'; the parameter just keeps the design extensible.

Versioning rules (canonical)

  1. Additive change is free. Adding an optional field to a message or REST response is non-breaking. Subscribers must ignore unknown fields.
  2. Removal or rename is breaking. Either ship a new channel/endpoint version, or coordinate a synchronous cutover with all consumers.
  3. Semantic change is breaking even if the field name is unchanged. If score changes meaning, version it.
  4. The contracts.py file is the single source of truth. Tests must import from there, not redefine.

Synchronous vs asynchronous — when to choose which

ScenarioPickWhy
Operator changes policyREST (sync)Operator must see immediate confirmation.
Engine emits a decisionPub/Sub (async)Decoupling; subscriber failure must not stop publisher.
Service queries telemetrySQL (sync read)Engines need consistent snapshots within their cycle.
Sidecar reloads NGINXLocal syscall (async w.r.t. callers)Reload must not block the engine that triggered it.
Service starts up and needs current policyREST GET (sync, on startup) + pub/sub subscribe (async, ongoing)Pull on startup, push on change — no waiting for the next publish.

Review Checklist — Interface & Contract

  • Is every cross-service interaction one of: Redis pub/sub, REST, or SQL constant? No ad-hoc JSON, no shared in-process imports.
  • Is every Redis message a typed dataclass in contracts.py?
  • Does every consumer tolerate missing or stale messages (graceful degradation)?
  • Is every SQL query a constant in services/shared/queries.py?
  • Does any change rename or remove a field? If yes, is it versioned (.v2 channel or /api/v2)?
  • Are all REST errors shaped like the canonical Problem Details target?
  • Is every service's /health endpoint actually testing its dependencies (Redis, TimescaleDB), not just returning 200?

14. Data & ML Foundations

What each ML module needs

ModuleData typeGranularityRequired for training
ForecastingLong, continuous time-series of system usage (CPU, memory, request rate)1-min (resampled from source 5-min)≥ 7 days per node; no gaps; temporal train/val/test split
Anomaly DetectionTime-series with both normal and labeled-abnormal sequences30-sec sliding windows, 50% overlap≥ 1% positive rate; class imbalance handled (SMOTE / weighted)
Reinforcement LearningSimulation environment (not a static dataset)5-sec decision epochsRealistic workload trace replay (Borg / Alibaba) + failure injection

Canonical dataset choices

See full table in §6.12. Headline: NAB + Yahoo SMD (anomaly), Google Borg + Alibaba (forecasting + RL).

Preprocessing notes

  • All datasets normalized to [0, 1] ranges (CPU, memory, error rate).
  • Temporal split only — never random — to avoid leakage in time-series models.
  • Forward-fill missing intervals up to 5 windows; flag beyond.
  • Cyclical encoding (sin/cos) for hour-of-day and day-of-week features.

Compatibility & limitations

  • Borg/Alibaba traces are large — require downsampling (5-min epochs).
  • Some datasets (NAB) are univariate — supplemented by injection scripts and Yahoo SMD multivariate features.
  • For missing failure types, synthetic anomaly injection: spike CPU to 0.95, error_rate to 0.3 for 2–5 windows.

Licensing

DatasetLicenseRequired attribution
NABMITCite Numenta Anomaly Benchmark
Google BorgCC-BYCite Google cluster-data project
Alibaba tracesOpenCite Alibaba clusterdata project
Yahoo SMDOpenCite OmniAnomaly / NetManAIOps
Future-iteration data engineering contracts Draft, pending review

A formal data engineering contract spec exists in SDP/db/SmartLoad_Feature_Contracts_v1.docx (Mar 27, 2026). It defines per-module input feature columns, type constraints, train/val/test temporal split rules (70/15/15), class-imbalance handling, label generation strategy from the V1 schema's anomaly_events table, and 8 data-quality SLAs (no timestamp gaps, value ranges in [0,1], no future leakage, etc.). Per the canonical-wiring rule, this is not adopted until reviewed.

15. Routing & Decision Authority

Canonical Design Intent

Routing is the only decision the LB makes per request. Every input that influences routing is pre-computed off the request path. The hierarchy below is the safety contract that lets RL be trusted at all: anomaly always constrains, policy can always short-circuit, RL is advisory inside the bounds set by the first two, and classical is always available as the deterministic floor. This order is law (principle 9).

Canonical routing decision hierarchy

The Decision Layer is a coordinated multi-intelligence controller — not an algorithm selector. Engines run in parallel on the same telemetry; their outputs are merged through a strict hierarchy enforced by the LB Sidecar:

  1. Anomaly constraints — remove or down-weight UNHEALTHY backends from the action space. Always applied first; cannot be overridden.
  2. Active policy constraints — apply safe_mode, min_backends/max_backends limits, SLO targets. Policy can short-circuit RL but cannot override anomaly exclusion.
  3. RL routing scores — when available and permitted (mode=active), set NGINX upstream weights from RoutingRecommendation.server_rankings.
  4. Classical fallback — round-robin / least-connections always available if RL is missing, warming up, or stale.
  5. Forecasts — drive scaling decisions, not per-request routing.

Architectural meaning of the hierarchy

LevelWhy it sits at this position
Anomaly firstHard safety constraint. Routing to a known-bad backend has unbounded cost (timeouts, errors, cascading failure). The cost of incorrect exclusion (slightly underutilizing a borderline backend) is bounded.
Policy secondThe operator's veto. safe_mode is the human-in-the-loop kill switch; it must dominate any AI signal. But the operator did not cause the bad health, so anomaly remains in force.
RL thirdRL is an optimization within already-safe bounds. It is allowed to influence weights, never to authorize routing to an excluded backend or to cross policy limits. RL operates on a curated action space, not the raw set of backends.
Classical floorDeterministic, well-understood, dependency-free. If everything else fails or is silent, the LB serves traffic correctly. Classical is also the comparison baseline for benchmarks — it is never deprecated.
Forecasts off the routing pathForecast error must not corrupt routing fairness. A bad prediction should at worst cause poor scaling, never a single bad request decision.

What may influence request routing — and what must not

SourceAllowed to influence routing?Why
Anomaly status (current cycle)✅ Yes — required first inputHard safety; principle 9.
Policy (current snapshot)✅ Yes — bounds RL and may short-circuitOperator authority.
RL recommendation (current cycle)✅ Yes — only when mode=active and within policyAdaptive optimization, but constrained.
Classical state (round-robin counter)✅ Yes — default behaviorThe deterministic floor.
Forecast❌ NoForecast belongs to scaling, not routing. Forecast error must not move per-request decisions.
Raw TimescaleDB query result❌ NoData plane never reaches into the metrics store.
External request headers (besides X-Forwarded)❌ NoNo client-controlled routing inputs.
Per-client identity❌ No (in prototype scope)No session affinity; stateless principle 7.
Important: Forecasts are intentionally not in the routing decision path. They influence the autoscaler (capacity) but never per-request server selection. This separation prevents prediction error from corrupting routing fairness.

Policy overrides

FieldEffect
safe_mode: trueRL routing immediately disabled; weights reset to equal; LB falls back to round-robin. Anomaly constraints still apply.
operating_mode: classicalRL recommendations ignored; pure classical routing.
operating_mode: hybridDefault. Anomaly + RL + classical fallback per the hierarchy.
operating_mode: learningRL fully drives routing with controlled exploration. Operator-only mode for benchmarking.

Autoscaling interactions

  1. Forecaster predicts predicted_rps for next horizon (60s/5min).
  2. Autoscaler compares to current_backends × per_instance_capacity_rps.
  3. If under capacity → scale_up(1) bounded by max_backends.
  4. If over capacity for cooldown duration → scale_in(1) bounded by min_backends.
  5. New backend registers; LB picks it up via dynamic upstream update or NGINX reload.

What's used for what

Engine outputDrives routing?Drives scaling?
Anomaly constraints✅ Yes (highest priority)❌ No (but may trigger replacement of failed instance)
RL recommendation✅ Yes (when active)❌ No
Classical algorithm✅ Yes (fallback)❌ No
Forecast❌ No (by design)✅ Yes (proactive scaling)
Telemetry (current load)❌ Indirectly (anomaly + RL state)✅ Yes (reactive scaling fallback)
Policy✅ Constrains routing✅ Sets min/max

Review Checklist — Routing & Decision

  • Does the LB Sidecar enforce all levels of the hierarchy in order, or does any level live inside an individual engine?
  • Is anomaly exclusion applied before any RL weight is multiplied in?
  • Does safe_mode immediately reset to equal weights and stop reading RL?
  • Does any forecast value reach the LB Sidecar? (Must be no.)
  • Is the classical fallback testable in isolation (RL absent, anomaly absent)?
  • Are routing decisions auditable — can we reconstruct from logs why a given backend was chosen at a given time?
  • Does any service other than the Sidecar mutate nginx.conf? (Must be no.)

16. Control Plane vs Data Plane

Canonical Design Intent

The deepest architectural commitment in SmartLoad. The data plane serves traffic; the control plane decides how traffic should be served. They communicate only through the LB Sidecar's config-write boundary. Mixing the two is the single biggest risk to a middleware project; this section is the rule that prevents that mixing.

Definition

PlaneMembersJobAllowed to doForbidden from doing
Data plane NGINX, backend pool Receive client requests, forward to a backend, return responses, append a JSON access log line per request. Read its loaded config; respond to graceful reload; passive health checks; write access log to stdout + shared volume. Make any network call beyond the backend it's forwarding to. Read TimescaleDB. Read Redis. Connect to the OTel Collector. Block on policy or AI service.
Control plane lb-otel-shipper, Telemetry Service, Anomaly Detector, Forecasting Engine, RL Engine, Autoscaler, Policy Manager, LB Sidecar Observe the system, decide on routing constraints / weights / capacity, push the resulting config into the data plane. Read TimescaleDB, publish/subscribe Redis, call REST endpoints, call Docker SDK, regenerate NGINX config files, tail data-plane log files. Sit on the request path. Block request flow. Be a single point of failure for traffic serving.

The boundary: LB Sidecar as config-writer

The Sidecar is the only process that crosses the planes. It sits in the control plane (subscribes to Redis, reads policy) and writes a single artifact (nginx.conf) that the data plane consumes. Once the file is written and nginx -s reload is signaled, the control plane retreats — NGINX continues serving on its own.

Why this split matters

  • Latency isolation. Slowness in the control plane (e.g., a stuck Redis subscriber) cannot slow down request serving.
  • Failure isolation. A crashed RL engine, a stalled anomaly detector, or a broken Redis instance does not stop traffic.
  • Replaceable intelligence. Engines can be swapped, redeployed, or scaled independently. The data plane sees only the resulting config.
  • Operability. Operators can disable the entire control plane (safe_mode + stop AI services) and the data plane still serves traffic with the last good config.

Common violations to refuse

  • An LB plugin that calls a Python service per request to ask which backend to pick.
  • An RL engine that proxies traffic itself ("RL gateway") instead of emitting a recommendation for the LB.
  • A "fast path" that lets one engine modify NGINX config directly, bypassing the Sidecar.
  • Telemetry collection that synchronously confirms persistence to TimescaleDB before returning a response (couples write throughput to request latency).
  • Policy reads that block the request path until the latest version is fetched.
  • Sidecar logic that performs ML inference (the Sidecar must be a thin merge + render function only).

Mapping every component to a plane

ComponentPlaneBoundary contract
NGINXDataReads nginx.conf; appends per-request JSON access log to stdout + shared volume. No outbound network calls beyond backends.
lb-otel-shipper (sidecar)ControlTails NGINX's shared-volume access log; emits OTLP/HTTP-JSON to OTel Collector. Drops on collector outage. Failure does not affect the request path.
OTel CollectorControlReceives OTLP from the shipper (and any future producer); forwards to the Telemetry Service.
Telemetry ServiceControlWrites metrics; serves GET /api/v1/metrics.
Anomaly DetectorControlReads metrics; publishes smartload.anomaly.
Forecasting EngineControlReads metrics; publishes smartload.forecast.
RL EngineControlReads metrics; publishes smartload.routing.
AutoscalerControlReads forecasts; calls Docker SDK; writes scaling_events.
Policy ManagerControlOwns policy.yaml; serves REST; publishes smartload.policy.
LB SidecarControl (writes to data plane)The only process that mutates data-plane config.
TimescaleDBControl storagePersistent state for the control plane only.
RedisControl busPub/Sub between control-plane services.
Grafana / PrometheusControl (read-only)Operator visibility into both planes; never on the request path.

Review Checklist — Plane Discipline

  • Can every component be unambiguously labeled "data plane" or "control plane"? Ambiguous components must be split.
  • Can the data plane serve traffic with the entire control plane stopped (last-known-good config)?
  • Does any data-plane component make a network call to any control-plane component on the request path? If yes, this is a violation.
  • Is the LB Sidecar the only component that writes nginx.conf or signals NGINX?
  • Does any control-plane component proxy or transform actual request bytes? If yes, redesign.
  • Do failure modes preserve plane isolation (a control-plane crash never blocks a request)?

Original 10-sprint implementation-first plan (superseded 2026-04-28)

Source: SDP/ImplementationPlan/Implementation_Plan.pdf + SDP/integration_first_report.tex

  • S1 (Feb 1–14): Repo setup, CI/CD, baseline arch
  • S2 (Feb 15–28): NGINX, health checks, basic telemetry
  • S3 (Mar 1–14): OTel pipeline, TimescaleDB, Grafana
  • S4 (Mar 15–28): Datasets, RL simulation env
  • S5 (Apr 1–14): Anomaly Detection microservice
  • S6 (Apr 15–28): Forecasting + Autoscaler
  • S7 (May 1–14): RL Engine shadow → production
  • S8 (May 15–28): Policy Manager + final integration
  • S9 (Jun 1–14): System testing + benchmarking
  • S10 (Jun 15–30): Deployment + final docs + presentation

Why superseded: 7 weeks of building in isolation before any service ever talked to another. Integration in Sprint 8 would have caused cascading rework. The advisor directive (Apr 2026) was "wire before you implement."

Test pyramid

LevelStatusScope
Unit tests (ALL.1)Phase 3APer-module logic. Tasneem: autoscaler decisions, policy API, routing hierarchy. Nada: anomaly detector, forecasting, RL agent. Rghda: data loaders, preprocessors.
Integration — Phase 0 connectivityDONE 33/33tests/integration/test_pipeline_health.py — 7 layers from Communications Channel Diagram.
Integration — Phase 3A scenarios (T2.3)Phase 3A3 scenarios: anomaly reroute, forecast scale-out, safe-mode override.
Performance benchmarks (ALL.2)Phase 3AB1 steady, B2 burst, B3 degraded backend.
Fault injectionPhase 3AKill RL engine → expect classical fallback. Disable forecasting → expect reactive autoscaling.
Shadow vs active comparisonPhase 2 N1.3 + N2.5RL recommendations logged in shadow; latency/throughput compared after switch.

Evaluation methodology

  • Run benchmarks on the same compose stack to keep environment constant.
  • For every benchmark, record P50/P95/P99 latency, throughput (RPS), error rate, active backend count over time, and SmartLoad component CPU/memory.
  • Each comparison (RL vs round-robin, forecast vs reactive autoscaling) uses identical workload generators and identical seed where possible.
  • Final report includes Grafana screenshots + tables for every KPI vs target.

Verification commands

# Spot-check service health
curl http://localhost:8081/health   # → {redis: true, timescaledb: true}
curl http://localhost:8086/api/v1/policy

# Watch the control bus
docker compose exec redis redis-cli psubscribe 'smartload.*'

# Watch a scenario unfold
docker compose run -d -e RESPONSE_DELAY_MS=2000 --name slow-backend test-backend
docker compose logs -f anomaly-detector

Reproducibility

  • docker compose down --volumes --rmi all wipes everything; up --build -d recreates the stack identically.
  • Schema is recreated from infrastructure/timescaledb/init.sql on first DB start (mounted to /docker-entrypoint-initdb.d/).
  • Redis intentionally has no persistence — restart is always a clean slate.

Future deployment options (out of scope)

  • Kubernetes via Helm charts (autoscaler talks to k8s API instead of Docker SDK).
  • AWS EC2 + RDS for managed TimescaleDB.
  • Cloud-native CI/CD with auto-deploy on tag.

21. Architecture Review Checklists

Canonical Design Intent

This section is the master review tool for SmartLoad. Every checklist below is derived from the System Design Principles (§4) and the section it serves. Use these checklists when reviewing teammate PRs, when evaluating proposed redesigns, when prompting an AI agent to generate code, or when comparing a real implementation to the canonical design.

How to use these checklists

  • For PR review. Pick the checklist that matches the area touched (routing, storage, contract, etc.). A failed item is not necessarily a blocker — but it must either be fixed or recorded as a knowing tradeoff in the PR description.
  • For proposed designs. Run the relevant checklists before code is written. A design that fails three or more items is a redesign, not a refinement.
  • For AI prompting. When asking an AI agent to implement a feature, paste the relevant checklist into the prompt and ask the agent to address each item explicitly.
  • For ongoing audit. Periodically (every release / sprint), walk every section's checklist and update §18 Build Status with what fails today.

Whole-system review checklist

Apply this when reviewing any non-trivial change.

  • Plane discipline. Does the change preserve data-plane / control-plane separation (§16)?
  • Single writer. Does the change introduce a second writer to any data class (§9)?
  • Contract surface. Is every cross-service interaction a typed contract (Redis, REST, or SQL constant) (§11)?
  • Graceful degradation. Does the change preserve the ability to fall back to classical routing (§4 principle 4)?
  • Routing hierarchy. If the change touches routing, does it preserve the anomaly→policy→RL→classical order (§15)?
  • Replaceable engines. Could the affected engine be swapped to a different algorithm without touching consumers (§4 principle 6)?
  • Observable. Does the change emit structured logs and OTel metrics? Does it have a meaningful /health?
  • Versionable. Are field additions backward-compatible? Are removals or renames coordinated through versioning?
  • Stateless on the request path. Does the change introduce per-request state in NGINX or the Sidecar (§4 principle 7)?
  • Bounded growth. Are new persistent stores covered by retention or eviction?

Routing change review checklist

  • Are anomaly constraints applied before any RL weight?
  • Is safe_mode immediate and effective on this code path?
  • Is the LB Sidecar the only writer of nginx.conf?
  • Does the change preserve classical routing as the dependency-free fallback?
  • Does the change keep forecast values out of routing decisions?
  • Is the routing decision auditable from logs?

Engine (anomaly / forecasting / RL) review checklist

  • Does the engine read only through canonical SQL constants (queries.py)?
  • Does the engine publish only its own canonical channel?
  • Does the engine subscribe to smartload.policy and react to safe_mode?
  • Does the runtime container exclude training data and training code?
  • Does the engine have a fallback for missing model files (skeleton mode)?
  • Are model artifacts loaded once at startup, not per cycle?
  • Is the cycle latency bounded and observable (metric per cycle)?

Storage / schema review checklist

  • Is the storage class right for the access pattern (time-series vs ephemeral vs versioned)?
  • Is there exactly one writer per table / channel / file?
  • Are indexes informed by the canonical query patterns?
  • Are init.sql + queries.py + tests updated together?
  • Is retention or eviction defined?
  • Does the design tolerate Redis restart?

Contract / interface review checklist

  • Is every Redis message a typed dataclass in contracts.py?
  • Is every consumer tolerant of missing or stale messages?
  • Does the change rename or remove a field? If yes, is it versioned?
  • Are REST errors shaped like the canonical Problem Details target?
  • Is every /health actually testing dependencies?

Deployment / infrastructure review checklist

  • Is every service containerized with a single Dockerfile and reproducible build?
  • Is every dependency declared explicitly (no implicit volume mounts)?
  • Does docker-compose up from a clean clone produce a green stack?
  • Is every service's image tagged and pushed by CI?
  • Are all secrets sourced from config/.env (never committed)?
  • Can the stack survive a restart of any single container?

Testing & verification review checklist

  • Does the change have unit tests for pure logic?
  • Does it have an integration test that exercises Redis + TimescaleDB end-to-end?
  • Are KPIs measurable from existing telemetry (no new instrumentation needed)?
  • Can the change be benchmarked against B1 (steady), B2 (burst), B3 (degraded backend)?
  • Are negative cases covered (missing model, dead Redis, slow backend)?

"Is this still middleware?" smell test

If too many of these are true, the project is drifting away from middleware identity:

  • The LB or Sidecar is starting to know about specific user-facing features.
  • An engine is starting to handle requests directly.
  • The control plane is becoming a single point of failure for traffic.
  • Operators must restart the LB to apply policy changes.
  • Engines have started importing each other's modules.
  • Database schema has started encoding business logic instead of telemetry.
  • Tests are stuck behind elaborate mocks that don't reflect the real Redis / TimescaleDB.

v1.0.7ab — 2026-06-11 — Isolation Forest anomaly engine shipped (#101) + empirical calibration finding

AspectWhat changed / mergedResolved by
N2.1 Isolation Forest engine landed PR #158 (Nada) trains scikit-learn IsolationForest on SMD (Server Machine Dataset / OmniAnomaly, real per-timestep test_label/ ground truth) and ships the bundle at services/anomaly-detector/models/isolation_forest.pkl. Search over (machine set, SMD dim → feature mapping, rolling window, contamination) selected machine-1-1 + machine-1-6, dim1 → latency family, dim15 → error_rate, rolling window 5, contamination=0.005 → test F1 = 0.8012 (precision 0.8681, recall 0.7439) against real SMD holdout — PASS of the > 0.80 KPI gate. This release

Marked @pytest.mark.slow; skipped with explicit reason when the stack is configured with a different engine. This release
Anomaly-engine comparison bench — empirical calibration finding experiments/anomaly-engine-bench/run.py sweeps a synthetic grid of (latency_ms × error_rate) and scores each cell with both engines. Headline result on the first 12×12 sweep: agreement rate = 25.0% (36/144 cells); the trained model said healthy on 107 of 108 cells where the threshold rule said unhealthy. This quantifies the domain-adaptation caveat in engines/isolation_forest/README.md: the production_scaler (fit on MST-2021 features as a proxy for live telemetry) maps real-millisecond latency into a space where the SMD-trained model's outlier boundary doesn't trigger. The model passes its own training-distribution gate (F1 = 0.8012 on SMD holdout) but is currently under-reacting at production scales. Recommendation: ANOMALY_ENGINE=threshold remains the compose default; a production-scale re-tuning of production_scaler (or a re-fit on real production telemetry once labeled traces exist) is tracked as #165 (root-cause analysis in the issue body — SMD-trained model lives in a different mean-0/std-1 coordinate system than the one production_scaler maps real-ms inputs into; standardisation pulls inputs toward the origin regardless of severity, so the model sees almost every production input as "near the training cluster"). Acceptance: bench agreement rate ≥ 80% on the same 12×12 sweep + tests/integration/test_isolation_forest_live_stack.py passes. §35.6 retains the LSTM-AE architectural-alternative deferral as the fallback path if the re-calibration approach (#165) fails to meet the criteria. This release
Doc sync docs/PROJECT_STATE.md closes #101 in the sprint table (S4 carry-forward 6 → 5; S5 active 3 → 2) and records the empirical calibration finding inline. docs/PROJECT_WALKTHROUGH.md §4.1 (anomaly-detector) gets a one-paragraph mention of the bench harness and the live-stack test as the new executable specs. New handle_scale() in runloop.py Pure-Python handler returns a new ScaleOutcome dataclass (applied, backend_count, action, mechanism, error). Synthesises an equal-weight upstream map from live_backends and calls adapter.set_upstream_weights(weights). The adapter's existing idempotency (if backend_weights == self._weights: return) means a scale event whose resulting pool matches upstream.conf becomes a free check — no double-rewrite, no spurious nginx -s reload. Anomaly-excluded backends in live_backends stay rendered as server ... down; via the adapter's existing exclusion path. Refuses to write an empty upstream.conf as the safety pin: if the docker query came back empty (daemon unreachable), the previous adapter state stands. Reads only action + mechanism from the payload — not instance_count: the canonical truth is the running-container query, not the autoscaler's intent. This release

v1.0.7y — 2026-06-10 — Decision-plane catch-all + /health liveness close §35.8-adjacent silent-thread bug (#163)

AspectWhat changed / mergedResolved by
Catch-all wraps every decision-plane run loop's iteration body Five services touched: services/forecasting/app.py, services/anomaly-detector/app.py, services/rl-engine/app.py, services/autoscaler/app.py, services/lb-sidecar/app.py. Each _run_loop / control_loop now wraps its while not stop_event.is_set(): body in try: ... except Exception as exc: log + stop_event.wait(LOOP_RECOVERY_BACKOFF_SECONDS) + continue. A single iteration's unexpected exception (psycopg2 OperationalError outside the inner try, Redis dropping mid-message, Docker SDK timeout, parse error) can no longer kill the daemon thread. The stop_event.wait() instead of time.sleep() means SIGTERM during the back-off still terminates the loop cleanly. This release
/health flips to degraded when the loop has not ticked recently New LIVENESS_STALE_SECONDS = 5 × POLL_INTERVAL_SECONDS constant per service. /health reads the latest "loop ticked" monotonic value; if older than the threshold, the response flips to {status: "degraded", code: 503, loop_stale: true, errors: [...]} regardless of whether Redis + TimescaleDB ping pass. Three services reuse their existing _last_inference_monotonic; autoscaler + lb-sidecar gain a new _last_loop_tick_monotonic updated at the top of every iteration (their existing per-action / per-routing-applied monotonics only update on real decisions and aren't liveness signals). This release
Tests Seventeen new integration tests under tests/integration/:
test_loop_liveness.py — 15 tests, 5 services × 3 scenarios (stale → degraded 503, fresh → ok 200, startup-None → not flagged). Uses a sys.modules-flushing import helper to isolate the five service apps in one pytest session despite the shared app / engine_base module names.
test_loop_catchall.py — 2 tests, forecasting as the representative pattern. Drives _run_loop against a raising _inference_cycle + a fake pubsub/redis/conn stack, asserts the loop runs at least twice (first iteration raises, second succeeds) and that stop_event still terminates cleanly. The second test covers the persistent-failure case (every iteration raises) and asserts ≥5 iterations under a 1 s window with 0.05 s backoff. 		This release
Adaptive-bench narrative impact The two acceptance gates blocked-by-#163 in §34.6 ("Pool grew during B", "Pool shrank during D") now have half the binding constraint cleared. With #163 closed, a re-run no longer requires a pre-flight docker compose restart dance to wake up the decision plane — the catch-all keeps the loops alive past any transient failure, and the liveness check makes a true death visible immediately. The second blocker (#164) is independent and still S5 high; once both close, the adaptive-bench produces affirmative gate strings on the next run. This release

v1.0.7v — 2026-06-09 — Adaptive-bench dynamic-pool foundation (#155 Round 1)

AspectWhat changed / mergedResolved by
Autoscaler cluster_client.py gains a second lifecycle pair: provision() / decommission() The existing start() / stop() pair toggles the pre-provisioned compose pool (the #148 routing bench path, unchanged). The new pair creates and destroys containers via the Docker SDK, gated by the AUTOSCALER_PROVISIONING_ENABLED env var (default false). Containers carry both com.docker.compose.service=test-backend (so get_backend_count() sees them) and a new smartload.dynamic=true label — the dynamic label is the safety contract that prevents decommission() from ever tearing down a compose-provisioned container. Index allocation picks the lowest unused integer ≥ 1 over existing labelled containers (1, 2, 4 → picks 3), so decommissioning a middle backend frees its slot for re-use rather than monotonically inflating the index. The Docker SDK containers.run call injects the same healthcheck spec compose configures on the test-backend service so dynamic backends report healthy state via the same State.Health.Status field the poller reads. This release
scale_out() / scale_in() now return (name, mechanism) tuples scale_out() returns (name, "start") when a stopped labelled container exists (cheap path) and (name, "provision") when provisioning runs. scale_in() returns (name, "decommission") when a dynamic container is running (preferred so the pool returns to its compose floor cleanly) and (name, "stop") when it falls through to the legacy path. None means nothing actuated. app.py::apply_decision and the manual POST /api/v1/scale endpoint unpack the tuple; the ScalingEvent.mechanism field carries the lifecycle path on the wire, and the reason text gains a textual marker like "... [provision]" for searchable audit. scaling_events schema unchanged — the existing action column still carries scale_out / scale_in, no migration needed. This release
Risk-1 de-risk: healthcheck-then-announce sequence provision() polls container.attrs["State"]["Health"]["Status"] until healthy (30 s default timeout, configurable via AUTOSCALER_PROVISIONING_HEALTHCHECK_TIMEOUT_SECONDS) before returning a non-None name. Only after the return does apply_decision() publish the ScalingEvent envelope, write the scaling_events row, and bump the cooldown clock. This prevents the provision-then-announce race where the lb-sidecar would rewrite upstream.conf with a hostname Docker DNS hasn't propagated yet — nginx -s reload would otherwise crash with host not found in upstream (the exact failure mode observed in the v1.0.7r LB restart loop). The integration test test_provision_returns_only_after_healthy asserts the ordering by re-querying State.Health.Status after provision() returns. This release
lb-sidecar dynamic backend discovery services/lb-sidecar/runloop.py gains discover_all_backends(docker_client, seed_backends) — queries containers labelled com.docker.compose.service=test-backend with status=running, returns a sorted name:port list. Used by both handle_routing (line ~279 — replaces the static all_backends in the merged-weights init) and handle_policy (line ~366 — safe-mode equal-weight reset path; the Plan-agent's catch). A 2 s TTL cache _BackendNameCache guards against per-message hammering of the Docker daemon; invalidate_backend_discovery_cache() is called on every Redis message in app.py so a freshly-provisioned backend is visible on the next decision rather than waiting up to TTL seconds. ALL_BACKENDS env var is now a cold-boot seed only — used until Docker is reachable and as a safety net on transient daemon errors. This release
Risk-3 de-risk: NGINX adapter hostname pre-flight services/shared/lb_adapters/nginx/__init__.py — _render_and_reload() now calls socket.gethostbyname() on every active backend hostname before writing upstream.conf. If any host fails to resolve, the reload is deferred this cycle and the next message re-triggers; the warning log line names the unresolved hosts. NGINX resolves upstream hostnames at config-load time, so without the pre-flight a freshly-provisioned backend would crash the reload (the exact failure observed in the v1.0.7r LB restart loop). The NginxAdapter constructor gains a dns_preflight: bool = True kwarg so unit tests using synthetic backend names (b1:8080, a:8080) can disable the check — production keeps it on by default. An autouse fixture tests/unit/lb-sidecar/conftest.py patches socket.gethostbyname so existing tests pass unchanged. This release
ScalingEvent.mechanism forward-compat field services/shared/contracts.py — adds optional mechanism: str | None = None to ScalingEvent with documented values "start" | "provision" | "stop" | "decommission". Forward-compat additive change — older subscribers (operator-UI live engines feed, audit page) ignore the field; the Round-2 adaptive-bench analysis pipeline reads it to plot toggle vs create/destroy activity separately. Envelope version is unchanged because the change is purely additive. This release
Feature flag (#148 backwards-compat preserved) docker-compose.yml autoscaler service gains four env vars — AUTOSCALER_PROVISIONING_ENABLED (default false), AUTOSCALER_PROVISIONING_IMAGE (default smartload-test-backend:latest), AUTOSCALER_PROVISIONING_NETWORK (default smartload_smartload-net), AUTOSCALER_PROVISIONING_HEALTHCHECK_TIMEOUT_SECONDS (default 30). The Round-2 adaptive-bench orchestrator flips ENABLED=true at preflight via an env-file and --force-recreate autoscaler; the standing dev stack and the #148 routing bench keep the toggle OFF and pay no extra cost. config/policy.yaml gains a YAML comment block documenting the env vars (deployment-time concern, not a runtime policy knob, so kept out of the policy-manager API surface). v1.0.7t — 2026-06-04 — Benchmark harness: backend heterogeneity + latency-spike anomaly + honest first finding (#148)

AspectWhat changed / mergedResolved by
test-backend gains a runtime-overridable latency endpoint test-backends/app.js grows a POST /_admin/delay {ms: N} route + a matching GET for inspection. The endpoint sets a process-local runtimeDelayMs that is added to every GET / + GET /health response. Validated input (0 ≤ ms ≤ 10000); response echoes {server, is_slow_host, static_baseline_ms, runtime_override_ms, effective_ms} for diagnostics. Two static knobs landed alongside but are intentionally NOT wired in compose: SLOW_HOSTNAME + SLOW_DELAY_MS — Docker scaled services give every replica the same short-ID kernel hostname, so a hostname-match doesn't cleanly identify one of N replicas. Knobs documented as aspirational; the runtime-override endpoint is the working mechanism. This release
Benchmark harness: real heterogeneity + latency-spike anomaly experiments/baseline-vs-smartload/scripts/run_experiment.sh now POSTs {ms: 15} to backend-1 at the start of each side (via docker exec against the in-container /_admin/delay) and clears it at tear-down. Mid-run, the anomaly switches from docker pause (binary freeze that NGINX's max_fails=3 fail_timeout=10s catches via passive checks) to POST {ms: 200} — a latency spike where requests still complete (no 5xx, no timeout) so NGINX's passive health checks never trip. This is the case where SmartLoad's lb-sidecar should win: it reacts to the published AnomalyEvent within seconds, while baseline RR keeps dispatching 1/5 of traffic to a slow backend indefinitely. This release
SHORT validation: harness exposes heterogeneity correctly Ran SHORT=1 bash run_experiment.sh against the live stack. Baseline aggregate p95 jumped from 6 ms (v1.0.7r, no heterogeneity) to 41 ms, confirming backend-1's +15 ms baseline + 200 ms anomaly delay is being eaten by 1/5 of RR's traffic exactly as designed. Per-phase breakdown shows the slowdown is concentrated in A_hold (steady-state heterogeneity), B_anomaly (anomaly window), and C_sustain (steady-state after recovery) — phases where backend-1 is slow. The harness mechanically works as intended. This release
Honest first finding: PPO doesn't yet beat RR on the new workload Per-phase p95 comparison: baseline=14/42/44/43 ms; smartload=23/41/50/44 ms (A_ramp / A_hold / B_anomaly / C_sustain). SmartLoad is tied or slightly worse in every phase. Smartload's max latency was 3082 ms (vs baseline's 150 ms) — that's the lb-sidecar's NGINX reload causing brief connection blips when it rewrites upstream.conf during the anomaly. The lb-sidecar mechanism costs latency during reload; with the currently-trained PPO model (Alibaba traces — homogeneous latencies, see §22 v1.0.7i) it doesn't earn that cost back via better steady-state routing. The result confirms the earlier reading: training-data heterogeneity (Rghda's retraining workstream) is the binding constraint for SmartLoad to beat RR in this scenario. The harness changes themselves are correct — they faithfully expose the truth. This release
What this release does NOT claim Does not claim SmartLoad beats baseline RR on this benchmark — it does not, with the current model. Does not move the §25 evidence paragraph from the v1.0.7r-still-owed list. Does not close #148 — the third acceptance gate (clear delta on ≥3 of 5 metrics) is still red, and ticking it depends on the retrained PPO model. What this release DOES: lock in the right benchmark *methodology* (heterogeneity + slow-anomaly that NGINX can't passively catch) so the post-retraining run produces meaningful before/after numbers. This release

3-phase Locust load profile + pause-based anomaly New experiments/baseline-vs-smartload/locust/locustfile.py implements a LoadTestShape with three phases: A — steady ramp (0 → RAMP_USERS over RAMP_SECS — exercises forecast-driven scale-out); B — anomaly injection (RAMP_USERS held, backend-1 frozen via docker pause + synthetic AnomalyEvent posted on /api/v1/isolate at ANOMALY_AT_SECS); C — sustained tail (RAMP_USERS held until SUSTAIN_END_SECS). Each request is tagged with the current phase via the request name so post-run analysis can slice per-phase latency distributions. SHORT=1 compresses to a 2-min-per-side harness validation profile. Orchestration + plotting scripts/run_experiment.sh sequences per-side: stop the standing traffic-simulator (to avoid polluting the load shape) → apply the env-file + recreate decision-plane services → wait for /api/v1/status to be ready → schedule the background anomaly-inject coroutine → run a one-shot locust container on smartload_smartload-net with --csv-full-history → snapshot pre/post /api/v1/status + Prometheus + scaling audit → tear down. scripts/plot_results.py reads the run's CSVs and emits 6 PNGs (RPS over time, p50/p95/p99 stacked, failure rate, cumulative requests, per-phase p95 bar chart, anomaly-window recovery zoom) plus a SUMMARY.md with the delta one-liner. Both sides produced full locust CSVs (38,098 baseline / 38,812 smartload requests over 2 min each at ~320 RPS). Median latency is similar between sides (smartload adds ~1 ms in p95 from sidecar overhead at SHORT scale), but the long-tail signal is already visible — 99.99%-percentile latency is ~6× lower on smartload (420 ms vs 2700 ms during the anomaly window). v1.0.7p — 2026-06-03 — policy-manager: strict POST body schema (#152 fix)

AspectWhat changed / mergedResolved by
Strict unknown-field rejection on POST /api/v1/policy Closes #152. validate_updates() in services/policy-manager/validation.py now rejects any POST-body key outside the canonical schema with HTTP 400 + the offending field name, before the body ever reaches the merge or the atomic YAML write. The repro that originally surfaced the bug — an actor field in the body — now returns {"error":"unknown field(s) in POST body: ['actor'] — not in the canonical policy schema","field":"actor"} with status 400. Header-only fields (X-Actor) and server-managed envelope fields (policy_version, timestamp, changed_fields) can no longer leak into config/policy.yaml. This release
Read-modify-write echo allowance Clients doing GET /api/v1/policy → modify → POST /api/v1/policy may echo the server-managed envelope fields back in the body without a 400. policy_version, timestamp, and changed_fields are accepted but silently stripped before the merge — the server rewrites policy_version monotonically on every write regardless, and the published envelope's timestamp is always set by contracts._now_iso() at publish time. Net effect: SDK callers that pass through a GET response unmodified still work; unknown fields like actor still get a clear 400. This release
Module constants exported CANONICAL_POLICY_FIELDS (frozenset of the 12 keys in _FIELD_CHECKS) is now a public module constant in validation.py — consumers (e.g. the SDK PolicyClient, future schema-introspection endpoints) can import it as the single source of truth for "what fields can a POST body contain". The previous behaviour where validate_field() silently accepted any key is preserved at the field-rule layer for backwards-compat with startup-time policy loads; the strict gate lives only in validate_updates(). This release
Test coverage New tests/unit/policy-manager/test_validation.py with 21 unit tests across 5 classes: TestValidateField (per-field shape checks), TestMergedInvariants (cross-field min_backends ≤ max_backends), TestUnknownFieldsRejected (the #152 bug repro + typo + multi-unknown + non-mutation of existing on failure), TestServerManagedEchoStrip (each of the three echo fields + a server-only body), and TestHappyPath (full canonical body, partial body, empty body, non-dict rejection, drift guard between CANONICAL_POLICY_FIELDS and _FIELD_CHECKS keys). v1.0.7j — 2026-05-29 — Live Engines SDK (#121 session 2, SDK leg)

AspectWhat changed / mergedResolved by
SDK Live Engines surface — three methods + four convenience aliases New clients/python/smartload_client/engines.py implements EnginesClient:
  • client.engines.snapshot() — synchronous fan-out via the operator-UI BFF /api/ui/engines/snapshot; returns {services, channels, recent}.
