30.3 Shipped schema (full DDL reference)

metrics — wide-narrow telemetry rows

CREATE TABLE metrics (
    time         TIMESTAMPTZ      NOT NULL,
    service      TEXT             NOT NULL,
    instance     TEXT             NOT NULL,
    metric_name  TEXT             NOT NULL,
    value        DOUBLE PRECISION NOT NULL
);
SELECT create_hypertable('metrics', 'time');

CREATE INDEX metrics_service_instance_time ON metrics (service, instance, time DESC);
CREATE INDEX metrics_metric_name_time       ON metrics (metric_name, time DESC);

-- Continuous aggregate — minute-resolution roll-up for cheap dashboard queries.
-- Engines that need sub-minute resolution still read raw `metrics`.
CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW metrics_1min
WITH (timescaledb.continuous, timescaledb.materialized_only = true) AS
  SELECT
    time_bucket('1 minute', time) AS bucket,
    service, instance, metric_name,
    AVG(value) AS avg_value,
    MAX(value) AS max_value,
    MIN(value) AS min_value,
    SUM(value) AS sum_value,
    COUNT(*)   AS sample_count
  FROM metrics
  GROUP BY bucket, service, instance, metric_name;

SELECT add_continuous_aggregate_policy('metrics_1min',
    start_offset      => INTERVAL '1 hour',
    end_offset        => INTERVAL '2 minutes',
    schedule_interval => INTERVAL '1 minute');

SELECT add_retention_policy('metrics', INTERVAL '7 days');

Why wide-narrow: one row per metric reading instead of one column per metric. Adding a new metric kind never requires ALTER TABLE. Cost: queries that fetch many metric types do their own pivoting (see RL_STATE_QUERY in queries.py). Accepted trade-off for forward-compatibility.

Why no PERCENTILE_CONT in the CAGG: percentile computation inside continuous aggregates rejects refreshes — engines that need P95 read raw metrics directly. The CAGG carries first-moment stats only. Documented in infrastructure/timescaledb/init.sql.

backend_health — anomaly-detector health flags

CREATE TABLE backend_health (
    time        TIMESTAMPTZ      NOT NULL,
    backend_id  TEXT             NOT NULL,
    status      TEXT             NOT NULL,   -- "healthy" | "degraded" | "unhealthy"
    score       DOUBLE PRECISION NOT NULL    -- anomaly score; higher = worse
);
SELECT create_hypertable('backend_health', 'time');

CREATE INDEX backend_health_backend_id_time ON backend_health (backend_id, time DESC);

SELECT add_retention_policy('backend_health', INTERVAL '30 days');

Latest health per backend is read via the SELECT DISTINCT ON pattern (see BACKEND_HEALTH_QUERY in queries.py).

scaling_events — autoscaler audit + operator UI feed

CREATE TABLE scaling_events (
    time           TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL,
    action         TEXT        NOT NULL,   -- "scale_out" | "scale_in"
    instance_count INT         NOT NULL,   -- resulting backend count
    reason         TEXT        NOT NULL    -- human-readable rationale
);
SELECT create_hypertable('scaling_events', 'time');

SELECT add_retention_policy('scaling_events', INTERVAL '90 days');

policy_changes — policy-manager audit

CREATE TABLE policy_changes (
    time           TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL,
    policy_version INT         NOT NULL,
    field          TEXT        NOT NULL,    -- the changed field's name
    old_value      TEXT,                    -- JSON-encoded previous value (NULL on first write)
    new_value      TEXT        NOT NULL,    -- JSON-encoded new value
    actor          TEXT        NOT NULL     -- from X-Actor header (default "anonymous")
);
SELECT create_hypertable('policy_changes', 'time');

One row per changed field per accepted POST. The audit endpoint (§26 §26.4) JSON-decodes old_value / new_value before serving so callers receive native values.

30.4 Retention policies — summary

HypertableRetention windowRationale
metrics7 daysHigh volume (per-request rows). One week is enough for engine training cycles + Grafana dashboards.
backend_health30 daysLower volume (per-poll cycle, ~12/min per backend). 30 days covers SLO trend analysis.
scaling_events90 daysEvent-driven (only on action). 90 days for incident retrospectives.
policy_changes(no policy — append only)One row per changed field per POST; growth is bounded by operator activity. Auto-retention not enforced; archival left to deployer.
metrics_1min CAGG(no policy)Aggregate is much smaller than raw metrics; no need to drop.

Every cross-service SQL access goes through services/shared/queries.py. No inline SQL in service code. Bind parameters only — no string interpolation, including intervals.

ConstantCallerPurpose
ANOMALY_QUERYanomaly-detectorPer-(instance, metric_name) avg / max / stddev over a window

30.7 Multi-tenancy plan Issue #129

Each hypertable gets a tenant_id TEXT NOT NULL column and a composite index (tenant_id, time DESC). Indexes used by existing queries get rebuilt as (tenant_id, <original columns>). Writers stamp tenant_id at insert time; readers scope every SELECT.

PhaseWhat lands
Phase 1 — schema only Add tenant_id column (NULLABLE for one release cycle, defaulting to 'default'). Backfill existing rows. Switch index definitions to composite. Zero query changes.
Phase 2 — writers Every service includes tenant_id in every INSERT. Today's single-tenant writers default to 'default'.
Phase 3 — readers + NOT NULL Every SELECT includes a tenant_id = $X predicate. Column flipped to NOT NULL. Backfill is complete; legacy NULL rows are gone.
Phase 4 — tenants + api_keys + webhooks New tables (§30.2 planned) land. API auth middleware resolves tenant_id from the API key (#132) and stamps it on every downstream call.

Rollout sequencing avoids any moment where the database is in a half-applied state. Each phase is independently revertable.

30.8 Migration strategy

  • Source of truth: infrastructure/timescaledb/init.sql contains the full canonical DDL applied on first container start. Subsequent schema evolutions live as numbered scripts under infrastructure/timescaledb/migrations/<N>__<description>.sql (planned — migrations/ folder not yet created; first migration arrives with #129).
  • Idempotency: every migration is CREATE … IF NOT EXISTS + ALTER TABLE … IF NOT EXISTS compatible. Re-running a migration on a partially-applied database completes the operation rather than failing.
  • Application strategy: services apply pending migrations on startup (planned). For Helm deploys, a Kubernetes Job runs migrations before the rolling update of dependent services.
  • Rollback: every migration ships with a paired migrations/<N>__<description>_down.sql. Not all migrations are reversible — destructive operations are flagged in the up script's comment header.
  • Policy schema versioning: orthogonal to DB migrations. Tracked by schema_version in policy.yaml (#134). The DB doesn't enforce policy schema — policy-manager does.

30.9 Backup and restore

Self-host deployers are responsible for backup; SmartLoad does not bundle a backup orchestrator. Recommended baseline (planned — not yet enforced):

  • Compose: nightly pg_dump against the smartloaddb database. The timescaledb-data named volume is the live store.
  • Kubernetes: use the TimescaleDB Operator's continuous WAL archiving + scheduled snapshots; ship the Helm chart with a sample CronJob in infrastructure/helm/smartload/templates/backup.yaml (planned).
  • Restore drill: documented in SDP/runbooks/restore-from-snapshot.md (planned).

Audit ledgers (policy_changes, scaling_events) deserve special attention: losing them loses the "who changed what" story. For SaaS, these tables are replicated to an immutable archive.

31. Background & Related Work Canonical

Canonical Design Intent

This section is the canonical positioning of SmartLoad in the existing technology and research landscape. It exists so that the project can be evaluated, written up, and presented without re-deriving why the system was designed the way it was. Every claim here that involves prior art carries an inline citation (author + venue + year). Concepts are explained in terms a reader of §2–§6 already has — no extra theoretical apparatus is assumed.

31.1 Classical L4/L7 load balancers

The dominant production load-balancing fabric is built on long-lived reverse proxies that select an upstream per request from a small set of well-understood scheduling rules. The four scheduling rules in routine use are:

RuleDecisionFailure mode
Round-robin (RR)Hand out requests in a fixed cyclic order over the upstream set.Fairness assumption (every backend has identical capacity and latency profile). One slow backend eats 1/N of traffic until passive health checks trip.
Weighted round-robin (WRR)Same as RR but each upstream carries a static weight; the cycle yields each upstream weight-many times before advancing.Weights are operator-configured at admit time and rarely revisited; degrades as load patterns drift.
Least-connections (LC)Send the next request to the upstream with the smallest current in-flight count.Open connections is a noisy proxy for true load; correlates with capacity only when per-request CPU is uniform.
IP-hash / consistent-hashHash a request header (client IP, session cookie) to a slot in the upstream set.Sticky by design — cannot react to backend health within the affinity window without breaking session identity.

The three open-source implementations SmartLoad most directly addresses are NGINX (Sysoev, 2004), HAProxy (Tarreau, 2002), and Envoy (Klein et al., USENIX SREcon 2017 / Lyft). All three publish the four scheduling rules above plus passive and active health checking. None of the three ship a built-in feedback loop that re-weights upstreams from external telemetry: in NGINX, dynamic upstream weights require either a paid module (NGINX Plus — upstream API), an external operator (the SmartLoad LB Sidecar pattern in §8.10), or a reload triggered by a config rewrite. The data plane in all three projects is therefore designed around in-memory static config + passive health probing; "intelligent" routing is explicitly out of scope.

Cloud-managed equivalents — AWS Application Load Balancer (Amazon, 2016), GCP Cloud Load Balancing (Google, 2015), Azure Front Door (Microsoft, 2018) — expose similar scheduling rules with managed control planes but constrain weight updates to coarse-grained autoscaling-group events. None expose a per-second decision surface for an external controller to drive.

Where SmartLoad sits: SmartLoad does not replace NGINX. It runs NGINX as its data plane and adds an out-of-band LB Sidecar (§8.10) that consumes Redis-published decisions and rewrites upstream.conf + nginx -s reload on the order of seconds. The classical scheduler stays in place as the deterministic safety floor (Principle 9, §4); it is never deprecated.

31.2 Cloud autoscalers

The dominant production autoscaling shapes split along two axes: what triggers a scaling decision (reactive metric thresholds vs predictive), and what dimension is scaled (replica count vs per-pod resources vs cluster nodes).

SystemTriggerDimensionReaction shape
Kubernetes HPA (Burns et al., USENIX OSDI 2016)CPU/memory utilization (default) or custom metrics via the metrics-server API.Replica count of a Deployment / StatefulSet.Reactive. Default 15s control interval; scales when observed metric crosses target for the stabilization window.
Kubernetes VPAObserved resource consumption.Per-pod CPU/memory requests.Reactive; typically requires pod restart on apply, so most operators leave it in "recommend" mode.
Kubernetes Cluster AutoscalerPending pods that cannot schedule.Node count in a managed node group.Reactive. Adds nodes when pods stay pending; removes underutilized nodes after a cool-down.
AWS Auto Scaling GroupsCloudWatch metric breach (target tracking, step scaling, or predictive scaling using a built-in forecaster).EC2 instance count in an ASG.Mostly reactive; predictive scaling (Amazon, 2018) is the closest commercial analogue to SmartLoad's forecast-driven autoscaler.
GCP Managed Instance GroupAverage CPU, HTTP load-balancing utilization, or custom Stackdriver metrics.VM instance count.Reactive.

The dominant production scheme is therefore reactive scaling: a metric crosses a threshold, a stabilization window prevents flapping, then the replica count moves. The lag between load arrival and the new replica being ready is structurally bounded below by container start time + warm-up — commonly 30–90 s for typical web workloads (Verma et al., EuroSys 2015 — Borg paper, observes similar tail latencies on Google's internal fleet).

AWS Predictive Scaling (Amazon, 2018) is the closest commercial analogue: it fits a recurrent forecasting model on CloudWatch metrics and pre-warms instances on the predicted shape. The model is a black box and the forecasting and routing decisions are not coupled — the forecaster cannot inform the per-request scheduler.

Where SmartLoad sits: SmartLoad's forecasting service (§8.4) publishes a horizon-N RPS forecast on smartload.forecast; the autoscaler (§8.6) consumes the forecast and scales the backend pool ahead of saturation. The split between predictive (forecast-driven) and reactive (telemetry-driven) is explicit: when the forecaster is silent or fails liveness, the autoscaler falls back to a reactive sliding-window rule. This is a direct application of Principle 4 (graceful degradation).

31.3 Reinforcement learning for routing and resource allocation

Applying RL to systems decision-making has a deep literature; the lines most relevant to SmartLoad's bandit-style routing policy (§32.4) are:

  • RL for job scheduling. DeepRM (Mao, Alizadeh, Menache, Kandula — HotNets 2016) is the canonical reference for "use RL to schedule jobs against a heterogeneous pool"; the framing — observation = system snapshot, action = where to place the next job, reward = makespan / completion-time signal — transfers almost directly to per-request routing if "job" is replaced by "next request."
  • RL for adaptive video bitrate routing. Pensieve (Mao, Netravali, Alizadeh — SIGCOMM 2017) used a neural policy trained with A3C on logged client traces; the offline-replay-of-logged-traces methodology — not online interaction — is essentially the framing SmartLoad uses with the Alibaba traces (see §32.4 — "contextual-bandit framing").
  • PPO (Schulman, Wolski, Dhariwal, Radford, Klimov — arXiv:1707.06347, 2017) is the on-policy actor-critic algorithm SmartLoad uses; it is preferred over earlier policy-gradient methods because the clipped surrogate objective is robust to mis-tuned learning rates and bounded-update KL constraints implicitly. PPO is implemented in Stable-Baselines3 (Raffin et al., JMLR 2021) and the action-masking variant in sb3-contrib MaskablePPO, both of which SmartLoad's training pipeline uses verbatim.
  • Action masking under invalid actions. The masking approach used in MaskablePPO follows Vinyals et al. (StarCraft II, Nature 2019) and Huang & Ontañón (arXiv:2006.14171, 2020) — replace invalid-action logits with -inf before the softmax so the policy assigns zero probability mass to masked actions and the policy gradient is well-defined. SmartLoad uses the mask to exclude unhealthy backends from the action space (§32.4).
  • Contextual bandits for content selection. Li, Chu, Langford, Schapire (WWW 2010) framed news article recommendation as a contextual bandit; the framing — per-step context, discrete arm choice, observed reward without environment dynamics — is precisely how SmartLoad treats the offline trace replay. The trained policy is a predictor of which backend will have low latency next, given the current state; it is not a learned model of how routing perturbs the system. Auer (NeurIPS 2002 — UCB) provides the foundational regret-bound analysis on the same framing.
Why bandit framing instead of full MDP Architecture decision

A full MDP framing of routing would require a learned environment model: "if I send this request to backend B, what does the next observation look like?" SmartLoad explicitly does not learn such a model. The reasons, in priority order:

  1. Production safety. A learned environment model would loop into the policy's training signal — bias in the model becomes bias in the policy. The deterministic feedback loops that already exist in production (NGINX max_fails passive health, anomaly-detector exclusions, autoscaler reactivity) handle the "consequence" axis cleanly; adding a learned model on top of them is a coupling risk.
  2. Reproducibility. Offline replay of logged traces (Alibaba) is byte-deterministic given a seed and partition. A learned environment introduces a second stochastic system whose evolution depends on weight initialisation. The evaluation harness (§33) needs reproducible runs to support a meaningful comparison.
  3. Training cost. Joint training of environment + policy roughly doubles the wall-clock and triples the failure modes. PPO on the bandit framing trains to convergence on the canary partition in ~75 minutes of CPU; the MDP variant was estimated at ≈10× on the same hardware.

This decision is recorded in services/rl-engine/training/simulator.py module docstring and in SOT §22 v1.0.7 review changelog entries. The trade-off is explicit: the trained policy is good at predicting next-window backend latency from current state; it does not reason about how its own routing decisions perturb the system.

None of the RL-for-routing papers above ship as integration-ready middleware. SmartLoad's positioning relative to them is to take the same trained-policy ↔ offline-trace methodology and package it as a serving plugin behind a stable contract (policies/ppo/policy.py ↔ policies/random_shadow/policy.py) so the same load balancer can swap between trained, shadow, and classical without restarting the data plane (§8.5, §8.10).

31.4 Anomaly detection for system telemetry

The relevant techniques for backend-health anomaly detection in the routing path:

TechniqueReferenceWhy it fits routing telemetry
Static thresholdingIndustry standard (Cloud Monitoring rules, PagerDuty alerts, Datadog monitors).Cheap, explainable, ships before any model. Used as SmartLoad's baseline anomaly engine (§8.3).
Isolation ForestLiu, Ting, Zhou — ICDM 2008 / TKDD 2012.Per-point unsupervised scoring; isolates anomalies via random recursive partitioning — anomalous points are isolated in fewer splits than normal points. Linear training cost; the model itself is fit without labels. Strong fit for SmartLoad: Yahoo SMD's test_label/ provides real per-timestep anomaly labels, used (not for training, only for evaluation) to validate F1 against the SOT's >0.80 gate (§8.3).
LSTM autoencoderMalhotra, Vig, Shroff, Agarwal — ESANN 2015. Extended by Park et al. (2018) and Hundman et al. (KDD 2018 — NASA telemetry).Reconstruction error on a learned sequential model; catches contextual anomalies that point-based methods miss. Heavier to train and deploy; SmartLoad treats it as a deferred upgrade path (§35.6).
Robust statistics (EWMA / median absolute deviation)Hochenbaum et al. — arXiv:1704.07706, 2017 (Twitter's AnomalyDetection R package).Resists outliers in the training window. Considered but not adopted — the threshold engine + rolling mean is the simpler baseline and the Isolation Forest upgrade path covers contextual cases.

The Numenta Anomaly Benchmark (Lavin & Ahmad — ICMLA 2015) and Yahoo Server Machine Dataset (Su et al., KDD 2019 / NetManAIOps OmniAnomaly) are the open reference traces SmartLoad's anomaly engine is benchmarked against. The implementation pointers and status are in §8.3 (engine deep dive) and §32.2 (algorithm theory).

31.5 Time-series forecasting for load and capacity

Three lines of work bear on SmartLoad's forecasting engine (§8.4):

  • Box-Jenkins ARIMA (Box, Jenkins, Reinsel — first edition 1970, fifth edition Wiley 2015) is the canonical autoregressive framework. SmartLoad's production forecaster is a pre-trained ARIMA(p,d,q) bundle produced by tools/forecasting-training/train.py and served by services/forecasting/engines/arima/engine.py (§32.3).
  • Prophet (Taylor & Letham — The American Statistician, 2017 / Facebook open-source) is the additive decomposition model with explicit trend + weekly/yearly seasonality + holiday components. Considered for the forecasting plugin but deferred: ARIMA + moving-average are sufficient for the prototype's 5-minute horizon and the project's KPI (§19 — MAPE < 20%) does not yet demand the extra capacity. The Prophet path is tracked as a future plugin (§35.6).
  • LSTM / GRU sequence models have published wins on long-horizon, highly seasonal load (e.g. Salinas et al. — DeepAR, International Journal of Forecasting 2020). Deferred for the same reason as Prophet — the marginal accuracy gain at the 5-min horizon does not justify the model-deployment surface area today.

SmartLoad's moving-average baseline (§32.3) is the same windowed-mean predictor that NGINX Plus, AWS Auto Scaling target-tracking, and most reactive autoscalers use under the hood. Its presence as a first-class plugin in the forecasting service ensures the system never has a "no forecast available" state — a direct embodiment of Principle 4 (graceful degradation).

31.6 AIOps and commercial AI-driven load management

The commercial AIOps space (Datadog Watchdog, New Relic Applied Intelligence, Dynatrace Davis, Splunk ITSI) has converged on a recognisable shape: read telemetry from an arbitrary tool stack, surface anomalies and forecasts to a human, do not automate the data-plane response. The boundary between detection and actuation is preserved because the cost of an incorrect automated action is too high for a horizontal vendor to take on the customer's behalf.

Google's internal Borg (Verma et al., EuroSys 2015) and Autopilot (Rzadca et al., EuroSys 2020) combine forecast + reactive control loops for cluster-wide resource allocation, but the system is closed-source and not deployable outside Google's fleet. Facebook's Katran (2018) is an XDP-based L4 load balancer with deterministic scheduling; it is the cleanest open-source data-plane reference but explicitly does not host a decision layer.

The gap SmartLoad addresses: a single, open-source, on-prem deployable middleware that closes the loop between AIOps-style detection (anomaly, forecasting, RL routing) and actuation in the data plane (NGINX upstream weights, container scaling), with the routing-hierarchy safety contract (§15) making automated actuation defensible by construction.

31.7 SmartLoad's positioning in one paragraph

Existing classical load balancers (§31.1) provide deterministic, well-understood scheduling but cannot react to backend health within seconds without external orchestration. Existing cloud autoscalers (§31.2) react to threshold breaches but cannot pre-warm against a predicted spike outside narrow proprietary offerings. RL-for-routing research (§31.3) demonstrates feasibility but does not ship as integration-ready middleware. AIOps platforms (§31.6) surface decisions but do not automate the data-plane response. SmartLoad combines these capabilities into a single coherent middleware: classical routing as the deterministic floor, anomaly detection as a hard safety constraint, RL routing as an adaptive optimisation inside the bounds, forecasting as the proactive scaling driver, and a strict routing decision hierarchy (§15) so the AI is trusted only inside provably-safe envelopes. The contribution is not any single algorithm — it is the integration shape that makes those algorithms safe to ship behind one load balancer.

32. Algorithm Foundations Canonical

Canonical Design Intent

This section is the canonical statement of what each engine actually computes, including the math, the parameters in the wired implementation, and the design choices that determined "this algorithm, not another." It is structured so that the same content can be lifted into a thesis methodology chapter without re-deriving anything. Every algorithm description lists: (a) the formula or rule, (b) the parameters wired today, (c) the file path of the implementation, (d) the rationale for the choice.

32.1 Classical routing baselines

Round-robin (cyclic over eligible backends)

Rule. Given the current set of backends B = {b1, ..., bN} and the most-recently-served backend id last, the next backend served is the lowest bi ∈ B' with bi.id > last; wrap to the first element of B' when no such backend exists. B' is the eligible subset — is_eligible(health) excludes unhealthy and unknown; healthy and degraded remain in rotation (degraded backends are slower but still serving).

Why backend_id pointer, not modular index. A modular index (self._idx % len(eligible)) reshuffles the entire rotation whenever the eligible set changes size. A backend_id pointer is stable: removing the just-served backend still serves the next id; adding a backend doesn't reset the cycle. This is the difference between "deterministic under set changes" and "well-behaved on a static set" — SmartLoad's eligible set is dynamic (anomaly excludes / restores backends in real time), so the pointer form is the correct choice.

Implementation. services/rl-engine/policies/round_robin/policy.py (use site: training-time baseline + RL_POLICY=round_robin); NGINX's built-in cyclic upstream selection is the production data-plane equivalent and is the deterministic floor (§15).

Least-connections (load proxy)

Rule. Sort the eligible set by (queue_depth ASC, backend_id ASC) and route to the head; secondary key is a deterministic tie-breaker. Scores are descending: rank i → (N−i)/N.

Queue-depth caveat. The Alibaba trace does not provide a separate queue-depth signal, so SmartLoad proxies it with the SUM of request_count per window (RL_STATE_QUERY in services/shared/queries.py). The proxy correlates with true load when per-request CPU cost is uniform — it does not when the workload mixes cheap and expensive requests. This is a documented limitation of the offline evaluation (§33.7).

Implementation. services/rl-engine/policies/least_connections/policy.py.

32.2 Anomaly detection

Threshold engine (baseline)

Rule. For each backend, given the current measured latencyms, the per-backend rolling-mean μlat, the error rate e, and the sample count n:

ConditionClassificationScore (∈ [0, 1])
n < min_sample_counthealthy0.0 — not enough data to call.
e > error_rate_thresholdunhealthymin(1, e / error_rate_threshold) — error-rate dominates other signals.
latencyms > latency_multiplier × μlatdegradedmin(1, ratio / (latency_multiplier × 2)) — saturates at 2× the multiplier.
otherwisehealthy0.0

Wired parameters (defaults in services/anomaly-detector/engines/threshold/engine.py):

  • latency_multiplier = 3.0 — latency > 3× rolling mean trips degraded.
  • error_rate_threshold = 0.05 — 5% error rate trips unhealthy.
  • min_sample_count = 10 — below this, no classification.

Design rationale. No labels are available on the production telemetry, so a parametric supervised model can't be the baseline. A static rule is cheap, explainable, and falsifiable — an operator can read the threshold and trace why a backend was flagged. The same rule is the fallback path for the Isolation Forest plugin (next sub-section) when the model artifact is missing.

Isolation Forest Scaffolded

Algorithm. Liu, Ting, Zhou (ICDM 2008) construct an ensemble of randomly-grown decision trees; each tree splits on a random feature and a random threshold within the feature's range. The anomaly score for a point x is derived from the expected path length E[h(x)] to isolate x across the ensemble: anomalous points sit in sparse regions of feature space and are isolated in fewer splits than normal points. The score is normalised to [0, 1] via

s(x, n) = 2 ^ ( -E[h(x)] / c(n) )

where c(n) is the average path length of an unsuccessful binary-search-tree probe on n points — an analytical constant. The free parameter is the contamination — the operator's prior on the expected fraction of anomalies in the training data.

Why this, not LSTM autoencoders. The LSTM-AE family (Malhotra et al., ESANN 2015; Hundman et al., KDD 2018) catches contextual anomalies that point-based methods miss, but requires a meaningful training set per workload and a GPU at inference time. Isolation Forest is point-based and works on the per-backend feature vector that SmartLoad already extracts (rolling latency, error rate, request rate). It ships under one Python package (scikit-learn), is trivially serialisable to a .pkl, and falls back cleanly to the threshold rule when the artifact is missing — the integration shape SmartLoad's plugin contract requires (Principle 6).

Implementation status. Shipped v1.0.7ab (#101 closed). services/anomaly-detector/engines/isolation_forest/engine.py loads a bundle dict {model, smd_scaler, production_scaler, feature_order, thresholds, metadata} with strict shape validation; falls back to threshold via bootstrap_engine() on any load failure. Trained model at services/anomaly-detector/models/isolation_forest.pkl (2 MB, sklearn 1.3.2 pinned). Training pipeline: tools/anomaly-training/train_smd.py on SMD (Server Machine Dataset / OmniAnomaly) with real per-timestep test_label/ ground truth — test F1 = 0.8012 on SMD holdout (PASS of the > 0.80 KPI gate). Empirical follow-up (#165): the comparison bench at experiments/anomaly-engine-bench/ shows the model agrees with the threshold baseline on only 25% of cells at production scales — the SMD-trained model lives in standardised-SMD coordinates while production_scaler maps inputs into standardised-MST coordinates; the two mean-0/std-1 spaces don't align, so standardisation pulls every production input toward the origin and the model classes it healthy regardless of severity. Compose default remains ANOMALY_ENGINE=threshold until #165's production-scale re-calibration lands.

32.3 Workload forecasting

Moving-average baseline

Rule. Given the last W windowed request-rate samples r1, ..., rW:

predicted_rps     = (1/W) Σ r_i
confidence_lower  = max(0, predicted_rps − σ)
confidence_upper  = predicted_rps + σ
σ                 = sqrt( (1/(W−1)) Σ (r_i − predicted_rps)² )   (sample stddev)

Wired parameters (defaults in services/forecasting/engines/moving_average/engine.py):

  • window_samples = 60 — last 60 windowed observations enter the mean.
  • horizon_minutes = 5 — the published forecast horizon label; the predictor is a one-step model, so the horizon is informational (the autoscaler treats the value as "expected RPS for the next decision cycle").

Why this is a first-class plugin, not a fallback. The moving-average predictor is the same windowed-mean model that AWS Auto Scaling target-tracking, NGINX Plus least-time, and most reactive autoscalers use under the hood. Keeping it in the engine catalogue rather than burying it as a private fallback means the operator can run the forecasting service on the baseline predictor without producing a "no forecast available" state. This is a direct application of Principle 4 (graceful degradation).

ARIMA(p,d,q)

Model. An autoregressive integrated moving-average model fits

(1 − Σ φ_i L^i)(1 − L)^d X_t = (1 + Σ θ_j L^j) ε_t

where L is the backshift operator, d is the integration order, φ are the autoregressive coefficients, θ are the moving-average coefficients, and ε is white noise. The Box-Jenkins methodology (Box, Jenkins, Reinsel; Wiley 2015) selects (p, d, q) via partial autocorrelation, autocorrelation, and unit-root testing on the differenced series.

Wired model. The training pipeline (tools/forecasting-training/train.py, owned by Nada Nabil — PR #144) writes a pickled bundle to services/forecasting/models/arima_model.pkl with shape:

{
  "result":    statsmodels ARIMA result object (.append + .get_forecast),
  "order":     (p, d, q) tuple,
  "freq":      "5min",                       # training frequency
  "exog_cols": list[str],                    # empty for plain ARIMA
  "exog_stats": dict                         # empty for plain ARIMA
}

The serving plugin (services/forecasting/engines/arima/engine.py) loads the bundle, calls result.append(recent_window, refit=False) on each cycle to nudge state, and emits a one-step forecast with the 95% confidence interval. The append window is capped at _MAX_APPEND_SAMPLES = 60 so a long-running service does not pay an O(history) cost per cycle. If the artifact is missing or the load fails, the engine falls back to a mean-of-history forecast so the service stays alive.

Current quality — honest. The shipped ARIMA artifact (v1.0.7g/i — see §22 changelog) measures MAPE ≈ 25.0% on the held-out partition. The SOT KPI is MAPE < 20% (§19), which means the artifact is wired but does not yet meet the gate; FORECAST_ENGINE=moving_average remains the default until tuning closes the gap. The retraining workstream is tracked in §35.2.

32.4 Reinforcement-learning routing

Framing: offline contextual bandit, not online MDP

SmartLoad treats per-request backend selection as a contextual bandit (Li, Chu, Langford, Schapire — WWW 2010), not a Markov Decision Process. The simulator (services/rl-engine/training/simulator.py) replays Alibaba trace windows independently of the agent's action — step(action) does not modify next_state based on the chosen backend. next_state is the next pre-recorded trace window, returned verbatim regardless of the policy's pick. The reward is the latency of the chosen backend in that next window: an observational signal, not a causal consequence.

The trained policy is therefore a good predictor of which backend will have low latency next, given the current state. It is not a learned model of how routing decisions perturb the system — the production feedback loops (NGINX max_fails, anomaly-detector exclusions, autoscaler reactivity) handle that axis. The bandit framing rationale is recorded in detail in §31.3 (collapsible).

Observation space

Flat Box(shape=(N_MAX_BACKENDS × 3,), dtype=float32, low=0, high=∞). Per-backend features, in fixed slot order:

IndexFeatureNormalisation
3ilatencyms of backend idivided by NormParams.latency_scale (default 2000 ms, matching Alibaba p99)
3i+1request count of backend idivided by NormParams.request_count_scale (default 200, typical 30s window count)
3i+2health flag of backend i1.0 if eligible, 0.0 otherwise

The same build_observation() function (services/rl-engine/obs_builder.py) is used at training time and at serving time, guaranteeing train/serve parity. NormParams are persisted to artifact_meta.json alongside policy.zip so the serving plugin can restore the exact scaling the policy was trained on.

Action space

Discrete(N_MAX_BACKENDS) — the index of the chosen backend (0-based, sorted by backend_id). The trained policy emits one chosen backend per step.

Action masking

The mask is constructed by build_action_mask(state, N_MAX_BACKENDS): True for slots whose health is eligible (healthy or degraded), False for unhealthy / unknown / empty slots. Invalid-action logits are replaced with −∞ before the softmax (Huang & Ontañón, arXiv:2006.14171, 2020) so the policy assigns zero probability mass to masked actions. When every slot is masked, all_masked_fallback() unmasks the least-bad backend so training doesn't deadlock; in production the routing-decision hierarchy (§15) takes over.

This is implemented via the sb3-contrib ActionMasker wrapper feeding MaskablePPO.

Reward function

The per-step reward in services/rl-engine/training/reward.py is:

r(s, a, s') = − ( latency_ms(s'_a) / latency_scale )
              − λ × imbalance(s')
              − 10.0 × [ s'_a.health = "unhealthy" ]

where:

  • s'a is the chosen backend in the next state.
  • latency_scale = 2000.0 ms (matches the observation normalisation).
  • λ = 0.1 — the imbalance penalty weight; imbalance(s') = std(queue_depths) / (mean(queue_depths) + ε), ε = 1.0.
  • The −10.0 health term is a defence-in-depth hard penalty for routing to an unhealthy backend. In practice the action mask prevents this; the term ensures a strong negative signal if the mask is ever misconfigured.

Why the imbalance term. Because the simulator does not model how the policy's choice perturbs queue depths, the imbalance term is a regulariser on the historical spread, not a learned consequence. It discourages picks that bias the policy toward backends whose contemporaneous load is an outlier and produces a smoother reward signal during training. Removing it did not improve held-out metrics during the canary run (§32.5 rationale).

PPO — clipped surrogate objective

PPO (Schulman et al., arXiv:1707.06347, 2017) optimises the clipped surrogate objective

L_CLIP(θ) = E_t[ min(  r_t(θ) A_t,
                       clip(r_t(θ), 1−ε, 1+ε) A_t  ) ]
r_t(θ) = π_θ(a_t | s_t) / π_θ_old(a_t | s_t)

where πθ is the current policy, πθold is the policy at rollout time, rt is the importance-sampling ratio, At is the advantage estimate (Generalised Advantage Estimation, Schulman et al. 2015), and ε is the clip range. The clip term turns off the gradient when the importance ratio leaves the trust region [1−ε, 1+ε], which makes the algorithm robust to mis-tuned learning rates without an explicit KL constraint.

SmartLoad uses MaskablePPO from sb3-contrib, which adds invalid-action masking to the action distribution before sampling and log-prob computation. The action-space layout makes the optimisation single-step per episode in the bandit framing — the on-policy machinery is preserved for hyperparameter robustness, not for credit assignment across time.

Training recipe

Driver: services/rl-engine/training/train_ppo.py. Hyperparameters and defaults:

ParameterValueRationale
algorithmMaskablePPO (sb3-contrib)Invalid-action masking is non-negotiable — the trained policy must never propose an unhealthy backend.
total_steps (canary)50,000Fast pre-flight on the first K partitions. Two gates must pass before full training begins.
total_steps (full)2,000,000~75 min on a single CPU; produces the artifact behind policies/ppo/policy.py.
episode_length200 windows (=100 minutes of 30 s windows)Long enough to span typical daily-load curvature in the trace; short enough that the GAE bootstrap is not dominated by trace-end edge effects.
n_max_backends5Matches the production backend-pool size in docker-compose.yml + the static upstream.conf. The serving plugin validates this at load time.
imbalance_lambda0.1The largest value where removing the term did not measurably change held-out metrics in the canary (see §22 v1.0.7 changelog).
norm.latency_scale2000 msAlibaba p99 across the training partitions.
norm.request_count_scale200 requests / 30sMean request count per window at moderate load.

Canary gates. Both must pass before full training is allowed to begin:

  1. policy_gradient_loss trending down across the last 10 logged updates (linear-regression slope ≤ 0 on the tail window).
  2. PPO mean reward > round-robin mean reward on 3 ad-hoc mini-episodes drawn from the canary dataset.

If either gate fails, the script exits with code 1 and prints instructions to switch to train_dqn.py. The DQN fallback exists per the risk register (§17 — "PPO training diverges or > 7 days").

Serving: argmax-dominant weighting

The trained PPO policy chooses one backend per step (Discrete(N) action space). At serving time, the LB needs to translate that into NGINX upstream weights. Two natural mappings exist; SmartLoad rejects one and adopts the other:

MappingRejected / adoptedReason
Softmax of logits across backends → weight distributionRejectedThe policy was trained to pick one, not to distribute mass. Softmax over logits is a different objective than the one optimised — using it at serving time would route traffic against the policy's training distribution.
Argmax-dominant: chosen backend gets _DOMINANT_WEIGHT, remainder split evenly across the other eligiblesAdoptedHeavily emphasises the policy's choice while leaving a meaningful floor so NGINX's health probing doesn't starve the other backends. _DOMINANT_WEIGHT = 0.7 in services/rl-engine/policies/ppo/policy.py.

Operating modes (serving)

ModeBehaviourUse case
shadow DefaultFull inference each cycle but RoutingAction.mode is always shadow; the LB sidecar ignores the envelope.Pre-production verification, validation runs, when the policy hasn't been trusted yet.
hybridInference runs; RoutingAction.mode = "active". The LB sidecar applies the weights iff every other gate agrees (anomaly mask, policy short-circuit; see §15).Production routing.
learningDeprecated; accepted for backwards-compat and silently mapped to hybrid.—

The shadow default is the canonical safety pin (see §22 v1.0.7g rollout: RL_RUNLOOP_ENABLED=true default, but RL_MODE=shadow default — the runloop publishes, the sidecar ignores). Flipping RL_MODE=active in .env is an explicit operator opt-in.

32.5 Why these algorithms, not others

Five design decisions concentrate the algorithm choice surface. Each is recorded below with its trade-off:

DecisionAdoptedRejectedWhy
Anomaly baselineThresholdIsolation Forest as day-1 baselineThreshold ships without any model or training data; IF needs an artifact. Threshold is also the fallback path for IF when the artifact is missing — making it the baseline means the fallback path is exercised continuously.
Anomaly upgrade pathIsolation ForestLSTM autoencoderIF is single-package, fast to train, serialises to a .pkl. LSTM-AE catches contextual anomalies IF misses, but the integration cost (GPU at inference, deeper training pipeline) is not warranted at the current KPI gate (anomaly F1 > 0.80). LSTM-AE remains the deferred upgrade (§35.6).
Forecasting baselineMoving-averagePer-service no-forecast stateEliminates the "no forecast available" failure mode by always having a predictor — Principle 4 in action.
Forecasting modelARIMAProphet, DeepARThe 5-min horizon does not yet demand the extra capacity. ARIMA's statsmodels footprint is well-understood; the pickle bundle is trivially shipped. Prophet and DeepAR are deferred (§35.6).
RL framingOffline contextual bandit on Alibaba tracesOnline MDP with a learned environment modelReproducibility + safety + training cost (§31.3 collapsible). The cost is that the policy doesn't reason about its own routing impact — SmartLoad relies on the production feedback loops to handle that axis.

32.6 Review checklist — algorithms

  • Does every engine ship with a baseline that requires no model artifact?
  • Does the trained-model path fall back to the baseline cleanly when the artifact is missing?
  • Are the parameters used at serving time persisted alongside the model artifact (e.g. artifact_meta.json)?
  • Does the action-masking layer guarantee the trained policy cannot propose an unhealthy backend?
  • Are the action / observation shapes identical between training and serving (single build_observation, single build_action_mask)?
  • Does flipping RL_MODE=active require an explicit operator change, not a silent default?
  • Is the reward function recorded in one canonical file and never duplicated in serving code?

33. Evaluation Methodology Canonical

Canonical Design Intent

This section defines the canonical evaluation contract for SmartLoad: what claims we test, what we compare against, what we measure, how we report. The harness implementation lives at experiments/baseline-vs-smartload/ (§22 v1.0.7r/t); this section is the framing that gives the harness numbers meaning. Two distinct evaluation surfaces co-exist and are documented separately: the offline PPO eval (training-time) and the baseline-vs-SmartLoad bench harness (system-level). The first lives in services/rl-engine/training/eval_harness.py; the second in experiments/baseline-vs-smartload/.

33.1 Research questions

SmartLoad's evaluation is structured around four questions. Each maps to a specific harness or eval layer.

#QuestionSurface
RQ1 Given identical infrastructure (same NGINX, same backend pool, same workload), does enabling the SmartLoad decision plane produce measurably better tail-latency and failure-rate outcomes than plain NGINX round-robin? System-level bench harness (experiments/baseline-vs-smartload/).
RQ2 Does the trained PPO routing policy, evaluated offline on held-out trace partitions, outperform the classical baselines (round-robin, least-connections) on per-window p50/p95/p99 latency and SLO-violation rate? Offline PPO eval (services/rl-engine/training/eval_harness.py).
RQ3 Does the LB sidecar successfully pull a misbehaving backend out of rotation faster than NGINX's passive health checks would, in the case where the backend is slow but still returns 200 OK (so passive checks never trip)? System-level bench harness, Phase B (anomaly window). Requires the latency-spike anomaly model from §33.4.
RQ4 Does the forecasting service publish a predicted-RPS curve far enough ahead of saturation that the autoscaler can pre-warm a new backend before the ramp tops out? Dual surface as of v1.0.7x: experiments/baseline-vs-smartload/ Phase A (ramp) for the static comparison, and the new experiments/adaptive-bench/ 5-phase shape (Phase B "forecast_burst" + Phase D "anomaly_scale_down") for the dynamic-pool surface that exercises the autoscaler's provision() lifecycle from §22 v1.0.7v. Quantitative time-to-react per forecast lands in the adaptive-bench's plot_time_to_react.png + SUMMARY.md; first numbers in §34.6.

33.2 Comparison protocol — same stack, only flags differ

The protocol minimises hidden variables: the docker-compose stack, the backend pool image, the NGINX configuration, the test-backend code, and the operator-UI BFF are identical across sides. Only the four decision-plane runloop flags and the RL operating mode change between sides. The two env-file profiles are reproduced below verbatim from experiments/baseline-vs-smartload/env/:

Variablebaseline.envsmartload.env
ANOMALY_RUNLOOP_ENABLEDfalsetrue
FORECAST_RUNLOOP_ENABLEDfalsetrue
RL_RUNLOOP_ENABLEDfalsetrue
LB_SIDECAR_RUNLOOP_ENABLEDfalsetrue
RL_MODEshadowactive

In baseline mode, the decision plane is silent — the AI services start up, expose /health, but never enter the inference cycle. The lb-sidecar does not subscribe to Redis; upstream.conf stays at the static round-robin baseline baked into services/load-balancer/nginx/nginx.conf. In SmartLoad mode the same services run their full inference cycle and the sidecar rewrites upstream.conf + reloads NGINX in response to the published envelopes.

The orchestrator script (scripts/run_experiment.sh) applies one env-file at a time via docker compose --env-file ... up -d --force-recreate <decision-plane services + lb-sidecar>. The load-balancer container itself does not depend on these env vars and is not bounced. The load-generation step is a one-shot Locust container run on the same Docker network as the LB — the harness does not use the standing traffic-simulator (which is stopped during the run to avoid polluting the load shape).

33.3 Workload model — three-phase Locust shape

The load profile is a three-phase shape implemented in experiments/baseline-vs-smartload/locust/locustfile.py. Each phase is named at request time so the post-run analysis can slice the histogram cleanly by phase.

PhaseWindowUser curveWhat it tests
A_ramp0 → RAMP_SECS0 → RAMP_USERS linear rampRQ4. Does forecasting drive scale-out before backends saturate?
A_holdRAMP_SECS → ANOMALY_AT_SECSRAMP_USERS heldSteady-state baseline. Exposes heterogeneity drag (§33.4).
B_anomalyANOMALY_AT_SECS → ANOMALY_AT_SECS + ANOMALY_HOLD_SECSRAMP_USERS heldRQ3. Does the LB sidecar react to the slow backend faster than NGINX's passive max_fails?
C_sustain(end of B) → SUSTAIN_END_SECSRAMP_USERS heldRQ1. Steady-state post-recovery — does the system return to a clean tail-latency distribution?

Defaults are RAMP_USERS=50, RAMP_SECS=60, ANOMALY_AT_SECS=120, ANOMALY_HOLD_SECS=60, SUSTAIN_END_SECS=360 — a six-minute run per side. The SHORT=1 override compresses these to a 2-minute-per-side harness-validation profile (used in v1.0.7r and v1.0.7t SHORT runs). User wait time is between(0.05, 0.20) seconds so per-user RPS is bounded and the aggregate rate is determined by the user count, not per-user burstiness.

33.4 Anomaly model — heterogeneity + latency spike

Two anomaly mechanisms operate concurrently. They were chosen to cover two distinct failure classes that round-robin's static configuration genuinely cannot handle.

Baseline-slowness heterogeneity (constant)

At the start of each side, the orchestrator POSTs {ms: BASELINE_SLOW_MS} (default 15 ms) to backend-1's in-container /_admin/delay endpoint, via docker exec. The endpoint sets a process-local runtimeDelayMs that is added to every GET / + GET /health response. The delay persists for the entire run on that side and is cleared at tear-down.

Why it matters. NGINX RR distributes traffic uniformly across the five backends, so 1/5 of every request is eating an extra 15 ms even at steady state. SmartLoad could in principle down-weight backend-1, but whether it does depends on whether the trained PPO model has learned latency-aware routing (see §34 for the honest answer).

Latency-spike anomaly (Phase B)

At t = ANOMALY_AT_SECS, the orchestrator concurrently:

  1. POSTs {ms: ANOMALY_DELAY_MS} (default 200 ms) to backend-1's /_admin/delay — raising the per-request latency by 200 ms on top of the baseline-slowness 15 ms.
  2. POSTs POST /api/v1/isolate with {backend_id: "smartload-test-backend-1", status: "unhealthy", reason: "benchmark latency-spike anomaly"} to the anomaly-detector. This publishes an AnomalyEvent envelope to smartload.anomaly so the lb-sidecar's signal flow can react immediately; the anomaly-detector hasn't observed enough latency this early to fire on its own.

After ANOMALY_HOLD_SECS, both actions reverse: /_admin/delay {ms: 0} clears the runtime delay, and a recovery isolate envelope (status: "healthy") re-enables routing.

Why the latency-spike (not docker pause) model. Earlier prototype runs used docker pause to freeze backend-1. The freeze causes connection timeouts that NGINX's passive max_fails=3 fail_timeout=10s health check catches reliably — baseline RR pulls the backend out of rotation on its own, and the comparison shows no SmartLoad advantage because there's no advantage to show. The latency-spike model is precisely the case where the backend still returns 200 OK — just slowly — so NGINX's passive checks never trip, and baseline RR keeps dispatching 1/5 of traffic to the slow backend indefinitely. This is the failure class SmartLoad's anomaly + lb-sidecar pipeline is designed to solve.

Design decision recorded: the SLOW_HOSTNAME + SLOW_DELAY_MS static-knob path documented in test-backends/app.js exists but is not wired in compose — Docker scaled services give every replica the same short-ID kernel hostname, so a hostname match doesn't cleanly identify one of N replicas. The runtime-override endpoint (POST /_admin/delay) is the working mechanism; the static knobs are aspirational. (Recorded in §22 v1.0.7t for traceability.)

33.5 Metrics taxonomy

Two metric layers are reported. The harness emits raw Locust CSVs + Prometheus snapshots + a SUMMARY.md; the post-run plotter (scripts/plot_results.py) lifts these into six PNGs and a one-line delta summary.

Per-request metrics (Locust)

MetricDefinitionWhy we report it
Aggregate p50/p95/p99 latency50th / 95th / 99th percentile of the per-request response time across all phases.SLO surface — p95 is the canonical KPI (§19).
Per-phase p50/p95/p99 latencySame, sliced by request name (which embeds the phase marker).Lets us see which phase the difference comes from. "We're faster" is meaningless if it's because the bad phase was short.
Failure rateFraction of requests with status ≥ 4xx.The latency-spike anomaly model is designed to keep this at 0 on both sides; if either side starts failing, something downstream of routing is misbehaving.
Max latencySingle highest per-request response time across the run.Catches LB-side blips (e.g. NGINX reload during an upstream rewrite). The SmartLoad side pays a non-zero max-latency cost during reload — this is reported honestly.
Aggregate RPSRequests/sec across the run.Sanity check that both sides exercised the same load. If one side processes fewer requests, the comparison is biased.

System-level metrics (Prometheus + scaling audit)

MetricSourceWhy we report it
Backend pool size over timePre/post /api/v1/status snapshots + scaling-event audit.RQ4. Did the forecaster's prediction actually drive a scale-out?
Scaling-event count per phaseGET /api/v1/audit/scaling?limit=50 snapshot.Detects flapping — many scales/min suggests a tuning issue, not adaptiveness.
Prometheus up per service/api/v1/query?query=up snapshot.Sanity check that every SmartLoad service stayed reachable across the run.

Offline PPO eval metrics

services/rl-engine/training/eval_harness.py emits one row per (policy, episode) over 20 held-out episodes, with columns:

  • policy — the policy under test (random_shadow / round_robin / least_connections / ppo).
  • episode_id, mean_reward, p50_latency, p95_latency, p99_latency, slo_violation_rate, utilization_variance.

The CSV is deterministic given the seed bank + dataset partitions (excluding the timestamp in the meta JSON). The git SHA is embedded in the filename for cross-run cross-reference.

33.6 Reproducibility recipe

Every harness run writes a MANIFEST.json with the timestamp, git SHA, git working-tree state (clean / dirty), the SIDES list, and every knob value. The plotter consumes the manifest so the SUMMARY.md cross-references the exact configuration. A reproduction by a third party requires:

  1. The same SmartLoad checkout at the recorded git SHA.
  2. The same Docker host class (CPU, memory).
  3. The same five-backend pool (docker-compose.yml is the canonical source).
  4. The experiments/baseline-vs-smartload/env/baseline.env + smartload.env verbatim.
  5. The trained PPO artifact at services/rl-engine/models/policy.zip + artifact_meta.json. The artifact_meta.json records the NormParams + the training data partition hashes; together these pin the trained model.
  6. The same trace partitions in datasets/alibaba/. The eval-harness CSV header embeds the partition MD5s in the meta JSON.

Running:

bash experiments/baseline-vs-smartload/scripts/run_experiment.sh        # full 6-min/side run
SHORT=1 bash experiments/baseline-vs-smartload/scripts/run_experiment.sh # 2-min/side validation

Plotting (six PNGs + SUMMARY.md):

python experiments/baseline-vs-smartload/scripts/plot_results.py experiments/baseline-vs-smartload/results/<TIMESTAMP>

33.7 Threats to validity

Five classes of threat are documented explicitly so the reader can weight the results accordingly.

Internal validity

ThreatMitigation / status
The LB-sidecar reload mechanism causes transient connection blips that show up as max-latency spikes.Reported honestly. The cost is part of the SmartLoad side and is included in the SUMMARY.md numbers without filtering. This is a real production cost; suppressing it would mis-represent the trade-off.
NGINX's max_fails=3 passive health check might trip on the baseline side if the anomaly is severe enough.The latency-spike anomaly (§33.4) is designed precisely so passive checks do not trip — the backend still returns 200 OK. This is verified in §22 v1.0.7r: baseline side aggregate p95 jumped from 6 ms (no anomaly) to 41 ms (with heterogeneity), confirming the slow backend stays in rotation.
The BASELINE_SLOW_MS heterogeneity is configured by env var; a stale value would silently change results.The MANIFEST.json captures every knob; the SUMMARY.md surfaces them in the rendered report. Comparison across runs is only valid when the knobs match.
The standing traffic-simulator is stopped during the run, but other dev-stack background load (operator-UI polling, demo-ui SSE) keeps generating low-rate traffic.Both sides see the same dev-stack background load; the comparison cancels. The absolute numbers are inflated by ~2–4 RPS of background traffic; the deltas are not.

External validity

ThreatMitigation / status
The PPO policy was trained on Alibaba traces with homogeneous backend latencies (~9.7 ms). The benchmark workload is heterogeneous (backend-1 at +15 ms baseline + 200 ms anomaly). The training distribution does not match the eval distribution.Recorded and discussed in §34. This is the binding constraint on the current honest finding; the retraining workstream on heterogeneous traces is tracked in §35.2.
The harness uses a single backend image (Node.js stub). Real backends have non-uniform per-request CPU cost; the queue-depth proxy SmartLoad uses for "load" doesn't track that.Acknowledged. Real-traffic shadow-mode runs are tracked as a deferred evaluation (§35.7).
The harness runs on a single Docker host. Cross-host network behaviour (different latency, jitter, packet loss) is not exercised.Acknowledged. K8s-deployment evaluations are tracked under §35.5.

Construct validity

ThreatMitigation / status
"SmartLoad beats baseline" requires defining what "beats" means. The harness reports a vector of metrics; no single scalar dominance criterion is defined.The acceptance gate in #148 reads "clear delta on ≥3 of 5 metrics." This is recorded but is not a one-number summary — the comparison is intentionally multi-dimensional. §34 reports the per-metric verdict.
p99 / p99.99 latencies on the SHORT 2-min profile are noisy; the distributions are not yet tight enough to support strong claims.Recorded. SHORT-mode statistics are validation-only; the full-length 6-min profile produces tighter distributions but has not yet been run with the retrained PPO model (§22 v1.0.7r outstanding item).

Conclusion validity

ThreatMitigation / status
One run per side does not control for run-to-run variance from cold caches, JIT effects, container warm-up.Acknowledged. Multi-run averaging is a deferred harness extension (§35.3).

33.8 Statistical reporting

The harness today reports point estimates: per-phase p50/p95/p99, max, failure rate, RPS. No confidence intervals are computed across runs — each side is a single run. The SUMMARY.md surfaces the one-liner delta on aggregate p95; the six PNGs visualise the full distribution. This is appropriate for the current evaluation stage (harness validation + first honest read) but is explicitly insufficient for the final write-up. A run-of-N batching + per-metric CI computation is tracked under §35.3.

33.9 Review checklist — evaluation

  • Does each research question map to a single harness or eval layer?
  • Is the comparison protocol "same stack, only flags differ" preserved by the recorded env-files?
  • Are the anomaly mechanisms (heterogeneity + latency spike) chosen so NGINX's passive checks cannot trivially solve the problem?
  • Does every reported metric have a clear definition and a rationale for why it's reported?
  • Does every harness run write a MANIFEST.json with the git SHA + every knob?
  • Are threats to validity called out before the results are presented (this section, not §34)?
  • Does the offline PPO eval CSV embed the trained-artifact and dataset-partition hashes?

34. Results & Discussion Canonical

Canonical Design Intent

This section is the synthesised, thesis-quality landing for SmartLoad's evaluation results. It is deliberately honest: where SmartLoad wins, where it ties, where it loses today, and what's required to close each gap. The same numbers appear in raw form in §22's per-release changelog rows; this section is the cross-release synthesis. The convention used here is the project's product-honest framing — the result is reported as it is, with the binding constraint identified, not framed away.

34.1 Offline PPO eval — trained on homogeneous Alibaba traces

The trained PPO artifact (policy.zip, 156 KB, latency_scale=100.0) was evaluated against the held-out seed bank (20 episodes) by eval_harness.py. Reproduced from §18 build-status row "RL training scripts + baselines (N2.4)":

PolicyMean rewardSLO violation ratePosition vs PPO
ppo−0.00560—
round_robin−0.00560Ties PPO. Best baseline.
least_connections−0.054 (4.8×10−2 below PPO)0Worse than both PPO and RR.
random_shadow(stochastic; baseline floor)0Lower bound.

Read. The trained policy ties round-robin on the held-out trace partitions. SLO-violation rate is 0 for every policy — the trace's backend latencies are uniformly below the 200 ms SLO. The policy is measurably better than least-connections by 4.8×10−2 in mean reward (driven by the imbalance regulariser term, which penalises LC's "concentrate on the lowest-load backend" tendency at the start of each window).

Why the tie. The Alibaba traces have homogeneous backend latencies (≈9.7 ms across the partition). Any reasonably balanced policy — including round-robin — produces effectively the same p95 latency, because the per-backend p95 distributions overlap to within the noise floor. PPO learned this; it converged to a near-uniform sampling policy that mimics round-robin's behaviour. This is the expected outcome of training a latency-sensitive policy on data where latency does not discriminate across backends — the optimal policy on that distribution is round-robin.

34.2 SHORT validation — v1.0.7r (harness mechanical sanity)

The first benchmark harness landed in v1.0.7r (2026-06-03). The SHORT validation profile (2 min/side, 3-phase shape, baseline-vs-SmartLoad) produced:

MetricBaselineSmartLoadComment
Total requests38,09838,812≈320 RPS for 2 min, both sides — sanity check OK.
Median latencybaseline+1 ms+1 ms vs baseline (sidecar overhead at SHORT scale)Visible but small at the central tendency.
p99.99 latency (during anomaly window)~2,700 ms~420 ms~6× lower on SmartLoad. Largest delta at SHORT scale.
Max latency (across run)~22 s~22 sBoth sides see the anomaly's tail; SHORT-mode statistics are not tight enough for a definitive max-latency verdict.
Plots + SUMMARY.md6/6 PNGs generated6/6 PNGs generatedHarness mechanically end-to-end clean.

Read. The SHORT validation proved the harness works end-to-end: env-file cycling, decision-plane recreate, status wait, locust headless run, anomaly injection, plot generation. The long-tail signal at p99.99 was a first directional read — SmartLoad reacts to the anomaly within seconds while baseline RR keeps dispatching to the slow backend — but the 2-minute distribution at SHORT scale is not tight enough to publish as a thesis-quality result. The mechanical sanity is the contribution of v1.0.7r; the numerical sharpness is a v1.0.7t / post-retraining concern.

34.3 First honest finding — v1.0.7t (heterogeneous workload + latency-spike anomaly)

The v1.0.7t harness extension introduced the two anomaly mechanisms described in §33.4: persistent heterogeneity (backend-1 at +15 ms baseline) and a latency-spike anomaly (backend-1 at +200 ms during phase B). The SHORT validation pass produced:

Sanity / scopeResult
Baseline aggregate p95 with heterogeneity41 ms — up from 6 ms in v1.0.7r (no heterogeneity). The slowness of backend-1 is being eaten by 1/5 of RR's traffic exactly as the design requires.
Per-phase concentration of the slowdownp95 jump concentrated in A_hold, B_anomaly, C_sustain — the phases where backend-1 is slow. A_ramp doesn't yet hit steady state, so the slowdown is muted there. The harness is mechanically correct.

Per-phase p95 comparison

PhaseBaseline p95 (ms)SmartLoad p95 (ms)Delta
A_ramp1423+9 ms slower
A_hold4241≈ tied
B_anomaly4450+6 ms slower
C_sustain4344≈ tied
Max latency (single highest response in the run)BaselineSmartLoad
Across full run150 ms3,082 ms

Read — honest first finding. SmartLoad is tied or slightly worse than baseline RR in every phase. SmartLoad's max latency is >20× the baseline's; the cost source is the lb-sidecar's NGINX reload during the anomaly window, which causes brief connection blips while upstream.conf is regenerated and nginx -s reload is issued. The lb-sidecar mechanism costs latency during reload; with the currently-trained PPO model (Alibaba traces — homogeneous latencies) it does not earn that cost back via better steady-state routing.

34.4 Why this is the result — analysis

The result is consistent with the offline eval (§34.1) and the training-data narrative recorded in §22 v1.0.7i. The chain of reasoning:

  1. The trained PPO policy converged to round-robin equivalence on the homogeneous Alibaba traces (§34.1). The policy's logits, when summed under the argmax-dominant weighting (§32.4), produce upstream weights that are close to uniform — not because the policy is broken, but because uniform is the optimal solution on the training distribution.
  2. The bench harness intentionally exposes a distribution that is not homogeneous: backend-1 is at +15 ms baseline + 200 ms during the anomaly window. To beat round-robin here, the policy needs to learn to down-weight backend-1.
  3. The policy has not been trained on a distribution that contains this heterogeneity. It cannot extrapolate from "uniform latency → uniform weights" to "+15 ms backend → downweight it." The policy is doing what it was trained to do, on a distribution it was not trained for.
  4. The lb-sidecar mechanism itself works. When the AnomalyEvent is published, the sidecar reacts within ~1 s and rewrites upstream.conf to exclude backend-1. The reload during this rewrite causes the +3 s max-latency tail observed on the SmartLoad side. Operationally, this is the right behaviour; numerically, it is a cost that the routing improvement was supposed to amortise — and on the current PPO model, it doesn't.

The harness has exposed the correct truth. SmartLoad's mechanism does what it claims; the trained policy has not been trained for the workload the mechanism is being asked to handle.

34.5 What this confirms — positive read

The result confirms three architectural choices and one product framing:

ConfirmedWhy
The harness methodology is sound.The per-phase decomposition, the latency-spike anomaly model, the env-file cycle — all surfaces produce the result they should. The 6 ms → 41 ms baseline p95 jump with heterogeneity is mechanical confirmation.
The lb-sidecar enforces the routing hierarchy correctly.On AnomalyEvent publish, backend-1 is pulled out of rotation; on recovery, it returns. The sidecar's upstream.conf rewrite + nginx -s reload mechanism is end-to-end functional in the live stack.
The fallback path is exercised continuously.Even with PPO not producing latency-aware routing, the classical scheduler under SmartLoad's wraparound (anomaly mask + RR floor + policy short-circuit) is the deterministic floor. The system never loses safe behaviour.
The product framing is honest.The §22 v1.0.7t row publishes this result openly. The acceptance gate for #148 (clear delta on ≥3 of 5 metrics) remains red. SmartLoad's design discipline is to report this and identify the binding constraint, not to revise the benchmark to make it pass.

34.6 Adaptive-bench R1 + R2 + R3 — first end-to-end RQ4 evidence

The adaptive-bench (#155 R1 + #156 R2 + #157 R3) is the second harness in the evaluation surface, designed to exercise the forecast-driven scale-out path (RQ4) that #148's three-phase shape does not. The first end-to-end run landed on 2026-06-10 against the live stack with all decision services freshly restarted (see §22 v1.0.7x). Numbers below are from experiments/adaptive-bench/results/20260610T135509Z/SUMMARY.md.

Per-phase observed RPS + p95 + pool size (from run.parquet):

PhaseWindowUsers (target)Observed RPS p50Latency p95Pool size (min..max)
A_bootstrap0 → 60 s0 → 2049.411 ms5..5
B_forecast_burst60 → 90 s200521.6200 ms5..5
C_sustain90 → 240 s200544.2210 ms5..5
D_anomaly_scale_down240 → 300 s30 + anomaly158.5210 ms5..5
E_steady300 → 360 s30204.433 ms5..5

Time-to-react (forecast publish → next autoscaler action):

#Forecast timePredicted RPSFirst actionDelay
f013:55:43266.4scale_in ic=35.7 s
f113:56:43265.8scale_out ic=4121.5 s
f213:57:43294.3scale_out ic=461.3 s
f313:58:44317.2scale_out ic=41.2 s
f4, f513:59:44, 14:00:44325.4, 314.7——

Read — mechanically honest. The harness, collectors, and join pipeline produce the timeline expected: 6 ForecastResult envelopes, 197 decision-plane envelopes total, 2 autoscaler decisions. The autoscaler responded in 1.2 s once a forecast crossed the capacity threshold (f3); the longer 121 s delays on f1/f2 are the autoscaler's cooldown (overridden to 10 s for the bench) + the band that 265 RPS sits inside given the 4-backend pool (band [300, 400] from 4 × 100 capacity).

Read — architectural gaps surfaced. The "Pool size (min..max)" column reads 5..5 across every phase — the lb-sidecar's view of upstream.conf never changed despite the autoscaler making real scale_out and scale_in decisions at the Docker level. Root cause: the lb-sidecar does not subscribe to smartload.scale (issue #164, S5 high). NGINX continued to route to the seed-list backends and relied on its own passive max_fails=3 to handle the missing scaled-down container, which is why locust still reported 0% errors. The "Pool grew during B" and "Pool shrank during D" acceptance gates from #155 R3 therefore cannot be evaluated affirmatively from this run; they will pass affirmatively once #164 lands and a re-run is performed.

A second gap also surfaced during the same run: on the first attempted bench, all four decision services returned actions_total: 0 — the daemon-thread run loops had died silently sometime during the multi-day container uptime (issue #163, S5 high). Restarting them brought the chain back online and produced the run above. The fix is a catch-all outer loop body + a liveness check on /health that reports degraded when last_tick_age exceeds N × the poll interval.

What the harness proved despite the gaps. The full collector + join + plot pipeline works end-to-end (4 PNGs + SUMMARY.md emitted from a single CLI). The forecast-driven decision path is observable at sub-second resolution. The phase-D anomaly injection (manual POST /api/v1/isolate + per-container /_admin/delay) succeeds. These are the foundations RQ4's quantitative answer rests on; once #163 + #164 close, the same harness re-runs without modification to produce affirmative gate strings.

34.7 What's needed to close the gap

The binding constraint is a single concrete deliverable, owned by a single workstream:

  1. Retrain the PPO model on a heterogeneous-latency training distribution. The current artifact was trained on Alibaba traces where the backend-1 ≈ backend-2 ≈ ... ≈ backend-5 latency assumption holds. A retrained artifact on synthetic-injection traces (or a heterogeneous subset of Alibaba where backend identity correlates with latency) should produce a policy that learns to down-weight slow backends. This workstream is owned by Rghda Salah — the retraining track recorded in §22 v1.0.7i.
  2. Re-run the full-length (6-min/side) harness after the retrained artifact lands. The SHORT-mode statistics in §34.2 / §34.3 are insufficient for the thesis-quality write-up; the full-length distributions are needed to make the per-phase deltas statistically defensible.
  3. Add multi-run batching with confidence intervals to the harness so the comparison reports CIs, not point estimates (§33.8 / §35.3 / #160).
  4. Land #163 + #164 so the adaptive-bench (§34.6) re-runs with a live decision plane + scale-event-driven upstream.conf rewrites. Closes the §34.6 "5..5 pool" caveat and produces the affirmative pool-growth / pool-shrink gate strings.

34.8 Cross-reference to §22 changelog entries

The raw run-by-run data and per-release commentary live in §22. The pointer table below maps each v1.0.7 release to the result it produced.

ReleaseDateContributionWhere to read

34.9 Review checklist — results & discussion

  • Does the results section report the negative result openly?
  • Is the binding constraint identified by name (the homogeneous training distribution)?
  • Is the closing-the-gap deliverable owned by a single workstream?
  • Does every reported number tie back to a release in §22?
  • Are the harness's mechanical confirmations (heterogeneity p95 jump, lb-sidecar reload cost) called out separately from the algorithmic outcomes?
  • Is the difference between "SmartLoad's mechanism works" and "SmartLoad's current model wins" stated explicitly?

35. Limitations & Future Work Canonical

Canonical Design Intent

This section consolidates the limitations and deferred work that are scattered across the deep-dives' "Gap to close" entries, the §22 changelog, and the §17 roadmap. It exists so a thesis reader can see the full forward surface at once without triangulating across 30 sections. The limitations are split by axis — scope, model, evaluation, deployment, operations — with the deferred deliverable that closes each one called out by issue number where applicable.

35.1 Scope limitations (current phase: single-tenant middleware)

The present-phase scope is single-tenant middleware. The following capabilities are explicitly out of scope for this phase and tracked as Phase 2 SaaS adaptations. Issues live under the dedicated "Phase 2 — SaaS adaptation" GitHub milestone (created 2026-06-11) so the deferral is explicit, not implicit via "no milestone":

The scope discipline is enforced: no present-phase slice may require any of these capabilities, and the OUI.7 auth track (§28) is explicitly deferred. Operator-UI access remains LAN-only on the prototype.

35.5 K8s posture — deferred but explicit

The K8s deferral is intentional and tracked. The Helm chart is the only supported K8s path for v1; the scaffold exists so the layout discipline is visible (Chart.yaml, values.yaml, the planned templates list in the README) but the templates themselves land under #133. The K8s-native autoscaling comparison (RQ4 against HPA / VPA, §35.3) requires the chart to ship first — and the comparison shape is now defined: it will reuse the experiments/adaptive-bench/ 5-phase Locust shape + join_run.py + plot_results.py pipeline shipped in v1.0.7x (#157), with the Docker-SDK cluster client swapped for a K8s adapter. The harness side is no longer the blocker; only the K8s chart + adapter remain.

Two open questions are recorded explicitly:

  1. The autoscaler's Docker SDK pathway is not portable to K8s. The current implementation uses docker.from_env() to start/stop test-backend containers; on K8s the equivalent would be a K8s API client modifying a Deployment's replica count. The plugin contract for the cluster client is in services/autoscaler/cluster_client.py; the K8s adapter is an open task under #133.
  2. The lb-sidecar's NGINX-config-rewrite pathway depends on a shared docker volume. On K8s the same pattern works via an emptyDir or shared PVC, but the operator must opt-in. Alternative: switch the production LB to the NGINX Plus dynamic-upstream API or Envoy (which has a first-class control-plane API). Tracked as a §35.6 future-engine item.

35.6 Future engines / future evaluation surfaces

SurfaceWhy deferredWhat it would add
LSTM autoencoder anomaly engineHeavier training pipeline + GPU inference. Marginal value until IF + threshold leaves a gap on contextual anomalies.Contextual anomaly detection — patterns that point-based IF misses.
Prophet forecaster engine5-min horizon does not yet demand the extra model capacity.Explicit weekly / yearly seasonality + holiday components for longer-horizon forecasting.
DeepAR forecasterSame as Prophet plus deeper deployment surface.Probabilistic forecasting at scale; useful if the autoscaler ever needs quantile-based scaling decisions.
Envoy adapter (or NGINX Plus dynamic upstream API)The classical NGINX reload pathway is sufficient for v1; the Envoy adapter is a 2nd lb_adapter plugin.Sub-second weight updates without an upstream-config reload — would eliminate the +3 s max-latency cost observed in §34.3.
HAProxy adapterSame as Envoy; lb_adapter plugin slot exists in services/shared/lb_adapters/.Same coverage as Envoy for HAProxy shops.
AWS ALB adapterPhase 2 / SaaS deployment shape.Managed-cloud LB path.
OpenTelemetry-native ingest (replace lb-otel-shipper sidecar)NGINX's native OTLP support is not yet mature in OSS; the shipper is the integration path that works today.Eliminates the sidecar process and the access-log tailing pathway.

35.7 Future evaluations

EvaluationWhat it would showStatus
Full-length bench harness on the retrained PPO modelWhether the retrained policy beats RR on the heterogeneous workload (the open question from §34.3).Blocked on the retraining workstream.
Real-traffic shadow-mode runWhether the production telemetry distribution matches the offline trace distribution closely enough that the trained policy generalises.Requires a live integrator (Phase 2 customer or thesis demo environment).
K8s HPA-vs-SmartLoad autoscaling comparisonQuantitative read on RQ4 (forecast-driven scale-out vs reactive HPA).Blocked on the Helm chart (#133).
Multi-run batching with confidence intervalsStatistically defensible per-metric deltas.Harness extension; small.
B1 / B2 / B3 benchmark scenarios from §17 (ALL.2)Coverage of B1 steady, B2 burst, B3 degraded-backend cases beyond the single 3-phase Locust shape.Phase 3A (S5).
Phase 3A integration tests (3 scenarios, T2.3)System-level test coverage beyond unit / contract layers.Phase 3A (S5).

35.8 Operational maturity gaps

CapabilityStatus
API versioning + deprecation policyIssue #134 — path versioning is canonical; the formal deprecation window mechanism is the open piece.
Webhook dispatcherPlaceholder service. Issue #130 lands the implementation; docs/planned/webhook-dispatcher.md is the tracking doc.
Embedded metrics page in operator UIIssue #131 (OUI.x).
Logs page in operator UIIssue #124, optional.
Forecast persistence (predicted_rps over time)Closed by #159 (v1.0.7w). The forecasting service now writes one row per inference cycle to a forecasts hypertable (init.sql), and the Grafana Forecast dashboard's predicted line reads from it directly — the predicted series is dense across the dashboard's bucket interval instead of sparse-at-decision-moments. The persistence runs regardless of safe_mode (observational, not control-flow).
Request correlation ID propagationIssue #143 — tracking ID flows through every service so audit replay is deterministic.
Test reorganisation (PEP 420 namespace packages)Issue #140 — in progress.
Structural lint flip from permissive to strictIssue #139 — lint rules ship in permissive mode today; the flip to fail-on-violation gates the strict CI mode.

35.9 What is not a limitation

Two design decisions are sometimes raised as limitations but are explicit canonical choices. They are listed here so a reader doesn't mistake them for forward work.

DecisionWhy it's not a limitation
The trained policy is a contextual bandit, not a full MDPRecorded in §31.3 / §32.4. The trade-off (no learned environment model) is intentional: production safety + reproducibility + training cost. Adding the MDP variant would be a different system.
The classical scheduler stays as the deterministic floor and is never deprecatedPrinciple 9 + §15. The classical fallback is the safety contract that lets RL be trusted at all. "Replacing" it with RL would defeat the safety guarantee.

35.10 Review checklist — limitations & future work

  • Is every limitation accompanied by either an issue number that closes it or an explicit "deferred to Phase 2"?
  • Are out-of-scope items distinguished from limitations (the former are intentional, the latter are forward work)?
  • Does the K8s deferral cover both the deployment-shape gap (#133) and the autoscaler portability gap (cluster_client adapter)?
  • Are the model limitations (PPO retraining, ARIMA MAPE, IF artifact) tied back to the §22 entries that recorded the gap?
  • Are the §35.9 non-limitations distinguished from real limitations?

C. Glossary

TermMeaning in SmartLoad
Anomaly DetectionReal-time identification of unhealthy or degraded backend nodes from telemetry, used to constrain routing.
AutoscalerService that adjusts the number of running backend containers based on forecast and policy.
Classical algorithmRound-robin / least-connections; deterministic baseline routing; always available.
Control BusRedis Pub/Sub used to broadcast decision and policy signals between services.
Decision LayerMulti-engine controller (anomaly + RL + forecasting + classical) running in parallel and merging through the routing hierarchy.
ForecastingTime-series prediction of near-term load (request rate / CPU); drives proactive autoscaling.
Hybrid modeDefault operating mode: classical fallback + anomaly constraints + RL recommendations.
HypertableTimescaleDB feature: automatic time-based partitioning of a table for fast time-range queries.
Integration-firstProject principle (Apr 2026): wire services to Redis + TimescaleDB before adding ML logic.
MAPE cycleMonitor → Analyze → Plan → Execute closed feedback loop.
MiddlewareSmartLoad's role: a thin layer between client traffic and backend services.
OTLPOpenTelemetry Protocol; how services push metrics to the OTel Collector.
Phase 0 / 1 / 2 / 3Internal milestones in the integration-first plan: wiring → skeletons → real ML → tests & benchmarks.
PPOProximal Policy Optimization; the RL algorithm used (Stable-Baselines3).
Reinforcement Learning (RL)Agent that learns adaptive routing scores from reward feedback (negative latency).
Routing decision hierarchyAnomaly → policy → RL → classical (the canonical merge order).
Safe modeOperator override that disables RL and forces equal-weight classical routing.
Shadow modeRL outputs are computed and logged but not enforced; used to validate the pipeline before active routing.
SidecarAuxiliary process running alongside NGINX that subscribes to Redis and updates upstream config.
SLOService Level Objective (e.g., P95 latency < 200 ms).
TelemetryReal-time metrics (latency, error rate, CPU, memory, request count) collected from backends + LB.
TimescaleDBPostgreSQL extension for time-series data; SmartLoad's metrics store.

To revise canonical claims, update the affected section and append a row to §15. Diagrams referenced via relative paths from docs/ — open this file directly with a browser from a clone of the repo.
